<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-conductor-power-condition**: For fixed integers n and an odd prime r, the splitting conditions on r used to build the cyclic real field of degree 2^n (r congruent to 1 modulo 2^{n+1} and 2 a 2^n-th power modulo r) are decided exactly by one congruence test and one modular exponentiation.
- **inv-frobenius-generator-nonsquare**: For an odd prime l different from r, the Frobenius of l generates the cyclic quotient exactly when l is a nonsquare modulo r.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-residue-test-as-modularity-proof**: Treat a passing residue test as evidence that a given 2-adic representation is modular. -> The test decides only elementary congruence conditions on a conductor prime; it carries no information about Galois representations, eigenforms or Hodge–Tate weights.
- **anti-ignore-degree-range**: Apply the congruence and power test without keeping the standing relation between the exponents n and a. -> The cyclic layer structure used later (a layer of smaller degree inside the full cyclic field) is not guaranteed, so the selected prime may not yield the intended tower.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `algorithmic`. The main theorem is existential, but the field-construction step contains a finite, explicit residue check on a conductor prime (congruence modulo a power of two, a power-residue test for 2, and a nonsquare test for an auxiliary prime), which is implemented and independently verified here.

```python
# INPUTS: one line 'n r l' of three integers: n >= 1, r an odd prime, l an odd prime different from r
# PRECONDITIONS: n >= 1; r and l odd primes; l != r
# OUTPUTS: one line 'A B' where A=1 iff r = 1 mod 2^(n+1) and 2 is a 2^n-th power mod r; B=1 iff l is a nonsquare mod r
# TIME: O(log r) modular multiplications
# SPACE: O(1) integers
# ARITHMETIC: exact Python integers, modular exponentiation
# FAILURE: malformed input raises an exception (nonzero exit); no other failure mode on valid input
import sys


def conductor_ok(n, r):
    if r % (2 ** (n + 1)) != 1:
        return 0
    if pow(2, (r - 1) // (2 ** n), r) == 1:
        return 1
    return 0


def generates(r, l):
    if pow(l % r, (r - 1) // 2, r) == r - 1:
        return 1
    return 0


def main():
    n, r, l = (int(t) for t in sys.stdin.read().split())
    print(conductor_ok(n, r), generates(r, l))


main()
```

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The main theorem is an existence statement: it gives no procedure that could be turned into a program for building the eigenform from the representation. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The only discretisation in the paper is a lattice-and-finite-extension argument for profinite group representations. It has no dynamics, state evolution or time stepping to simulate. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The Hecke-component dimension bound is a theoretical lower bound on dimensions of spectra. It supplies no checker, converter or utility with a defined input and output. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The polynomial-orbit lemma proves that a vector is locally algebraic via limits in a Banach space and does not give a finite computation with bounded cost. The overall route uses ultraproducts, density and Baire-type arguments, which are non-constructive. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Compact modules of completed forms are infinitary Iwasawa-algebra objects. They give no finite representation with defined operations and costs. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The growth-degree bound for compact duals of smooth representations measures module growth over augmentation ideals. It says nothing about circuits, throughput or memory hierarchy. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The Galois representations concern modularity, not hardness assumptions, adversaries or protocols. Finite ramification is a structural hypothesis, not a security parameter. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The dense set of representations from the ordinary family is a Zariski-density statement used to apply a modularity theorem. It is not a learning, fitting or generalisation result. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The auxiliary-prime count is a bookkeeping inequality that makes a rank positive, not an objective to optimise under constraints. No feasibility or optimality notion appears. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Solvable base change along cyclic steps is an arithmetic descent between number fields. It has no nodes, messages, failures or consensus. |
| other (mathematical_certification_reference) | True | weak | inspirational | inferred | The statement gives a precise, residual-image-free hypothesis list under which a 2-adic representation of the rationals is modular up to Tate twist. It can serve as a reference for deciding whether a modularity claim in a theoretical database is covered by the theorem. It supplies no construction of the eigenform. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **hyp-oddness** (hypothesis): The modularity conclusion is stated only for representations whose determinant at complex conjugation is minus one. The statement gives no way to treat even representations. Blocked use: ['Citing the main theorem for an even two-dimensional representation of the rationals', 'Using the theorem for a Maass-type or real-quadratic-field representation with trivial determinant at complex conjugation']
- **hyp-regular-de-rham** (hypothesis): Regularity at 2 is a hypothesis: the local representation must be de Rham with two different Hodge–Tate weights. Equal weights are excluded. Blocked use: ['Applying the theorem to a representation whose two Hodge–Tate weights at 2 coincide', 'Applying the theorem to a representation that is not de Rham at 2']
- **scope-gl2-over-q** (formal_scope): The main theorem is a statement about two-dimensional continuous irreducible representations of the absolute Galois group of the rationals. The solvable totally real fields are internal devices and are descended back to the rationals. Blocked use: ['Using the theorem for higher-dimensional representations', 'Using the theorem directly for representations of the Galois group of a base field other than the rationals']
- **nonconstructive-existence-conclusion** (nonconstructive): The conclusion asserts that a classical cuspidal eigenform exists up to a Tate twist. The statement supplies no procedure, level bound or coefficient formula for the eigenform. Blocked use: ['Using the theorem as an algorithm that outputs Fourier coefficients of the eigenform', 'Reading the theorem as giving an explicit level or weight from the representation']
- **stage-virtually-solvable-split** (edge_case): The argument separates virtually solvable representations from the rest. Persistence of absolute irreducibility over finite extensions is proved only for representations that satisfy the hypotheses and are not virtually solvable. Blocked use: ['Assuming absolute irreducibility after finite restriction for a virtually solvable representation', 'Applying the later propagation and connectedness stages to the virtually solvable case']
- **stage-propagation-curve-hypotheses** (hypothesis): Localized propagation requires a characteristic-two curve that is potentially pro-modular, has non-virtually-solvable generic representation, and has finite local images at the non-dyadic places of the allowed set. Blocked use: ['Propagating pro-modularity from a curve whose local images at non-dyadic places are infinite', 'Starting propagation from a curve whose generic representation is virtually solvable']
- **stage-transverse-charts** (hypothesis): Connectedness between components is established only at transverse points, where no marked line is globally invariant. The transverse condition is geometric and is imposed in the closed residual fiber. Blocked use: ['Transferring potential pro-modularity across components at a non-transverse residual point', 'Using the chain argument with marked lines that are globally invariant']
- **stage-auxiliary-primes-existence** (nonconstructive): The auxiliary primes in the patching stage are shown to exist for a sufficiently large number of primes. Their size and location are not controlled by a construction. Blocked use: ['Extracting an explicit list of auxiliary primes from the proof', 'Deriving an explicit bound on the level at which patching occurs']
- **stage-hecke-dimension-lower-bound** (approximation): The dimension statement for Hecke components is a lower bound. It is stated for a fixed residual factor and does not give the exact dimension of any component. Blocked use: ['Treating the bound as an exact dimension of every Hecke component', 'Using it for a Hecke algebra outside the stated residual factor setting']
- **stage-higher-line-distinct-exponents** (edge_case): The existence of an invariant line with larger exponent is proved when the local representation is reducible with distinct cyclotomic exponents. The reducible local case then needs the ordinary family and polynomial-orbit stages. Blocked use: ['Assuming the larger-exponent line exists when the two cyclotomic exponents are equal', 'Using the ordinary-family stage for a locally irreducible target']
- **stage-thorne-external-input** (hypothesis): The modularity of the dense family points rests on an external theorem about ordinary potentially crystalline representations. The classicality stage depends on that theorem and on the normalizations that make its hypotheses hold. Blocked use: ['Claiming the classicality stage is self-contained within this paper', 'Applying the dense-points step to points that fail the ordinary or potentially crystalline hypotheses after normalization']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove that every continuous, irreducible, odd two-dimensional 2-adic representation of $`G_{\mathbb Q}`$, unramified outside finitely many primes and de Rham at 2 with distinct Hodge–Tate weights, is modular up to Tate twist. This resolves the odd, regular two-dimensional Fontaine–Mazur conjecture over ℚ at 2, including all residual representations.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

The Fontaine–Mazur conjecture predicts that irreducible $p$-adic Galois representations satisfying suitable finiteness and local conditions arise from algebraic geometry (Fontaine and Mazur 1995) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[FM1995](#ref-FM1995)]. Modular forms supply a central class of examples through Deligne’s cohomological construction (Deligne 1971, secs. 3.9–3.12 and 5.2–5.5) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Deligne1971](#ref-Deligne1971)]. Over $\mathbb Q$, its odd two-dimensional form has a more precise modularity formulation. A continuous representation $$r:G_{\mathbb Q}\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{GL}}_2(\overline{\mathbb Q}_p)$$ is *odd* if $\det r(c)=-1$ for a complex conjugation $c$. It is *regular de Rham* at $p$ if its restriction to $G_{\mathbb Q_p}$ is de Rham and has two distinct Hodge–Tate weights. The conjecture asserts that an irreducible such representation, unramified outside finitely many primes, comes from a classical cuspidal eigenform, allowing a Tate twist.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *Let $$r:G_{\mathbb Q}\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{GL}}_2(\overline{\mathbb Q}_2)$$ be continuous and irreducible. Suppose that $r$ is odd, is unramified outside finitely many primes, and is de Rham at $2$ with distinct Hodge–Tate weights. Then $r$ is isomorphic to a Tate twist of the $2$-adic Galois representation associated with a classical cuspidal eigenform.*

</div>

Thus Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> resolves the odd, regular two-dimensional Fontaine–Mazur conjecture over $\mathbb Q$ at $2$. Its scope includes every semisimple residual representation, including scalar and reducible ones. Together with the odd-prime results discussed below, it gives the stated modularity conclusion at every prime. The geometric realization of modular representations also gives the geometric-origin conclusion in this odd, regular setting.

Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, in particular its absence of a residual-image hypothesis, supplies the weight-two modularity input in the height argument of *Hilbert’s tenth problem over the rational numbers* (OpenAI 2026, sec. 4) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[OpenAIH10Q2026](#ref-OpenAIH10Q2026)], whose undecidability proof also uses substantial additional arithmetic and logical constructions.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Background and the dyadic problem

The deformation-theoretic approach to modularity lifting developed by Wiles and Taylor–Wiles compares Galois deformation rings with Hecke algebras (Wiles 1995; Taylor and Wiles 1995) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Wiles1995](#ref-Wiles1995), [TaylorWiles1995](#ref-TaylorWiles1995)]. Serre’s conjecture concerns the residual starting point (Serre 1987) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Serre1987](#ref-Serre1987)]; its proof by Khare–Wintenberger and Kisin supplies modularity for odd absolutely irreducible residual representations, including characteristic two (Khare and Wintenberger 2009a, 2009b; Kisin 2009a) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[KhareWintenberger2009](#ref-KhareWintenberger2009), [KhareWintenberger2009II](#ref-KhareWintenberger2009II), [Kisin2009](#ref-Kisin2009)]. The existence of a residual modular form still leaves the problem of proving modularity for a specified characteristic-zero lift. For a reducible residual representation, even the modular seed must be obtained by a different construction.

Skinner and Wiles developed ordinary families and pro-modularity arguments for residually reducible representations and then for nearly ordinary deformations of irreducible residual representations (Skinner and Wiles 1999, 2001) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[SkinnerWiles1999](#ref-SkinnerWiles1999), [SkinnerWiles2001](#ref-SkinnerWiles2001)]. Allen adapted this family method to nearly ordinary dyadic representations with solvable absolutely irreducible residual image (Allen 2014) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Allen2014](#ref-Allen2014)]. Kisin brought local deformation geometry and the $p$-adic Langlands correspondence into the regular Fontaine–Mazur problem (Kisin 2009c) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Kisin2009FM](#ref-Kisin2009FM)]. Emerton’s local–global compatibility work separated occurrence in completed cohomology from the production of classical vectors (Emerton 2011) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Emerton2011](#ref-Emerton2011)]. These developments explain why both stages of the proof below are needed, and why ordinary auxiliary families can be useful without ordinarity being imposed on the final target.

At odd primes, Pan’s work treats the residually reducible case and, together with preceding modularity lifting theorems, establishes the regular conjecture apart from one local residual configuration at $3$ (Pan 2022, Theorems 1.0.2 and 1.0.4) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[Pan2022](#ref-Pan2022)]. Zhang removes that configuration, obtaining the stated odd, regular theorem for every odd prime (Zhang 2025, Theorem 1.0.2) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Zhang2025](#ref-Zhang2025)]. At $2$, Tung removes the remaining local restriction in a modularity lifting theorem whose global residual image is nonsolvable (Tung 2021, Theorem A) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Tung2021Dyadic](#ref-Tung2021Dyadic)]. Thus local scalar residual behavior and a globally scalar residual representation must be distinguished: a local theorem covering the former does not by itself settle the latter.

The proof has a useful precedent in the passage from unrestricted pro-modularity to classicality developed by Pan, and in Zhang’s potential pro-modularity argument (Pan 2022; Zhang 2025) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[Pan2022](#ref-Pan2022), [Zhang2025](#ref-Zhang2025)]. The present dyadic problem requires integral control of the local actions and of deformation families even when their residual representation is scalar. We first construct potential occurrence in completed definite forms, then extract classical vectors, and finally descend. The distinction between occurrence and classicality also appears in Pan’s theorem for representations in the completed cohomology of modular curves: that theorem assumes occurrence as a hypothesis (Pan 2026, Theorem 7.1.2) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Pan2026Classicality](#ref-Pan2026Classicality)].

Thorne’s theorem supplies an essential set of classical points: ordinary, potentially crystalline representations of weights $\{0,1\}$ are modular at every prime, without a residual irreducibility hypothesis (Thorne 2026, Theorem D) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Thorne2026](#ref-Thorne2026)]. After a global normalization and passage to the dual, our family construction makes this theorem applicable at a dense set of points, and the subsequent specialization argument recovers the original target’s arbitrary weight gap. Prescribing global lifts with chosen local components, as in Emerton–Gee–Pan–Zhu (Emerton et al. 2026, Theorem 1.1.1) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[EGPZ2026](#ref-EGPZ2026)], is a different conclusion; their dyadic theorem also retains a nonsolvable residual-image hypothesis.

The local input is the $p$-adic Langlands correspondence of Colmez (Colmez 2010) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Colmez2010](#ref-Colmez2010)], in the all-prime form of Colmez, Dospinescu and Paškūnas (Colmez, Dospinescu, and Paškūnas 2014) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[CDP2014](#ref-CDP2014)]. Paškūnas and Tung’s block finiteness theorem (Paškūnas and Tung 2021) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[PaskunasTung2021](#ref-PaskunasTung2021)] supplies an integral local pseudodeformation action even in scalar residual blocks. This resolves a local representation-theoretic obstacle, but leaves a global one: a trace does not record a residual extension class, and globally reducible families can be too large for a direct connectedness argument. We work with actual representations and temporarily record local invariant lines to obtain the necessary dimension loss. These auxiliary marks are used in connectedness; the propagation theorem imposes no ordinary condition at the dyadic places.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### From occurrence to classicality and descent

A *pro-modular locus* is a closed trace locus in a Galois pseudodeformation spectrum defined by a completed Hecke algebra of definite quaternionic forms. No local condition is imposed at $2$. Its characteristic-zero points give Hecke eigensystems in completed forms. The central problem is to pass from this occurrence statement to a classical cuspidal form with the prescribed Galois representation. We do so in three stages, summarized in Figure <a href="#fig:proof-route" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:proof-route">1</a>.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
###### Potential occurrence for a whole family.

The first stage places the target and the deformation families needed later in one pro-modular trace locus over a common solvable totally real extension $F$, completely split at $2$. Two constructions work together. Localized propagation starts at a characteristic-two curve whose generic representation remains absolutely irreducible under finite restriction. If the curve is pro-modular and has finite local images at the allowed non-dyadic primes, patching gives potential pro-modularity of every irreducible deformation locus containing it (Theorem <a href="#thm:localized-propagation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:localized-propagation">22</a>). The local deformation problem at $2$ is unrestricted.

To produce the curves to which this result applies, we construct abelian $2$-extensions $\mathbb Q\subset A\subset F$, keeping the number of allowed ramified primes under control while their degrees grow. In the scalar and reducible cases we mark a small proportion of the dyadic places. Recording an invariant line at those places retains information that traces alone would lose. We select the open part of the residual fiber on which the marked lines are transverse to every globally invariant line. A Selmer calculation makes the globally reducible locus too small to disconnect these charts. A modular seed and a curve cut from a component containing the target enter the same connected residual fiber. Applying propagation along it yields Theorem <a href="#thm:potential-promodularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:potential-promodularity">33</a>. The conclusion concerns the whole closed trace image of the prescribed family at one common tame level; it does not require that this family itself have the dimension of the larger component used to find the curve.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
###### Classical vectors at the target.

When the local representation at $2$ is irreducible, the local Langlands correspondence supplies locally algebraic vectors. For a reducible local target, we choose an invariant line with the larger cyclotomic exponent and retain its character as a parameter. The resulting family records trace and character jointly and is finite over a one-dimensional weight space. Its continuous members are not all de Rham; the useful points are a dense set of specially chosen weights. After a global twist the corresponding representations have cyclotomic exponents $\{1,0\}$, or Hodge–Tate weights $\{-1,0\}$. Their *duals* have Hodge–Tate weights $\{0,1\}$ and satisfy the ordinary potentially crystalline hypotheses of Thorne’s theorem.

Those classical points determine the order of the two characters in the ordinary induction. A finite closed joint support transports this order to the target. A quotient, lattice and Tate-module construction then supplies a genuine eigenvector in the target eigenspace. Finally, a direct polynomial-orbit argument turns its character and exponent inequality into a nonzero locally algebraic vector. This last step avoids assuming that algebraic vectors commute with specialization.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
###### Descent of a classical packet.

The vector gives a regular cuspidal automorphic representation over $F$. We descend along prime-degree cyclic steps in a solvable tower. At each step, a finite-character discrepancy between the descended Galois representation and the target is removed by a Hecke-character twist. Descent is applied only after classicality has been established; no descent statement for pro-modular loci is needed.

<figure id="fig:proof-route">

<figcaption>The proof route. The connectedness construction uses the propagation theorem; the two upper boxes are cooperating inputs to occurrence. The target enters through that construction. The common field $F$ is solvable, totally real, of even degree and completely split at $2$. The ordinary family is used in the reducible local branch. Both branches produce a classical packet before descent.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### The constructions used in the proof

Several established methods must be kept compatible at the characteristic-two curve. Böckle–Iyengar–Paškūnas develop local deformation theory with Laurent-series residue fields and coefficient orders, including the auxiliary-variable comparison of completions (Böckle, Iyengar, and Paškūnas 2023, sec. 3.5) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[BIP](#ref-BIP)]. We use this framework with an extra coefficient variable and global representation and incidence spaces. Lemma <a href="#lem:curve-completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:curve-completion">7</a> proves the resulting identification of continuous deformation and obstruction problems, including the topology on the residue field and the dimension change.

The propagation construction follows the auxiliary-level method of Taylor–Wiles, Kisin’s formulation relative to local deformation rings, and the characteristic-two determinant-twist argument of Khare–Wintenberger (Taylor and Wiles 1995; Kisin 2009b; Khare and Wintenberger 2009b) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[TaylorWiles1995](#ref-TaylorWiles1995), [Kisin2009FiniteFlat](#ref-Kisin2009FiniteFlat), [KhareWintenberger2009II](#ref-KhareWintenberger2009II)]. Pan’s localization at one-dimensional primes is the direct global precedent (Pan 2022) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[Pan2022](#ref-Pan2022)]; Zhang’s potential formulation is used in the same overall progression (Zhang 2025) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[Zhang2025](#ref-Zhang2025)]. At the Laurent-series residue field we patch finite jets, meaning quotients by fixed powers of the local maximal ideal. The exactness argument works with filtrations whose successive quotients are fixed old-level modules. A uniform Artin–Rees bound preserves their actual old deformation actions. Determinant twists and explicit quadratic sign intertwiners then give support on every local component. All of these action comparisons are retained integrally.

The marked-line construction serves a different purpose. Its local factors are integral even over the characteristic-two real-place base, and transversality gives a strict Selmer loss at every mark. The number of marks grows more slowly than the total degree, so this loss is compatible with the global dimension estimates. The argument uses the unrestricted propagation theorem after entering a transverse chart; it does not replace that theorem by ordinary patching.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
###### Organization.

Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a> fixes conventions and removes the virtually solvable case. Section <a href="#sec:deformation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:deformation">3</a> constructs the representation spaces, their curve completions and the local models. Section <a href="#sec:completed" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:completed">4</a> compares the actual Galois and local block actions on completed forms and proves the Hecke-dimension bound. Section <a href="#sec:propagation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:propagation">5</a> proves localized propagation. Section <a href="#sec:fields" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:fields">6</a> supplies the field and Selmer estimates, and Section <a href="#sec:connectedness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:connectedness">7</a> combines them with a modular seed to obtain potential pro-modularity. Section <a href="#sec:classicality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:classicality">8</a> constructs classical vectors and carries out solvable descent.

<a id="sec:preliminaries"></a>
### Conventions and initial reductions

We fix the coefficient and automorphic conventions before introducing deformation spaces. We then remove the virtually solvable case and specify the trace loci used throughout the proof. Table <a href="#tab:conventions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tab:conventions">1</a> collects the normalizations that link deformation theory, completed forms and classicality.

<div id="tab:conventions">

| Object                  | Convention                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
|:------------------------|:-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Frobenius               | Geometric by default; local reciprocity sends a uniformizer to geometric Frobenius. The tame relation with exponent $q_v$ uses arithmetic Frobenius explicitly.                                                                                                        |
| Weights                 | $\varepsilon=x|x|$, cyclotomic exponent $1$, $\mathop{\mathrm{HT}}(\varepsilon)=\{-1\}$, and $|2|=2^{-1}$.                                                                                                                                                             |
| Determinants and blocks | $\chi=\det r$, completed-form central character $\psi=\chi\varepsilon$, local block parameter $r\otimes\varepsilon$ with determinant $\chi\varepsilon^2$.                                                                                                              |
| Ordinary target         | At a locally reducible de Rham target choose the line character $\eta_1$ with exponent $a>b$, where $\eta_2=\chi/\eta_1$ has exponent $b$. The favorable upper-Borel, unnormalized inducing pair is $(\eta_2\varepsilon,\eta_1)$.                                      |
| Algebraic factor        | $\operatorname{Sym}^{a-b-1}\det^{b+1}$. The continuous family retaining $\eta_1$ need not be de Rham.                                                                                                                                                                  |
| Curve coefficients      | $K=k((t))$ has its Laurent topology; $\mathcal L=\widehat{\mathcal O[[z]]_{(\varpi)}}$, with $z\mapsto t$. The auxiliary ultrapower in Section <a href="#sec:propagation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:propagation">5</a> is specified separately.    |
| Dual actions            | Group duals use the indicated contragredient action. On scalar eigenspaces the commuting Hecke, torus and $U$ operators are transposed without inversion, as in Section <a href="#sec:classicality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:classicality">8</a>. |
| Support                 | Images in pseudodeformation spectra are closed images; pro-modular support containments are set-theoretic.                                                                                                                                                             |

Conventions used at the interfaces of the proof. The character orientation in the table concerns the chosen target and the normalized dense points, not every member of the family.

</div>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Coefficients and cyclotomic exponents

Write $\mathcal O$ for the ring of integers in a sufficiently large finite extension $E/\mathbb Q_2$, $\varpi$ for a uniformizer, and $k$ for its residue field. Finite coefficient extensions are allowed. A lattice means a finite free $\mathcal O$-module of full rank. The cyclotomic character is denoted by $\varepsilon$. We count weights as *cyclotomic exponents*, so that $\varepsilon$ has exponent $1$. In the Hodge–Tate convention $\mathop{\mathrm{HT}}(\varepsilon)=\{-1\}$, exponents are the negatives of Hodge–Tate weights.

We use geometric Frobenius and local reciprocity sending a uniformizer to geometric Frobenius. Thus, as a character of $\mathbb Q_2^\times$, $$\varepsilon=x|x|,\qquad x(a)=a.$$ The determinant of a Galois representation is denoted by $\chi$. For a representation in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, de Rham class field theory gives $$\chi=\chi_{\mathrm{fin}}\varepsilon^w$$ for a finite-order character $\chi_{\mathrm{fin}}$ and an integer $w$. Indeed, Tate’s local algebraicity theorem (Serre 1989, chap. III, Section 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Serre1989Abelian](#ref-Serre1989Abelian)] gives the integral cyclotomic exponent at $2$. Removing this power leaves a character locally constant at every finite place. Finite ramification and global reciprocity make it a character of a finite ray class group, hence of finite order. In the convention for quaternionic forms used below, the central character is $$\psi=\chi\varepsilon.$$ The normalized local block parameter attached to a Galois representation $\rho$ is that of $\rho\otimes\varepsilon$. These conventions agree with (Pan 2022, secs. 3.1–3.5) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Pan2022](#ref-Pan2022)].

For the statement of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, one may use the arithmetic normalization $V_f$ of the representation of a primitive eigenform of weight $k$ and nebentype $\psi_f$: at a good prime $q$, $$\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}V_f(\operatorname{Frob}^{\mathrm{ar}}_q)=a_q(f),\qquad
 \det V_f=\psi_f\varepsilon^{k-1}.$$ These trace and determinant formulas use the normalization of (Deligne and Serre 1974, Theorem 6.1) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[DeligneSerre1974](#ref-DeligneSerre1974)]; arithmetic Frobenius is fixed there in Section 3.1. The cyclotomic exponents of $V_f$ are $\{0,k-1\}$. Evaluating the same representation at geometric Frobenius inverts its eigenvalues; it does not change the representation into its dual. If a construction instead produces $V_f^\vee$, the wedge pairing gives $$V_f^\vee\simeq V_f\otimes\psi_f^{-1}\varepsilon^{1-k}
       \simeq V_g\otimes\varepsilon^{1-k},$$ where $g$ is the primitive form associated with the finite-character twist $f\otimes\psi_f^{-1}$. Thus an expression $r\simeq V_f^\vee\varepsilon^n$ becomes $r\simeq V_g\varepsilon^{n+1-k}$. The up-to-Tate-twist conclusion of the main theorem is unchanged by this conversion.

<a id="stmt-lem-finite-coefficients"></a>
<div id="lem:finite-coefficients" class="lemma">

**Lemma 2**. *A continuous finite-dimensional representation of a profinite group on $\overline{\mathbb Q}_2^n$ is defined over a finite extension of $\mathbb Q_2$ and admits a stable lattice there.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-finite-coefficients"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-finite-coefficients</summary>

*Proof.* There are countably many finite subextensions of $\overline{\mathbb Q}_2/\mathbb Q_2$. For each one $E$, the set of group elements whose representation matrices lie in $\mathop{\mathrm{GL}}_n(E)$ is a closed subgroup. These subgroups cover the profinite group. By Baire’s theorem, one has nonempty interior, hence is open. Enlarge $E$ to contain the finitely many matrix entries of coset representatives. Then every matrix lies in that finite extension.

The image is compact. Its entries, and the entries of its inverses, have bounded valuations. The $\mathcal O$-submodule generated by the orbit of $\mathcal O^n$ therefore lies between two scalar multiples of $\mathcal O^n$. It is a stable lattice. ◻

</details>

We apply Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-coefficients" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-coefficients">2</a> whenever a characteristic-zero representation is first introduced. Over a curve field, the analogous compact-image lattice argument gives an integral realization over $k'[[t]]$.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### The virtually solvable case

For a representation over any field, after extending scalars to an algebraic closure, call it *virtually solvable* if the identity component of the reduced Zariski closure of its image is solvable. The closure here is taken over the algebraic closure, so that the resulting algebraic group is smooth. This property is unchanged by restriction to a finite-index subgroup.

<a id="stmt-lem-solvable-reduction"></a>
<div id="lem:solvable-reduction" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3**. *Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> holds for virtually solvable representations. A representation satisfying its hypotheses and not virtually solvable remains absolutely irreducible and not virtually solvable over every finite extension of $\mathbb Q$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-solvable-reduction"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-solvable-reduction</summary>

*Proof.* Let $H$ be the algebraic monodromy group of an irreducible two-dimensional representation over an algebraic closure. Lie–Kolchin and the structure of connected solvable groups (Milne 2022, Theorems 17.4 and 16.33) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[MilneAlgebraicGroups](#ref-MilneAlgebraicGroups)] give the following alternatives. If $H^\circ$ is solvable and has a nontrivial unipotent radical, the latter has a unique fixed line; normality makes that line $H$-stable, contradicting irreducibility. The remaining possibilities are that $H^\circ$ is scalar, or that its two distinct character lines are permuted by $H$.

In the scalar case the projective image is finite. After a finite extension of the base field, the representation is a scalar character on a two-dimensional space. Its two Hodge–Tate weights are equal, which is excluded. In the second case the action on the two lines gives a quadratic extension $L/\mathbb Q$ and $$r\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{Ind}}_{G_L}^{G_{\mathbb Q}}\theta$$ for a continuous character $\theta$. The character is de Rham and finitely ramified, hence is the $2$-adic avatar of an algebraic Hecke character, by the following rank-one consequence of Tate’s theorem and the construction in (Serre 1989, chap. III, Sections 1.2 and 2.3) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[Serre1989Abelian](#ref-Serre1989Abelian)]. The integral algebraic exponents on open local units obey the global unit relation. Principal ideals in a suitable ray subgroup therefore receive the prescribed algebraic monomials. A positive power of each of finitely many ray-class representatives is principal in that subgroup, so its character value is algebraic. Adjoining these values places all ideal-character values in one number field and constructs the algebraic Hecke character. This argument does not assume that the given character was initially rational over a number field. If $L$ is real quadratic, its algebraic exponents at the two real embeddings are equal by the unit theorem: a unit of infinite order forces their difference to vanish in the algebraic character relation. This again contradicts regularity. For imaginary quadratic $L$, the theta-series construction gives the required cuspidal eigenform, with its algebraic Tate normalization; equivalently, use quadratic automorphic induction (Jacquet and Langlands 1970, Proposition 12.1) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[JacquetLanglands1970](#ref-JacquetLanglands1970)].

Finally, restriction to finite index preserves $H^\circ$. If a restriction of a non-virtually-solvable representation were reducible, its identity component would lie in a Borel subgroup and be solvable. This is impossible. ◻

</details>

Henceforth the target $r$ is not virtually solvable. In particular, its restriction to every field used below is absolutely irreducible.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Fields, deformation spaces, and trace loci

All auxiliary base fields $F$ are totally real, solvable over $\mathbb Q$, of even degree, and split completely at $2$. We may replace them by Galois closures. Let $S$ contain the real places, all places above $2$, and a finite set of other places. Set $$d=[F:\mathbb Q],\qquad
 u=\#\{v\in S:v\nmid 2,\ v\text{ finite}\},\qquad
 s=2d+u.$$ The group $G_{F,S}$ is the Galois group of the maximal extension of $F$ unramified outside $S$.

A two-dimensional pseudorepresentation means a determinant in the sense of Chenevier, with its determinant character retained. In particular, no division by $2$ is used to recover a determinant from traces. Fixing $\chi$ and a semisimple residual representation gives a complete local pseudodeformation ring, denoted by $R_F^{\mathrm{ps},\chi}$. Framed representation rings and schemes, including those with specified local invariant lines, will be constructed in Section <a href="#sec:deformation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:deformation">3</a>. Whenever a component is mapped to a pseudodeformation spectrum, its *image* means the closure of its image unless stated otherwise.

There is a unique quaternion algebra $D_F/F$, up to isomorphism, ramified at all real places and at no finite place. The evenness of $d$ makes this possible, by the classification by ramification sets (Voight 2021, Main Theorem 14.6.1) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[Voight2021](#ref-Voight2021)]. A completed Hecke algebra of definite $D_F^\times$-forms, with central character $\psi=\chi\varepsilon$, defines a closed trace locus in $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}R_F^{\mathrm{ps},\chi}$. We call such a locus *pro-modular*. The tame level is open, is hyperspecial away from $S$, and is unrestricted at the places above $2$. A closed trace locus is *potentially pro-modular* if its restriction becomes pro-modular after a further solvable totally real extension split completely at $2$. All support containments in this terminology are set-theoretic.

A *characteristic-two curve* is a one-dimensional domain quotient of a complete local $k$-algebra. After finite extension, its normalization has the form $k'[[t]]$; its generic point has residue field $k'((t))$. We also use the word curve for this generic point and its associated representation when there is no ambiguity. Whenever such a representation is used, we choose a compact-image lattice. Agreement of traces and determinants modulo the maximal ideal determines the semisimple residual representation, but need not determine a residual extension class. The latter distinction will be important in the marked-line connectedness argument.

The reductions now leave a fixed determinant and a target that remains absolutely irreducible after every finite restriction. We next construct the spaces in which its trace, and when needed its local line character, can vary. The distinction between these data is essential: a trace space is sufficient for completed-form occurrence, whereas the final reducible classicality argument also needs the chosen character.

<a id="sec:deformation"></a>
### Deformations and local geometry

We establish the deformation-theoretic tools used in both propagation and connectedness. The two points requiring particular care are deformation theory at a characteristic-two curve and the geometry of several local conditions imposed simultaneously. We retain determinant characters throughout. In particular, in characteristic two a pseudorepresentation means a two-dimensional determinant, not merely its trace.

There are three outputs. First, finite trace-and-character spaces let us compare dimensions before and after forgetting frames and lines. Second, curve completion gives legitimate continuous local and global deformation problems with a common coefficient field. Third, two distinct local geometries serve different later uses: unrestricted dyadic factors enter propagation, whereas marked scalar factors enter connectedness.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Representation spaces and formal dimensions

The groups occurring below, namely local Galois groups and the groups $G_{F,S}$ with $S$ finite and containing the dyadic and infinite places, satisfy Mazur’s finiteness condition $\Phi_2$: for every open subgroup $H$, the set $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathrm{cts}}(H,\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z)$ is finite. We use continuous deformation functors. A frame is a specified basis; a local line, when recorded, is part of the deformation data. This is the framed form of the deformation framework of (Mazur 1989) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[Mazur1989](#ref-Mazur1989)].

<a id="stmt-prop-rep-space"></a>
<div id="prop:rep-space" class="proposition">

**Proposition 4** (Representation spaces). *Let $G$ satisfy $\Phi_2$, let $\overline D$ be a continuous two-dimensional determinant over the finite field $k$, and fix a continuous lift $\chi$ of its determinant character. The following statements hold.*

1.  *The determinant deformation functor is represented by a complete Noetherian local $\mathcal O$-algebra $R^{\mathrm{ps}}$. There is a finite $R^{\mathrm{ps}}$-algebra $\mathscr E$ carrying its universal Cayley–Hamilton representation. Matrix representations of $\mathscr E$ compatible with the universal determinant form an affine scheme of finite type over $R^{\mathrm{ps}}$. Its completion at a closed residual matrix representation is that representation’s framed deformation space.*

2.  *Invariant lines at finitely many closed subgroups can be imposed by closed incidence conditions in products of projective lines. On the absolutely irreducible global locus, existence of such a line with a specified character is closed in the trace and character parameters.*

3.  *Suppose the subgroups’ abelianizations have finitely generated maximal pro-$2$ quotients. Adjoining the characters on the specified lines gives a finite extension of the completed trace-and-determinant image.*

4.  *At an absolutely irreducible representation over a coefficient field, determinants classify unframed deformations. After a splitting extension, the framed fiber is a conjugacy orbit of dimension three.*

*These assertions do not require the residual determinant to be multiplicity-free.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-rep-space"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-rep-space</summary>

*Proof.* For representability under $\Phi_2$, we use (Chenevier 2014, Propositions 3.3 and 3.7, Remark 3.5) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[Chenevier2014](#ref-Chenevier2014)], with the numbering of the cited arXiv version. Finiteness of the universal Cayley–Hamilton algebra and the algebraization of its compatible matrix representations are (Wang-Erickson 2018, Propositions 3.2 and 3.6, Theorems 3.7 and 3.8) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[WangErickson2018](#ref-WangErickson2018)]. Compatibility here includes the characteristic-polynomial equations; no generalized matrix algebra at a scalar residual point is being assumed. Fixing $\chi$ is a base change of these statements.

For clarity, finite type has a direct algebraic explanation. Choose finitely many algebra generators for the finite algebra $\mathscr E$, assign a $2\times2$ matrix to each, and impose the multiplication and characteristic-polynomial identities. These are polynomial equations over the Noetherian base. Completing at a residual matrix point recovers continuity and the chosen residual representation, including its extension class.

Line invariance and the prescribed action on a line are closed equations in the relative projective line. Over the absolutely irreducible locus the Cayley–Hamilton algebra is an Azumaya algebra of rank four; one may split it faithfully flat locally and then descend the proper incidence image. This proves the closedness assertion.

If $g_1,\ldots,g_a$ topologically generate the relevant abelian character quotient, every recorded character value $\lambda(g_j)$ satisfies $$X^2-T(g_j)X+\chi(g_j)=0.$$ Modulo the maximal ideal of the trace image, the corresponding parameters are nilpotent after the chosen residual roots have been fixed. The closed fiber of the completed character algebra is therefore finite. Complete Nakayama proves finiteness of that algebra over the trace image. Finite torsion generators cause no change.

Finally, the absolutely irreducible determinant theorem identifies the Cayley–Hamilton algebra with a matrix algebra after a splitting extension (Chenevier 2014, Theorem 2.22 and Corollary 2.23) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[Chenevier2014](#ref-Chenevier2014)]. Two compatible representations are conjugate and their stabilizer consists of scalars. This gives the final assertion. ◻

</details>

In dimension two the fixed-determinant ring is topologically generated by traces. There is no division by two in this statement: for group elements $g_j$ the determinant of a formal sum is $$D\Bigl(\sum_j t_jg_j\Bigr)
=\sum_jt_j^2\chi(g_j)
 +\sum_{j<l}t_jt_l\bigl(T(g_j)T(g_l)-T(g_jg_l)\bigr).$$ This identity also explains why the determinant character must remain part of the data in characteristic two.

For a representation chart with recorded local lines, its completed trace-and-character image is the closed algebra generated by the global traces and the character values on those lines. Passing to this image forgets the frame and the positions of the lines, but retains their characters. Under the abelianization hypothesis of Proposition <a href="#prop:rep-space" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:rep-space">4</a>(3), forgetting the characters is then a finite map and does not change the dimension of the image. The next lemma bounds the dimension lost in the first passage.

<a id="stmt-lem-dimension-comparison"></a>
<div id="lem:dimension-comparison" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (Formal dimension comparison). *Let $X$ be one of the finite-type representation spaces of Proposition <a href="#prop:rep-space" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:rep-space">4</a>, including any recorded local lines, over an excellent complete local trace-and-character base $R$. Let $x$ be a closed point above the closed point of $R$, and let $P$ be a prime of $\widehat{\mathcal O}_{X,x}$. Suppose its generic global representation is absolutely irreducible. If $p=P\cap R$, then $$\dim\bigl(\widehat{\mathcal O}_{X,x}/P\bigr)
 \leq \dim(R/p)+3+\ell,$$ where $\ell$ is the number of recorded lines not known to have distinct characters generically. A line with distinct characters contributes zero to this bound. The analogous statement holds with any established bound for the relevant generic representation fiber.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-dimension-comparison"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-dimension-comparison</summary>

*Proof.* Contract $P$ to a prime $q$ of $\mathcal O_{X,x}$. There is a surjection from the completion of $\mathcal O_{X,x}/q$ to $\widehat{\mathcal O}_{X,x}/P$, and completion preserves dimension (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tags 02IJ and 07NV) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[StacksProject](#ref-StacksProject)]. The finite-type dimension formula over the excellent local base therefore bounds the latter dimension by $\dim(R/p)$ plus the generic-fiber dimension of the contraction. Over an algebraic closure of $\mathop{\mathrm{Frac}}(R/p)$ the representation is unique up to a three-dimensional conjugacy orbit. Each unspecified invariant line has dimension at most one in $\mathbf P^1$; with distinct characters its choices are finite. This gives the inequality. Notice that the proof contracts the formal prime before applying the finite-type formula. It does not assert that an arbitrary formal prime itself algebraizes. ◻

</details>

Thus passing from a representation chart to its trace-and-character image loses at most the three frame dimensions and the dimensions of the lines whose character has not separated. The next lemma supplies curves and coefficient points on which these comparisons can be tested.

<a id="stmt-lem-formal-point-density"></a>
<div id="lem:formal-point-density" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6** (Curves and coefficient points). *Let $A$ be a complete Noetherian local domain with finite residue field.*

1.  *If $A$ has characteristic two and positive dimension, then its dimension-one domain quotients have generic points dense in $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}A$. Their normalizations are $k'[[t]]$ for finite extensions $k'/k$.*

2.  *If $A$ is a flat $\mathcal O$-algebra, continuous maps from $A$ to rings of integers in finite extensions of $E$ are dense on $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}A$.*

*In either assertion the points can be chosen outside any prescribed proper closed subset.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-formal-point-density"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-formal-point-density</summary>

*Proof.* Use formal Noether normalization, over $k$ in the first case and over $\mathcal O$ in the second; the coefficient and finite normalization facts used here are (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tags 032A, 032D, 07QW and 07QV) <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[StacksProject](#ref-StacksProject)]. Thus $A$ is finite over a power-series subring $S$. Given $0\ne f\in A$, integrality supplies a nonzero element of $fA\cap S$: use an integral equation for $f$ with nonzero constant term in the fraction field extension, and clear denominators.

In the first case choose a one-parameter specialization of $S$ on which that element is nonzero. Such a specialization exists by choosing positive weights for the variables and, if necessary, coefficients in a finite residue extension so that the lowest weighted part does not vanish. Its image is finite over $k[[t^a]]$ for some $a>0$. Lying-over gives a one-dimensional prime of $A$ avoiding $f$. Excellence makes its normalization finite; a complete equicharacteristic discrete valuation ring with finite residue field has the asserted form.

In the second case choose sufficiently small integral values for the variables on which the selected nonzero series does not vanish. Existence follows successively from the one-variable preparation theorem. A prime above this specialization has a finite extension of $E$ as fraction field. Integrality puts the image of $A$ in its ring of integers; locality and finiteness make the map continuous. Again $f$ does not vanish. Applying this to an element of the ideal of the excluded closed subset proves density. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Continuous deformations at a curve

Deformation theory over Laurent-series residue fields, with integral residual bases and finite coefficient orders, is developed in (Böckle, Iyengar, and Paškūnas 2023, sec. 3.5, Proposition 3.34 and Lemmas 3.35–3.37) <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[BIP](#ref-BIP)]. We give the completion argument needed here with its global frames and incidence data. Keeping the Laurent topology makes continuous cocycles and obstructions the correct ones.

Let $\rho_*:G\to\mathop{\mathrm{GL}}_2(K)$ be a continuous curve representation, where $K=k((t))$. After a finite extension of constants and of the curve, choose a stable lattice and write its matrices in $\mathop{\mathrm{GL}}_2(k[[t]])$. Let $\overline\rho$ be their reduction at $t=0$. A restriction of the curve to a local group need not generate all of $K$ from its matrix entries. Introduce a new variable $z$, send it to $t$, and carry this same variable through every local and global deformation ring. Their enhanced curve maps then have the common residue field $K$. The enhanced coefficient ring is $$\mathcal L=\widehat{\mathcal O[[z]]_{(\varpi)}}.$$ It is a complete discrete valuation ring with residue field $K$, and $$\mathcal L/\varpi^a=(\mathcal O/\varpi^a)((z)).$$ Finite-length $\mathcal L$-modules carry their Laurent-series topology. In particular $K$ is not given the discrete topology.

<a id="stmt-lem-curve-completion"></a>
<div id="lem:curve-completion" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7** (The curve completion). *Let $R$ be the framed deformation ring of $\overline\rho$, with the specified determinant and any of the incidence data above. Let $\mathfrak p$ be the kernel of the enhanced curve map $R[[z]]\to k[[t]]$. Then $$R_*:=\widehat{(R[[z]])_{\mathfrak p}}$$ classifies continuous deformations of $\rho_*$ to finite-length local Artinian $\mathcal L$-algebras with residue $K$. This identification commutes with restriction to local groups, with frames and invariant lines, and with fixing the determinant. The analogous assertion holds for determinants; on the absolutely irreducible locus it agrees with unframed matrix deformation theory.*

*If a component of the original complete local ring has dimension $e$ and contains the curve, its enhanced completed localization has dimension $e$. At a characteristic-zero coefficient point the corresponding assertion uses the usual completion over its finite $2$-adic coefficient field, without adjoining $z$; a horizontal integral component of dimension $e$ then gives a completed local component of dimension $e-1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-curve-completion"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-curve-completion</summary>

<a id="eq:curve-order"></a>
*Proof.* We first prove the integral-order assertion underlying the functorial claim. Let $C$ be one of the Artinian algebras, with maximal ideal $\mathfrak n$, $\mathfrak n^N=0$, and $\varpi^a C=0$. Write $$A_0=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\bigl((\mathcal O/\varpi^a)[[z]]\longrightarrow C\bigr).$$ The inverse image of $k[[t]]$ under $C\to K$ is $A_0+\mathfrak n$. A bounded subset of this inverse image lies in a finite $A_0$-order: subtract coefficientwise lifts of its residual entries, choose a finite $A_0$-lattice $U\subset\mathfrak n$ containing the resulting bounded differences, and put $$\label{eq:curve-order}
B=A_0+U+U^2+\cdots+U^{N-1}.$$ Powers here mean finite module spans of products. Nilpotence makes $B$ an algebra finite over $A_0$. Its quotient by $B\cap\mathfrak n$ is $k[[t]]$, with nilpotent kernel. Hence $B$ is local with residue field $k$. The finite local ring $B/zB$ is Artinian, so a power of $\mathfrak m_B$ is contained in $zB$. Its maximal-ideal topology is therefore its finite-module topology; $B$ is complete and compact.

Apply this to the compact set of matrix entries of a continuous lift of $\rho_*$. These entries have integral residues because the residual basis was chosen above. Their determinants have residual values in $k[[t]]^\times$, and so are units of $B$. We obtain a continuous representation in $\mathop{\mathrm{GL}}_2(B)$ reducing to precisely $\overline\rho$. Negative powers of $z$ in nilpotent entries belong to $B\cap\mathfrak n$ and disappear at that closed residual point. There is no uniform lower valuation bound as $C$ varies, nor is one needed. The same construction simultaneously contains any finite collection of local data, and two such orders admit a common enlargement.

Universality gives a map $R\to B$. Adjoin $z$ using its coefficient value. Elements outside $\mathfrak p$ have nonzero residue in $K$, so are units in $C$, while $\mathfrak p^N$ maps to zero after increasing $N$. The map factors uniquely through $R_*$. Conversely, a local map $R_*\to C$ factors through a finite jet. Write the original formal coordinates as integral lifts of their curve values plus nilpotent increments. Expansion in the increments has only finitely many terms at this jet depth; the residual coordinates, after their constants are removed, lie in $(t)$. Substitution in each original formal series consequently converges in the Laurent-series topology. It is continuous, and so gives a continuous lift of the universal representation. The two constructions are inverse. They plainly preserve determinant equations, line incidences, and restriction maps. For determinants use their characteristic coefficients, followed on the absolutely irreducible locus by the matrix-algebra identification in Proposition <a href="#prop:rep-space" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:rep-space">4</a>.

For dimensions, adjoining $z$ increases a component dimension from $e$ to $e+1$, while the enhanced curve quotient has dimension one: the map on $k[[z]]$ is already onto $k[[t]]$. The dimension formula for complete equidimensional excellent local rings gives height $e$ at the enhanced prime. Localization and completion have dimension $e$; see also the dimension and catenarity facts in (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tags 00KW, 032C, 0ECF and 07NV) <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[StacksProject](#ref-StacksProject)]. At a horizontal characteristic-zero coefficient point the integral quotient again has dimension one, but no new variable has been adjoined. Its height and completed dimension are therefore $e-1$. The deformation functor uses the usual $2$-adic topology. ◻

</details>

The restriction to the inverse image of $k[[t]]$ in <a href="#eq:curve-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:curve-order">[eq:curve-order]</a> is essential. For example, the bounded singleton $\{z^{-1}\}$ cannot lie in a finite order having integral residual image.

For a representation $V$ over its coefficient field $L$, write $h^i(G,V)=\dim_L H^i_{\mathrm{cts}}(G,V)$, omitting $G$ when understood. For global $V$, write $h^i_v(V)=\dim_L H^i_{\mathrm{cts}}(G_{F_v},V)$. These are ordinary cohomology dimensions; at real places the duality pairings use Tate cohomology, as specified below.

<a id="stmt-lem-curve-cohomology"></a>
<div id="lem:curve-cohomology" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:global-euler-curve"></a>
**Lemma 8** (Curve-field cohomology). *Let $V$ be a finite-dimensional continuous $K$-representation of a local Galois group or of $G_{F,S}$. Continuous cohomology is finite dimensional and satisfies local duality, local and global Euler characteristics, and Poitou–Tate duality with arbitrary linear local conditions. The twist in characteristic two is the residual Tate twist. At real places local duality uses Tate cohomology; the $h^0_v$ appearing in the Euler and Selmer formulas below still denotes the dimension of ordinary invariants. In particular, if $F$ is totally real of degree $d$ and $\dim_KV=r$, then $$\begin{aligned}
h^0_v(V)-h^1_v(V)+h^2_v(V)&=-r[F_v:\mathbb Q_2]
                       &&(v\mid2),\\
h^0(V)-h^1(V)+h^2(V)&=-rd+\sum_{v\mid\infty}h^0_v(V).
\label{eq:global-euler-curve}
\end{aligned}$$ At a real place the local contribution to a Selmer dimension difference is $\dim L_v-h^0_v(V)$, where $L_v\subset H^1(F_v,V)$. The same formulas, with the usual real-place interpretation, hold for finite $2$-adic coefficient fields.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-curve-cohomology"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-curve-cohomology</summary>

*Proof.* Choose a stable $k[[t]]$-lattice $T\subset V$. Continuous cochains with values in $T$ are the inverse limit of the cochain complexes for $T/t^nT$. Finite-level cohomology is finite, so the derived inverse limits vanish and cohomology commutes with this inverse limit. The resulting compact cohomology modules are finite over $k[[t]]$: their quotients by $t$ inject into the appropriate finite-level cohomology group, and compact Nakayama applies. Every continuous cochain into $V$ has bounded image, since its domain is compact. Thus its cochain complex is obtained by inverting $t$, and the same is true of cohomology.

Apply finite-coefficient local duality and Poitou–Tate to $T/t^nT$ and the dual torsion lattice, then pass to the inverse limit and invert $t$. This gives the perfect field-valued pairings, with Tate cohomology at real places, and the exact global sequence. Taking lengths in the finite-level Euler formulas and dividing by $n$ gives the displayed dimensions; bounded torsion contributes zero in the limit. The real-place terms pass through the same calculation and must not be discarded at $2$. Intersections with lattices and saturation allow any linear local condition and its annihilator. The finite-coefficient inputs are (Milne 2006, I, Corollary 2.3, Theorems 2.8, 2.13, 4.10 and 5.1) <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[Milne2006](#ref-Milne2006)]. The identical lattice argument with an $\mathcal O$-lattice gives the finite-$2$-adic-field version. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Relative presentations

Curve completion has now identified the deformation functors, and the preceding cohomology calculation supplies their tangent and obstruction spaces. We turn these data into a bound on actual relations cutting out the global ring over its local base. At real places the displayed invariant dimensions are ordinary $H^0$, while the duality argument uses Tate-modified groups.

Put $D=\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}^0(\rho)$ for a fixed-determinant two-dimensional representation. Its dimension is three, also in characteristic two. Its dual there is $\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}(\rho)/\text{scalars}$, not in general $D$ itself.

<a id="stmt-prop-relative-presentation"></a>
<div id="prop:relative-presentation" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:relative-one-frame"></a>
**Proposition 9** (Relative relation bounds). *Let $F$ be totally real of degree $d$, let $S$ contain the real and dyadic places, and let $\rho$ be a representation over a finite residue field, a curve field as above, or a finite $2$-adic field. Use one global frame and a fixed determinant. Let $T\subset S$ consist of all real places and $m$ dyadic places, each isomorphic to $\mathbb Q_2$. Over the completed product of the unrestricted local framed deformation rings at $T$, the global framed ring has a power-series presentation with $g$ relative variables and $r$ relations satisfying $$\label{eq:relative-one-frame}
g-r\ge 3-6m-h^0\bigl(D^\vee(1)\bigr).$$ Local quotient conditions and local line charts may then be imposed by base change without changing this bound. This includes replacing the local base by its reduction.*

<a id="eq:relative-independent-frames"></a>
*For the variable-determinant problem with independent frames at the places of $S$, the relative tangent dimension over the local framed base is $$\label{eq:relative-independent-frames}
h^1_{\mathrm{Sel}}(\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\rho)
 +\sum_{v\in S}h^0_v(\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\rho)-h^0(\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\rho),$$ where the Selmer condition is zero at $S$ and any other prescribed conditions are included in the Selmer group. On the absolutely irreducible locus, forgetting these independent frames has smooth fiber dimension $4|S|-1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-relative-presentation"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-relative-presentation</summary>

*Proof.* The relative tangent space in the one-frame problem is the kernel of restriction on $Z^1(D)$. We spell out the obstruction bound since it is also used at enhanced curves. Across a small extension, choose continuous matrix lifts with the prescribed determinant. Multiplication errors form a continuous $2$-cocycle. Given the specified local lifts, its class belongs to the kernel of restriction on $H^2(D)$. If that class vanishes, correct the matrices to a global representation. Its differences from the chosen local lifts are $1$-cocycles, and the second obstruction belongs to the cokernel of restriction on $Z^1(D)$. Continuous set sections of finite-length coefficient modules suffice: coefficientwise digit sections supply them. The determinant map on invertible matrices is smooth, so determinant correction needs no division by two.

Consequently the relation number is at most the sum of these two obstruction dimensions. One can verify that this bounds actual minimal relations as follows. For a minimal relative presentation $H\twoheadrightarrow R$ with kernel $I$, test the small extension $$H/(\mathfrak m_HI+\mathfrak m_H^j)
 \longrightarrow H/(I+\mathfrak m_H^j)$$ for $j$ large enough that its kernel is $I/\mathfrak m_HI$. The two obstruction maps must jointly detect its dual relation directions. Indeed, quotient the kernel onto an undetected one-dimensional direction. Vanishing of both obstructions would give a lift compatible with the local base. Its relative coordinates differ from the tautological ones by elements of the small kernel, which is killed by $\mathfrak m_H$. Changing these coordinates therefore alters a relation only through its residual linear terms in the relative variables. Those terms vanish by minimality, so the nonzero surviving relation cannot become zero. This contradicts the existence of the lift and proves the relation bound for the complete rings.

Poitou–Tate bounds the first obstruction dimension by $$h^2(D)-\sum_{v\in T}h^2_v(D)+h^0(D^\vee(1)).$$ Subtracting it and the $Z^1$ cokernel from the tangent dimension gives $$\begin{aligned}
g-r
&\ge 3(1-|T|)
 -\bigl(h^0-h^1+h^2\bigr)(D)\\
&\qquad +\sum_{v\in T}
       \bigl(h^0_v-h^1_v+h^2_v\bigr)(D)
 -h^0(D^\vee(1))\\
&=3(1-d-m)+3d-3m-h^0(D^\vee(1)).
\end{aligned}$$ For the last equality, the real terms in <a href="#eq:global-euler-curve" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:global-euler-curve">[eq:global-euler-curve]</a> cancel the real local terms; the dyadic Euler characteristic is $-3$. This proves <a href="#eq:relative-one-frame" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:relative-one-frame">[eq:relative-one-frame]</a> in both coefficient characteristics. Base change carries the same presentation to the extra local conditions.

For independent frames, first choose a global cohomology class vanishing at the prescribed local places. Local choices of trivialization contribute the local invariant spaces, and a common global infinitesimal automorphism is quotiented out. This gives <a href="#eq:relative-independent-frames" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:relative-independent-frames">[eq:relative-independent-frames]</a>. If the global representation is absolutely irreducible, the common automorphisms are exactly the scalars; the quotient of the $|S|$ frame spaces therefore has dimension $4|S|-1$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cor-charzero-line-dimension"></a>
<div id="cor:charzero-line-dimension" class="corollary">

**Corollary 10** (A characteristic-zero line chart). *For a two-dimensional representation of $G_{\mathbb Q_2}$ over a finite $2$-adic field with a chosen invariant line and fixed determinant, every component of its framed line-deformation chart has dimension at least five. The odd fixed-determinant real chart has dimension two. In particular, over $\mathbb Q$, the relative bound for these two local charts is $-3$ whenever $h^0(D^\vee(1))=0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-charzero-line-dimension"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-charzero-line-dimension</summary>

*Proof.* Hold the line fixed in an adapted basis. The coefficient Lie algebra is the trace-zero upper triangular algebra of dimension two. The local Euler formula gives $\dim Z^1-\dim H^2=4$, so the relation bound gives local dimension at least four. Moving the line adds one formal variable. At a real place the odd involution has two distinct eigenvalues in characteristic zero and its conjugacy orbit has dimension two. The final assertion is <a href="#eq:relative-one-frame" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:relative-one-frame">[eq:relative-one-frame]</a> with $d=m=1$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Small pseudodeformation loci and control of inertia

<a id="stmt-lem-pseudo-bad-loci"></a>
<div id="lem:pseudo-bad-loci" class="lemma">

**Lemma 11** (Virtually solvable loci). *Let $F$ be totally real of degree $d$, let $S$ contain its dyadic and infinite places, and fix the determinant. In the characteristic-two pseudodeformation space, the irreducible dihedral curve points lie in a closed subset of dimension at most $d$. Finite-projective-image curve points give only the residual pseudorepresentation. If $F/\mathbb Q$ is abelian, the reducible locus is closed and has dimension at most one.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-pseudo-bad-loci"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-pseudo-bad-loci</summary>

*Proof.* There are only finitely many quadratic extensions of $F$ unramified outside $S$. This follows from Hermite–Minkowski, since fixed local degree bounds the finitely many possible local discriminants. For each such extension $F'/F$, use the character deformation space of $G_{F',S}$ with the product of the character and its conjugate fixed. Class field theory identifies its free pro-$2$ parameters with a quotient of the dyadic local units; non-dyadic unit groups and class groups contribute only finite groups. The dyadic unit rank over $F'$ is $2d$, and its minus rank for the quadratic involution is $d$. Fixing the product fixes the plus part up to finite groups: multiplication by two is an isogeny of the relevant free $\mathbb Z_2$-modules even though two is not a unit. Thus the reduced special character space has dimension at most $d$.

Its map to pseudorepresentations has finite image algebra. Indeed the finitely many generating character values satisfy the characteristic polynomials of the induced traces, and complete Nakayama applies as in Proposition <a href="#prop:rep-space" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:rep-space">4</a>. Its image is consequently closed and has dimension at most $d$. Taking the finite union gives the dihedral bound. The argument applies to curve points after finite coefficient and curve extensions, and Lemma <a href="#lem:formal-point-density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:formal-point-density">6</a> gives the asserted closed-locus formulation.

If the projective image is finite, each scalar in the representation has square fixed by its determinant. The determinant has finite image in the special fiber, so the scalar image, and hence the whole image, is finite. Its traces are algebraic over a finite field. An element of $k'[[t]]$ algebraic over a finite field is constant; its constant reduction is already prescribed. Thus these traces give exactly the residual pseudorepresentation.

Finally, a reducible determinant is a pair of characters with fixed product, so one character determines the other. For an abelian totally real $F$, Brumer’s theorem on Leopoldt’s conjecture (Brumer 1967) <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[Brumer1967](#ref-Brumer1967)], in the form proved in (Fresnel 1968--1969, Theorem 1 and Proposition 8, pp. 9-08–9-09 and 9-14) <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[Fresnel1969](#ref-Fresnel1969)], gives free pro-$2$ character rank one. The same finite character-image argument therefore gives a closed locus of dimension at most one. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-inertia-basechange"></a>
<div id="lem:inertia-basechange" class="lemma">

**Lemma 12** (Inertia and solvable base change). *Consider finitely many complete deformation or pseudodeformation families over finite-residue coefficient rings, and a finite set of places not above two. After a fixed finite local extension at each place, all their field-valued representations have unipotent inertia. For a characteristic-two curve in such a family whose determinant has finite image, imposing the residual value of a Frobenius trace makes the local image finite.*

*Any further finite local extensions needed to kill these finite images, to kill the finitely many inertial types, to make the fixed determinant unramified, or to arrange residue cardinalities congruent to $1$ modulo $4$, can be realized inside a totally real solvable base change which splits completely at the dyadic places. One may take its Galois closure over $\mathbb Q$. New ramification of the base field away from the original allowed set creates no new ramification of the restricted representations.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-inertia-basechange"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-inertia-basechange</summary>

*Proof.* Use the finite universal Cayley–Hamilton algebra $\mathscr E$ for each family. Its reduction modulo the base maximal ideal is finite. After killing that finite residual group image locally, the group acts through the pro-$2$ group $1+\mathfrak m\mathscr E$. At a place of residue characteristic $\ell\ne2$, wild inertia and the non-$2$ part of tame inertia therefore act trivially. Write $\tau$ for the remaining tame generator and $\phi$ for arithmetic Frobenius, so that $$\phi\tau\phi^{-1}=\tau^q.$$ At any field-valued point the multiset of eigenvalues of $\tau$ is stable under the $q$th-power map. Each eigenvalue consequently has order dividing $q^2-1$. A further fixed tame extension kills this semisimple inertia at every point, leaving unipotent inertia. The same finite extension works for the finite list of source families.

In characteristic two a unipotent $2\times2$ matrix has square one. After these extensions the inertia image is therefore finite: it is generated by the image of the single pro-$2$ tame generator. The original inertia image is finite as well, since the inertia subgroup upstairs has finite index in the original one. Now use the Frobenius chosen in the original local group, whose trace is prescribed by the curve constraint. Its characteristic polynomial has coefficients in a finite field, since that trace is the residual constant and the determinant has finite image. Its eigenvalues have finite order, and its possible Jordan part has order at most two. This original Frobenius consequently has finite order. The full local image is an extension of the finite inertia image by the finite quotient generated by Frobenius, and is therefore finite.

Every finite Galois group over a nonarchimedean local field is solvable: wild inertia is an $\ell$-group, and the successive tame inertia and residue quotients are cyclic. A composition tower therefore realizes each required local extension through cyclic prime-degree steps. At each step the prime-degree case of the Grunwald–Wang theorem (Milne 2020b, chap. VIII, Theorem 2.4) <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[MilneCFT](#ref-MilneCFT)] prescribes the finitely many local extensions while imposing splitting at dyadic and real places. For degree two this is simply weak approximation for squareclasses; no higher-power Grunwald–Wang assertion is needed. Composita and Galois closure preserve solvability, total reality, and complete dyadic splitting. An unramified local extension of even degree makes an odd residue cardinality $1$ modulo $4$. The determinant’s finite non-dyadic inertia can be killed at the same time.

Finally, inertia in the enlarged field maps into inertia downstairs. A representation unramified at a place outside the original allowed set remains unramified after restriction, even if the field extension itself ramifies there. This proves the last assertion. ◻

</details>

The unipotent assertion may be carried to closures of restricted pseudorepresentation images. On the globally absolutely irreducible locus this can be checked by characteristic polynomials and by traces against a matrix-algebra basis of group elements. For example, the equations $$\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl((\rho(\tau)-1)^2\rho(g)\bigr)=0$$ for such a basis force $(\rho(\tau)-1)^2=0$ by the perfect trace pairing on $M_2$. Thus these are closed identities on the locus where the corresponding trace Gram matrix is invertible. This justifies the set-theoretic use of the uniform inertia assertion on the source-image closures below.

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### The local product used in propagation

We now define the local model independently of any patched global ring. Fix $d$ real factors and $d$ dyadic factors, the latter all over $\mathbb Q_2$. At each use the prescribed fixed determinant, odd at the real places. Start with integral residual bases over a common finite field, take the completed product of the framed local rings, and form its enhanced completion at any chosen curve as in Lemma <a href="#lem:curve-completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:curve-completion">7</a>. Denote the resulting $\mathcal L$-algebra by $A^{\mathrm{rd}}$.

<a id="eq:tame-model"></a>
We enlarge $\mathcal O$ once so that it contains $i$ with $i^2=-1$. Choose $u$ odd residue cardinalities $q_j\equiv1\pmod4$, and $g\ge0$. Put $$A=A^{\mathrm{rd}}[[X_1,\ldots,X_g]].$$ For $\eta\in\{1,i\}$ define $B_\eta$ by adjoining at each of the $u$ places two formal invertible matrices $\tau_j,\phi_j$, both reducing to the identity at the curve, and imposing $$\label{eq:tame-model}
\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\tau_j=\eta+\eta^{-1},\qquad
\det\tau_j=1,\qquad
\phi_j\tau_j\phi_j^{-1}=\tau_j^{q_j}.$$ There is no condition on $\det\phi_j$. This is the raw completed matrix quotient: we do not silently replace it by its reduction. At characteristic-zero field points the $i$-condition gives the two distinct inertia eigenvalues $i,-i$, whereas the $1$-condition makes inertia unipotent. Both $i$ and $-i$ reduce to one in characteristic two. The two conditions thus have identical special-fiber equations but different characteristic-zero component geometry, described below.

<a id="stmt-prop-local-geometry"></a>
<div id="prop:local-geometry" class="proposition">

**Proposition 13** (Local component geometry). *The ring $A^{\mathrm{rd}}$ is a flat complete-intersection normal domain over $\mathcal L$, of dimension $1+8d$, and its special fiber is a normal domain of dimension $8d$.
