Set $$e=1+8d+4u+g.$$ Then the following statements hold for the model <a href="#eq:tame-model" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tame-model">[eq:tame-model]</a>.*

1.  *$\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}B_i$ is irreducible of dimension $e$, and its unique irreducible component is horizontal.*

2.  *The two rings have a canonical common special fiber, compatible with their universal matrices and the variables $X_1,\ldots,X_g$. Its irreducible components have dimension $e-1$ and are indexed by a choice, at each tame place, of the unramified component or the closure of the nontrivial-unipotent component.*

3.  *The irreducible components of $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}B_1$ are horizontal and have dimension $e$. They are indexed by the same choices. The specialization of each has precisely its corresponding special-fiber component as underlying support. In particular the generic point of a special-fiber component lies on exactly one component of $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}B_1$.*

*The assertions remain valid after finite extension of the constants and curve field and the resulting enhanced completion. No condition on the dyadic curve representations is required.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-local-geometry"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-local-geometry</summary>

*Proof.* *The unrestricted real and dyadic base.* We first establish the assertion about $A^{\mathrm{rd}}$. An odd real framed ring is given by trace zero and determinant $-1$. These equations are equivalent to the involution relation with that determinant, by Cayley–Hamilton and invertibility. At the scalar residual point it has the form $$\mathcal O[[a,b,c]]/(a^2+2a+bc).$$ Its special fiber $a^2+bc=0$ is a geometrically normal surface: it is an integral hypersurface whose singular locus has codimension two (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tag 031S) <a id="pm-cite-44"></a>[[StacksProject](#ref-StacksProject)]. At a non-scalar residual involution one obtains a smooth chart of the same relative dimension two.

For a dyadic factor, the fixed-determinant framed deformation theorem (Böckle, Iyengar, and Paškūnas 2023, Theorem 1.5) <a id="pm-cite-45"></a>[[BIP](#ref-BIP)] gives a flat complete-intersection normal domain of dimension seven and a normal special-fiber domain of dimension six. The theorem applies at $2$, including scalar and reducible residual representations; the prime restriction on a different density theorem is not being used. The same statement holds after each finite coefficient extension.

The completed tensor product of these special-fiber rings over the finite, hence perfect, field is a normal domain. Here we use the completed tensor-product normality theorem (Tabaâ 2020, Theorem 3.3 and Proposition 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-46"></a>[[Tabaa2020](#ref-Tabaa2020)]; the residue tensor product is the field itself, so the product is local and connected. Its dimension is $8d$. The mixed-characteristic completed product is a flat complete intersection: concatenate presentations, first checking the regular sequences modulo $\varpi$, and then lift them together with the nonzerodivisor $\varpi$. Its special fiber is the normal domain just obtained, and the regular-element normality criterion makes the product normal. It is a domain as well: the lowest nonzero $\varpi$-adic terms of a putative product of two nonzero elements could not multiply to zero in the special fiber.

Adjoining $z$, localizing at the enhanced curve, and completing preserve normality by excellence (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tags 07QW and 0C23) <a id="pm-cite-47"></a>[[StacksProject](#ref-StacksProject)]. They preserve the asserted dimensions by Lemma <a href="#lem:curve-completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:curve-completion">7</a>, and the same applies to the special fiber. The element $\varpi$ remains a nonzerodivisor, so the enhanced ring is flat over the discrete valuation ring $\mathcal L$. This proves the assertion about $A^{\mathrm{rd}}$ and also shows why no special dyadic hypothesis is needed. The construction can be repeated after any of the finite extensions in the statement.

<a id="eq:i-incidence"></a>
*The integral incidence for the $i$-type.* We prove the tame assertions using proper incidences, keeping their images distinct from the incidences themselves. In the $i$-case, Cayley–Hamilton gives $\tau_j^2=-1$, hence $\tau_j^{q_j}=\tau_j$. Adjoin a $\phi_j$-stable $i$-eigenline for each $\tau_j$. This gives a closed proper incidence $Y_i$ in a product of projective lines over $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}B_i$. In an adapted basis on a slope chart, its matrices are $$\tau_j=\begin{pmatrix}i&n_j\\0&-i\end{pmatrix},\qquad
\phi_j=\begin{pmatrix}1+d_j+x_j&h_j\\0&1+d_j\end{pmatrix},$$ and their sole remaining equation is $$\label{eq:i-incidence}
x_jn_j=2i h_j.$$ The line slopes are free chart coordinates.

<a id="eq:i-incidence-completion"></a>
Here is a completion argument which also works for all places at once. On a product of affine slope charts put $$C=\widehat{A[s_1,\ldots,s_u]}^{\,\mathfrak m_A}.$$ This is completion along the entire closed fiber, not at a selected slope origin. Restricted coefficient series embed it into $A[[s_1,\ldots,s_u]]$, so $C$ is a domain. Exactness of completion identifies $C/\varpi C$ with the corresponding restricted-series ring over $A/\varpi A$, also a domain. Completion of the incidence chart along that fiber is $$\label{eq:i-incidence-completion}
C[[x_j,n_j,h_j,d_j:1\le j\le u]]/
                  (x_jn_j-2ih_j)_{j=1}^u.$$ Reduce each monomial by replacing paired factors $x_jn_j$ by $2ih_j$ until they never occur together. Assign weights $1,1,2$ to $x_j,n_j,h_j$. Reduction preserves weight, so it converges in the power-series topology and yields a normal form. The difference between a monomial and its reduction is a sum of the relations <a href="#eq:i-incidence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:i-incidence">[eq:i-incidence]</a> times series whose weights are lower by two; these series converge as well. The substitution $$h_j\longmapsto x_jn_j/(2i)$$ embeds these normal forms into $\mathop{\mathrm{Frac}}(C)[[x_j,n_j,d_j:1\le j\le u]]$. Indeed the two output exponents recover the exponent of $h_j$ as their minimum, and each output coefficient receives only finitely many input terms. This proves uniqueness of the normal form and shows that <a href="#eq:i-incidence-completion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:i-incidence-completion">[eq:i-incidence-completion]</a> is a domain. This argument needs no uniform bound on denominators. It also shows that the successive relations are nonzerodivisors and gives dimension $\dim A+4u=e$ on the incidence.

We explain why chart completions suffice. At every point of the closed fiber, localization of the chart maps faithfully flatly to a localization of its completed chart. The latter is a domain, so the incidence is locally integral there. Every irreducible component of a proper scheme over a local base meets the closed fiber; so does any nonempty closed intersection of components. Local integrality therefore makes the components disjoint. The closed fiber is $(\mathbf P^1_K)^u$, which is connected, hence there is only one component. A nonempty nilradical support would also meet the closed fiber, so the incidence is reduced. Thus $Y_i$ is integral.

After inverting two the eigenline is uniquely given by the idempotent $(\tau_j+i)/(2i)$, and the incidence projection is an isomorphism. In characteristic two, commuting matrices have a common eigenline after algebraic extension, so the proper projection covers every geometric point as well. Its image is therefore all of $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}B_i$ set-theoretically. The integral incidence is horizontal and generically finite over that image. This proves assertion (1). It does not assert that the raw ring $B_i$ is reduced.

*The horizontal unipotent patterns.* For $\eta=1$, write $N_j=\tau_j-1$. The equations give $N_j^2=0$ and $\phi_jN_j=q_jN_j\phi_j$. At a field point either $N_j=0$, or its kernel and image are the same line. The first alternative is the unramified factor, with $\phi_j$ unrestricted. For the closure of the second alternative use the proper line incidence with adapted matrices <a id="eq:unipotent-incidence"></a>$$\label{eq:unipotent-incidence}
\tau_j=\begin{pmatrix}1&n_j\\0&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
\phi_j=\begin{pmatrix}q_j(1+d_j)&h_j\\0&1+d_j\end{pmatrix}.$$ At a nonzero $N_j$ the relation forces exactly this diagonal ratio. Its chart has three formal matrix coordinates and one slope.

Choose either alternative at each place. The resulting proper incidence has free completed slope charts over $A$, so the same closed-fiber argument proves that it is integral, horizontal, and of dimension $e$. Its projection is an isomorphism where every chosen ramified $N_j$ is nonzero, because the line is then $\mathop{\mathrm{im}}N_j=\ker N_j$. Each image is consequently an integral horizontal closed subset of dimension $e$. These finitely many images cover all geometric points of $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}B_1$.

<a id="eq:tame-special-ring"></a>
*Specialization and uniqueness of the containing component.* The dimension calculation alone is not enough for support transfer: we must know exactly which horizontal component contains each generic special-fiber point. It remains to identify their specializations and distinguish them. The two raw presentations reduce to the same one modulo $\varpi$, since $i\equiv1$ and $q_j$ is odd. At one place write $$\tau=\begin{pmatrix}1+a&b\\c&1+a\end{pmatrix},\qquad
\phi=\begin{pmatrix}1+d&e'\\f&1+g'\end{pmatrix}.$$ The common special matrix factor is $$\label{eq:tame-special-ring}
K[[a,b,c,d,e',f,g']]/
 (a^2+bc,\ b(d+g'),\ c(d+g'),\ bf+ce').$$ Its two reduced components are $$U:\ a=b=c=0,
\qquad
R:\ g'=d,\quad a^2+bc=bf+ce'=0.$$ Both have dimension four and are geometrically integral. For $R$, prove this first in the homogeneous polynomial quotient. On $b\ne0$ one eliminates $c=a^2/b$ and $f=a^2e'/b^2$, obtaining a domain. The locus $b=0$ has dimension three, whereas every component of the two-equation quotient has dimension at least four. There is thus only one component, generically reduced. The quotient is a complete intersection and has no embedded primes, so it is a domain. Completion is the product of its finite homogeneous pieces; lowest-degree nonzero terms show that it remains a domain. The same proof works over every field extension. This argument is made before completion; it does not substitute into arbitrary formal series after inverting $b$.

For several places over $A/\varpi A$, the polynomial products of the chosen $U$ and $R$ factors are flat over that base and have geometrically integral generic fiber. They are domains, and the same homogeneous completion argument applies, completing the base coefficients and then the positive-degree variables. Each pattern therefore gives an integral special-fiber component of dimension $e-1$. Reduction of its proper incidence is integral and maps onto precisely this component. Proper surjectivity onto the image is preserved by base change, so this is the entire underlying specialization of the corresponding horizontal image.

The patterns are distinct. On the generic ramified special piece $\tau_j\ne1$, whereas every unramified piece has $\tau_j=1$. On the generic unramified piece $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\phi_j\ne0$, whereas every ramified special piece has $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\phi_j=0$. Thus no horizontal image contains the generic special point of a different pattern. The images already cover the raw spectrum, proving assertions (2) and (3), including uniqueness of the horizontal component through each special-fiber generic point. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-auto-remark-1"></a>
<div class="remark">

*Remark 14*. The common raw special fiber need not be reduced. In <a href="#eq:tame-special-ring" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tame-special-ring">[eq:tame-special-ring]</a>, the element $a(d+g')$ can be nonzero with square zero. The proper incidence is also not a normalization: over $\tau=\phi=1$ its fiber contains $\mathbf P^1$. Our conclusions concern precisely the spectra, components, and universal matrix actions required for support propagation, and do not make either of these stronger assertions.

</div>

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### Several marked scalar places

The marked local condition used in connectedness is different from the unrestricted dyadic condition. At a scalar residual dyadic representation, record an invariant line and retain no other local restriction. We need the reduced characteristic-two local base, jointly with the real factors, to be integral. Connectedness will use this entire completed product as a domain, so integrality of each individual dyadic factor does not suffice.

<a id="stmt-lem-marked-local-base"></a>
<div id="lem:marked-local-base" class="lemma">

**Lemma 15** (Simultaneous marked factors). *Over a finite characteristic-two field, take $d$ fixed-determinant real framed factors and $m$ dyadic framed factors whose residual matrices are scalar, with one invariant line recorded at each dyadic factor. The determinant is fixed, and after the residual scalar twist its dyadic reduction is one. The reduction of their completed local product is an integral complete-intersection base of dimension $$2d+5m.$$ It remains integral after a finite extension of the residue field. Equivalently, one may first kill the nilpotent torsion parameters in the diagonal character rings; the resulting completed product is already reduced and integral and has the same spectrum as the original product.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-marked-local-base"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-marked-local-base</summary>

*Proof.* Move a marked line in its one-dimensional formal chart and choose the corresponding adapted basis. After the scalar twist, the diagonal entries are characters $\lambda,\lambda^{-1}$ congruent to one. Their reduced character ring is $$C_0=k[[x,y]].$$ Indeed $\mathbb Q_2^\times$ has maximal pro-$2$ quotient $\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z\times\mathbb Z_2^2$, and the first factor contributes only a nilpotent character parameter in characteristic two.

<a id="eq:marked-cocycle"></a>
The maximal pro-$2$ Galois group of $\mathbb Q_2$ has three generators and one relation. The odd-degree dyadic case was established by Serre (Serre 1964, sec. 4.4 and 8) <a id="pm-cite-48"></a>[[Serre1964Demushkin](#ref-Serre1964Demushkin)]; in Labute’s classification, the $q=2$, odd-local-degree case gives generators with relation $g_1^2g_2^4[g_2,g_3]=1$ (Labute 1967, sec. 5, Theorem 8) <a id="pm-cite-49"></a>[[Demushkin](#ref-Demushkin)]. If $b(g)$ is the upper-right entry of the triangular matrix with diagonal entries $\lambda(g),\lambda(g)^{-1}$, put $c(g)=\lambda(g)b(g)$. Matrix multiplication gives the left-cocycle identity $$c(gh)=c(g)+\lambda(g)^2c(h).$$ Its values $u_j=c(g_j)$ satisfy one homogeneous linear equation $$\label{eq:marked-cocycle}
a_1(x,y)u_1+a_2(x,y)u_2+a_3(x,y)u_3=0.$$ All these deformations factor through the maximal pro-$2$ quotient: their reduction is the identity and their finite coefficient quotients have pro-$2$ congruence image. Thus this presentation does describe the complete triangular deformation ring after the diagonal reduction, rather than just a subfunctor of it.

Write $B=1+x=\lambda(g_2)$ and $C=1+y=\lambda(g_3)$. The reduced diagonal relation gives $\lambda(g_1)=B^{-2}$. Using $[g_2,g_3]=g_2^{-1}g_3^{-1}g_2g_3$ and this left-cocycle identity, direct substitution gives $$(a_1,a_2,a_3)=
 \left(\frac{x^4}{B^4},\;
       \frac{x^6}{B^8}+\frac{y^2}{B^2C^2},\;
       \frac{x^2}{B^2C^2}\right).$$ Multiply <a href="#eq:marked-cocycle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:marked-cocycle">[eq:marked-cocycle]</a> by the unit $B^2C^2$ and set $$v=u_3+\frac{x^2C^2}{B^2}u_1+\frac{x^4C^2}{B^6}u_2.$$ This triangular formal change of variables transforms the relation into $x^2v+y^2u_2=0$. Thus the coefficient ideal is exactly $(x^2,y^2)$ and its radical is $(x,y)$; the ideal itself is not radical.

Let $A_\infty$ be the completed real special-fiber product. The real calculation in Proposition <a href="#prop:local-geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:local-geometry">13</a> makes it an excellent Cohen–Macaulay domain of dimension $2d$. Adjoin the independent pairs $x_j,y_j$ and the line variables $s_j$, obtaining $$A_0=A_\infty[[x_j,y_j,s_j:1\le j\le m]].$$ Before completing the upper entries, form the graded algebra <a id="eq:marked-polynomial-product"></a>$$\label{eq:marked-polynomial-product}
P=A_0[u_{jr}:1\le j\le m,\ 1\le r\le3]/(f_1,\ldots,f_m),$$ where each $f_j$ is the corresponding relation <a href="#eq:marked-cocycle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:marked-cocycle">[eq:marked-cocycle]</a>. The coefficients of $f_j$ involve only $x_j,y_j$ and have common-zero locus $(x_j,y_j)$.

On a base stratum where exactly $r$ coefficient rows vanish, the base loses $2r$ dimensions and the upper-entry fiber gains only $r$ dimensions. Thus the corresponding locus in $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}P$ has dimension at most $$\dim A_0+2m-r.$$ Every minimal prime of the homogeneous relation ideal is homogeneous and is contained in $(\mathfrak m_{A_0},u_{jr})$. In that excellent equidimensional local ambient ring, the height theorem for the $m$ equations makes every component have dimension at least $\dim A_0+2m$. No component can therefore lie over a nonempty rank-drop stratum. Where all coefficient rows are nonzero, the quotient is a vector bundle of rank $2m$ over an integral base. There is consequently only one component, generically reduced.

The same argument for every initial subset of the equations shows that their heights are the expected ones. They form a regular sequence in the Cohen–Macaulay ambient ring. The quotient has no embedded associated primes, and generic reducedness now proves that $P$ is a domain.

Finally, each homogeneous piece $P_n$ is a finite, torsion-free $A_0$-module. Over $F_0=\mathop{\mathrm{Frac}}(A_0)$ the $m$ independent linear relations eliminate one upper-entry variable in each triple, so $$\prod_{n\ge0}P_n
 \lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow
\prod_{n\ge0}(P_n\otimes_{A_0}F_0)
 \simeq F_0[[v_1,\ldots,v_{2m}]]$$ is an embedding of rings. Since $A_0$ and its finite modules are complete, the left side is precisely the completed local product in question. It is a domain, of dimension $\dim A_0+2m=2d+5m$. It is also a complete intersection by the regular sequence already exhibited.

Killing the original diagonal torsion parameters changes no prime ideal: each is nilpotent, and the ideal they generate is nilpotent. The obtained quotient is already reduced, proving the assertion about the original reduction. The real normality and the cocycle and dimension arguments hold after every finite constant extension, proving the final assertion. ◻

</details>

The two local outputs will be used separately. Propagation uses the unrestricted real–dyadic base and the correspondence between the horizontal tame components and their common special fiber. Connectedness uses the integral marked base of dimension $2d+5m$. The latter statement includes no normality assertion, and neither construction replaces the raw tame rings by their incidences.

<a id="sec:completed"></a>
### Completed forms and their local parameters

In this section $F,S,\chi,\psi$ have the meaning fixed in Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a>. We construct completed forms and identify their integral local-block action with the restriction of the global Galois action. This comparison gives finiteness over the local pseudodeformation base and describes the Banach coefficient fibers needed for classicality. We then prove a lower bound on every Hecke component and exact auxiliary-level identities preserving the old deformation action; these are the inputs to patching. All local groups at $2$ are $\mathop{\mathrm{GL}}_2(\mathbb Q_2)$, since $2$ splits completely in $F$.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### Integral forms and classical density

Fix an open tame level $U^2$, hyperspecial outside $S$. We may also prescribe a finite-order transformation character at the tame level, compatible with the central character. Write $\widetilde S_\psi(U^2,\mathcal O)$ for the continuous $\mathcal O$-valued functions on $$D_F^\times\backslash(D_F\otimes_F\mathbb A_F^\infty)^\times$$ with the prescribed right tame transformation and central transformation $f(gz)=\psi(z)f(g)$. Its scalar extension to $E$ is a unitary Banach representation of $$G_2=\prod_{v\mid2}\mathop{\mathrm{GL}}_2(F_v).$$ The torsion version has values in $E/\mathcal O$ and is smooth. The compact dual of the integral space is denoted by $M$; equivalently, it is the Pontryagin dual of the torsion space. We also use this notation for a residual Hecke summand.

<a id="stmt-prop-completed-forms"></a>
<div id="prop:completed-forms" class="proposition">

**Proposition 16**. *For the tame level $U^2$, central character $\psi=\chi\varepsilon$, and full compact forms module $M$ just defined, the following properties hold. In the second assertion weights and local parameters use the normalization of Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a>.*

1.  *For a sufficiently small product $K$ of uniform open subgroups of $\mathop{\mathrm{SL}}_2(F_v)$, the full compact module $M$ is finite free over $\mathcal O[[K]]$. The integral and torsion function spaces are admissible and commute with reduction of coefficients.*

2.  *Locally algebraic vectors are classical definite quaternionic forms of the corresponding algebraic weight. Their regular noncharacter eigenpackets transfer to Hilbert cuspidal eigenforms. They have Galois representations of determinant $\chi$, with the local compatibility described below.*

3.  *The regular classical vectors are dense in the Banach space. They remain dense if, at each $v\mid2$, one fixes a smooth principal-series type with two distinct inertial characters and varies the algebraic factor with the prescribed central degree.*

4.  *Every residual local factor $\mathbb T$ of the completed Hecke algebra away from $S$ is a complete quotient of $R_F^{\mathrm{ps},\chi}$. It is reduced and $\mathcal O$-torsion-free, and its spectrum is the closure of its classical coefficient points.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-completed-forms"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-completed-forms</summary>

*Proof.* Finiteness of quaternion ideal classes gives finitely many double cosets at compact open level (Voight 2021, Main Theorem 17.7.1) <a id="pm-cite-50"></a>[[Voight2021](#ref-Voight2021)]; passing to a smaller compact level has finite fibers. Stabilizers modulo the center are finite as well. Their reduced norms have even valuation at every finite place. The group $$\{a\in F^\times:v(a)\in2\mathbb Z\text{ for every finite }v\}
       /(F^\times)^2$$ is finite: the class of the half-valuation ideal maps it to $\operatorname{Cl}(F)[2]$, with kernel given by unit squareclasses, and both groups are finite (Milne 2020a, Theorems 4.4 and 5.1) <a id="pm-cite-51"></a>[[MilneANT](#ref-MilneANT)]. The kernel of reduced norm modulo squares has, after scalar rescaling, norm-one representatives in a fixed compact subgroup; these are finite by total definiteness (Voight 2021, Lemma 17.7.13) <a id="pm-cite-52"></a>[[Voight2021](#ref-Voight2021)]. Shrink $K$ until it meets none of these finite projective stabilizers nontrivially. Choosing local sections for the central and tame transformations identifies the function space with continuous functions on a finite disjoint union of copies of $K$. Its compact dual is a finite direct sum of $\mathcal O[[K]]$. This also proves admissibility and exact coefficient reduction. Compatibility of the transformation characters on their intersections is part of the definition; the central character is totally even, since both $\chi$ and $\varepsilon$ are odd.

The identification of locally algebraic vectors is the definite double-coset description of algebraic automorphic forms. Jacquet–Langlands transfers the regular noncharacter packets to Hilbert cusp forms (Jacquet and Langlands 1970, Theorems 14.4 and 16.1) <a id="pm-cite-53"></a>[[JacquetLanglands1970](#ref-JacquetLanglands1970)]; their Galois representations, weights and de Rhamness are given by (Barnet-Lamb et al. 2014b, Theorem 2.1.1) <a id="pm-cite-54"></a>[[BLGGT2014](#ref-BLGGT2014)]. At the dyadic places we use the potentially semistable comparison, including ramified types: (Barnet-Lamb et al. 2014a, Corollary 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-55"></a>[[BLGGTLocalGlobal2014](#ref-BLGGTLocalGlobal2014)]. For the principal types used here, the two distinct inertial characters force monodromy to be zero. Thus the local representation is potentially crystalline and crystabelian, with the weights specified by the algebraic factor.

We spell out the density assertion with fixed type. Let $K_0=\prod_{v\mid2}\mathop{\mathrm{GL}}_2(\mathcal O_{F_v})$. Matrix coefficients of algebraic representations of central degree zero form a subalgebra of the continuous functions on $K_0/Z(K_0)$. They contain constants and separate points: the adjoint representation is faithful on the projective group. Nonarchimedean polynomial approximation, equivalently Stone–Weierstrass for this compact totally disconnected space, makes this algebra dense.

Choose a nonzero smooth type $\tau$ and an algebraic representation $V$ with the needed central degree. The matrix coefficients $a_1,\ldots,a_N$ of $\tau\otimes V$ have no common zero, since every representation matrix is invertible. For a continuous section $f$ with the prescribed central transformation, choose a finite clopen partition subordinate to sets on which some $a_i$ is bounded away from zero. The functions $f/a_i$ on these clopens, extended by zero, are continuous of central degree zero. Approximate them by the algebra just described and multiply by $a_i$. Every resulting product is a matrix coefficient of $\tau\otimes V\otimes A$, where $A$ is algebraic of central degree zero. Complete reducibility in characteristic zero decomposes $V\otimes A$ into algebraic irreducibles of exactly the same central degree. The smooth factor $\tau$ is unchanged. Averaging over the finitely many projective stabilizers gives density in the double-coset function space; its fixed denominators cause no problem over $E$.

At every dyadic factor we can choose the two inertial characters to be distinct with the required product, using characters of sufficiently large $2$-power order. The associated compact principal-series type forces the smooth local representation to be principal series of that type. Here we use Henniart’s compact types (Breuil and Mézard 2002, Appendix A, Sections A.1.1–A.1.4 and A.2.3–A.2.4) <a id="pm-cite-56"></a>[[BreuilMezard2002](#ref-BreuilMezard2002)], whose scope includes residue cardinality two. In particular it excludes norm characters. The algebraic central degree is common at all embeddings, as required for classical weights. This proves the assertion about a dense span of regular cuspidal packets.

*The integral Hecke algebra and its residual factors.* Form the Hecke algebra as the closure of the integral Hecke operators in the endomorphisms of the finite Iwasawa module. Reduction modulo its maximal augmentation ideal gives a finite-dimensional commuting algebra. Its topologically nilpotent kernel permits lifting idempotents, giving finitely many residual factors.

*Interpolation as an identity of integral operators.* We next construct a continuous Galois trace in this integral Hecke algebra. To pass from classical traces to actual operators, we first specify finite tests for the operator topology. Work with the full compact module $M\simeq\Lambda_K^r$, where $\Lambda_K=\mathcal O[[K]]$ and $\mathfrak a=(\varpi,\ker(\Lambda_K\to\mathcal O))$. The topology on $\mathop{\mathrm{End}}_{\Lambda_K}(M)$ is its $\mathfrak a$-adic matrix topology. An operator $A$ is zero at depth $j$ precisely when $A(M)\subseteq\mathfrak a^jM$, or equivalently when its dual kills the finite $\mathcal O$-module $$Q_j=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_\mathcal O(M/\mathfrak a^jM,E/\mathcal O).$$ Choose finitely many generators of $Q_j$. Exact coefficient reduction in the function model represents them by $\varpi^{-e_i}f_i$ modulo integral functions, with each $f_i$ integral. Thus the finitely many tests $A^\vee f_i\in\varpi^{e_i}\widetilde S_\psi(U^2,\mathcal O)$ detect depth $j$.

Approximate these $f_i$, to the required precisions, by finite sums of classical eigenvectors. Clear the denominators of this finite collection once. Continuity of the finitely many classical Galois traces then makes the corresponding Hecke operators agree to those precisions on the approximations whenever their Frobenius conjugacy classes are sufficiently close. Integral Hecke operators and their differences have norm at most one, so the approximation errors are controlled uniformly. Hence a Chebotarev sequence approaching any Galois conjugacy class is Cauchy at every depth $j$. Completeness gives a unique integral limit, independent of the sequence; the same finite tests prove continuity of the resulting trace function. Restriction to the integral Hecke-idempotent summands gives the assertion for each residual factor, with its inherited operator topology.

*The pseudodeformation quotient and its support.* The determinant is the fixed character $\chi$. The polynomial identities for a two-dimensional determinant hold on all classical packets and hence on their dense span. Boundedness and torsion-freeness make these identities integral. The universal property gives a continuous map from $R_F^{\mathrm{ps},\chi}$ onto the completed trace algebra: the Frobenius traces generate its dense subalgebra, and the source is compact.

An operator vanishing at every classical eigenpacket vanishes on the dense classical span, hence is zero. Classical Hecke actions are semisimple. A nilpotent operator therefore vanishes there, proving reducedness; the action on integral functions proves $\mathcal O$-torsion-freeness. The same test proves the asserted closure of the classical coefficient points. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Blocks, integral finiteness, and Banach fibers

Fix a residual factor $\mathbb T$. Extend $k$ so that the residual local parameters and the simple objects of the corresponding local blocks are absolutely split. For each $v\mid2$, choose one representative from each simple isomorphism class in the smooth block with central character $\psi_v$. Let $P_v$ be the projective envelope of the direct sum of their duals, taken in the compact dual category, and put $E_v=\mathop{\mathrm{End}}(P_v)$. This is the standard block generator of (Paškūnas and Tung 2021, sec. 4.1) <a id="pm-cite-57"></a>[[PaskunasTung2021](#ref-PaskunasTung2021)]. The block parameter is the semisimplification of $\bar r_v\otimes\bar\varepsilon$. Denote the corresponding local pseudodeformation ring by $R_v^{\mathrm{ps}}$; its determinant is $\psi_v\varepsilon=\chi_v\varepsilon^2$. Set $$R_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\mathrm{ps}}
   =\widehat\bigotimes_{v\mid2,\mathcal O} R_v^{\mathrm{ps}},
 \qquad
 E_{\mathrm{loc}}
   =\widehat\bigotimes_{v\mid2,\mathcal O} E_v,
 \qquad
 P=\widehat\bigotimes_{v\mid2,\mathcal O}P_v.$$ Untwisting by $\varepsilon$ identifies this local base with the fixed-$\chi_v$ local parameters of the global problem.

<a id="stmt-prop-block-comparison"></a>
<div id="prop:block-comparison" class="proposition">

**Proposition 17**. *The residual Hecke summand belongs to the product block just specified. The exact projective-generator functor gives $$L=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathrm{cts},G_2}(P,M),$$ taken in the compact dual category of the specified product block, with the right $E_{\mathrm{loc}}$-action by precomposition. It is faithful over $\mathbb T$, finite over $R_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\mathrm{ps}}$, and finite over $\mathbb T$. The local pseudodeformation action on $L$ is the restriction of the global Galois action, with the stated cyclotomic twist. In particular, $\mathbb T$ is finite over $R_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\mathrm{ps}}$.*

*At a coefficient point $x:\mathbb T\to E'$, after finite extension of $E'$, its Banach Hecke eigenspace contains $$\widehat\bigotimes_{v\mid2}\Pi_v,$$ where $\Pi_v$ is an irreducible unitary Banach representation with local parameter $(r_x|_{G_{F_v}}\otimes\varepsilon)^{\mathrm{ss}}$, of determinant $\chi_v\varepsilon^2$, in the block normalization of Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a>. If every local parameter is absolutely irreducible, de Rham, and regular, the eigenspace has a nonzero locally algebraic vector. If the untwisted parameter at $v$ is $\gamma_{1,v}\oplus\gamma_{2,v}$, with ratio outside $\{1,\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{-1}\}$, the possibilities are the continuous unnormalized upper-Borel inductions $$\mathop{\mathrm{Ind}}_B^{\mathop{\mathrm{GL}}_2(\mathbb Q_2)}
       (\gamma_{i,v}\varepsilon\otimes\gamma_{j,v}),
 \qquad \{i,j\}=\{1,2\}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-block-comparison"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-block-comparison</summary>

*Proof.* *The one-factor input.* We first specify the one-factor results. The integral center map and finiteness theorem of Paškūnas–Tung apply to every block, including the scalar residual block at $2$ (Paškūnas and Tung 2021, Theorems 1.1–1.4) <a id="pm-cite-58"></a>[[PaskunasTung2021](#ref-PaskunasTung2021)]. In particular $E_v$ is finite over $R_v^{\mathrm{ps}}$; an integral-surjectivity claim about the center is unnecessary. More precisely, integral finiteness is Theorem 1.3; the center comparison in Theorem 1.4 can have integral cokernel killed by $2$. Their compact projective-generator equivalence is exact. Their generic reducible and absolutely irreducible characteristic-zero fibers are described in (Paškūnas and Tung 2021, Corollary 6.10 and Proposition 6.11) <a id="pm-cite-59"></a>[[PaskunasTung2021](#ref-PaskunasTung2021)]. The all-prime correspondence and locally algebraic comparison are supplied by (Colmez, Dospinescu, and Paškūnas 2014, Theorems 1.1 and 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-60"></a>[[CDP2014](#ref-CDP2014)] and (Colmez 2010, Theorem 0.20) <a id="pm-cite-61"></a>[[Colmez2010](#ref-Colmez2010)]. Thus the old residual-scalar restriction at $2$ does not enter the present application.

*The product category.* These statements extend to the required product category by the argument of (Gee and Newton 2022, Lemmas B.7–B.8) <a id="pm-cite-62"></a>[[GeeNewton2022](#ref-GeeNewton2022)], adapted from the projective group to a fixed central character using the all-prime inputs just stated. Here are the details. Given a smooth admissible vector, fix compact opens in all but one factor. Its cyclic span under the remaining factor is finitely generated and admissible, so is finite length by (Emerton 2010, Lemma 2.3.6 and Theorem 2.3.8) <a id="pm-cite-63"></a>[[Emerton2010OrdinaryI](#ref-Emerton2010OrdinaryI)]. The commuting actions give a surjection from the tensor product of the one-factor cyclic spans onto the full cyclic span. Absolutely split simple factors tensor to simple product objects; filtering proves finite length. Hence the smooth product representation is locally finite.

Apply the one-factor compact equivalences successively. On dual finite-length objects this can be checked on simple tensors and extended by exactness. Taking inverse limits gives the equivalence for the compact locally finite category, with endomorphism ring $E_{\mathrm{loc}}$. Admissibility gives finite multiplicities in the cosocle: a homomorphism from a simple is detected by a nonzero compact-fixed generating vector. There are only finitely many simples in the block. Compact Nakayama therefore makes $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}(P,M)$ finite over $E_{\mathrm{loc}}$.

*Identifying the actual local and global actions.* It remains to identify the block and its central action with the global Galois restriction. Test one factor at a time on the dense classical principal types of Proposition <a href="#prop:completed-forms" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:completed-forms">16</a>. Local-global compatibility gives a regular crystabelian Galois parameter with distinct inertial characters. For an irreducible parameter, the universal unitary completion of its algebraic-smooth principal series is the corresponding Banach representation by (Berger and Breuil 2010, Theorem 4.3.1 and Corollaries 5.3.1–5.3.4) <a id="pm-cite-64"></a>[[BergerBreuil2010](#ref-BergerBreuil2010)] and the locally algebraic comparison in (Colmez, Dospinescu, and Paškūnas 2014) <a id="pm-cite-65"></a>[[CDP2014](#ref-CDP2014)]. Distinct inertia excludes both equal-root and special degeneracies.

For an ordinary parameter with cyclotomic exponents $a>b$, the algebraic factor is $$\operatorname{Sym}^{a-b-1} E^2\otimes\det^{b+1}.$$ The ordinary completion theorem (Breuil and Emerton 2010, Proposition 2.2.1) <a id="pm-cite-66"></a>[[BreuilEmerton2010](#ref-BreuilEmerton2010)] identifies the relevant locally algebraic induction with a dense subspace of the continuous unitary induction in the order $$(\eta_l\varepsilon,\eta_h),$$ where $\eta_h,\eta_l$ have exponents $a,b$. The following character calculation fixes the inducing order on the dense ordinary test types; that order will matter again in Section <a href="#sec:classicality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:classicality">8</a>. For the precise normalization, put $n=a-b-1\geq0$ and $\delta=(\eta_l\varepsilon)\eta_h^{-1}$. Twisting by the full unitary character $\eta_h^{-1}\circ\det$ reduces this induction to $\mathop{\mathrm{Ind}}_B^{\mathop{\mathrm{GL}}_2(\mathbb Q_2)}(\delta\otimes1)^{C^0}$. With $x$ the identity character and $\varepsilon=x|\cdot|$, the source characters in the cited proposition, with $k=n+2$, are $$\chi_1=|\cdot|x^{-n},\qquad
 \chi_2=\delta|\cdot|^{-1}x^n.$$ Since $\delta$ is unitary of algebraic exponent $-n$, both $\chi_2$ and $(\chi_2/\chi_1)x^{-n}$ are smooth, and $$v_2(\chi_1(2))=-n-1,\qquad
 v_2(\chi_2(2))=n+1.$$ The two characters in its completion formula are $\chi_1|\cdot|^{-1}x^n=1$ and $\chi_2|\cdot|x^{-n}=\delta$, in the reversed inducing order $(\delta,1)$. Twisting back by $\eta_h\circ\det$ gives the displayed order and changes the algebraic factor $\det^{-n}\operatorname{Sym}^n$ into $\det^{b+1}\operatorname{Sym}^{a-b-1}$. On each lower-coordinate disk its vectors are precisely bounded-degree polynomial sections. The theorem concerns this locally algebraic subspace, not the whole locally analytic induction. When $n=0$, the excluded equality $\chi_1=\chi_2|\cdot|^2$ is exactly $\delta=1$, which is ruled out by the distinct finite inertial characters. The ordinary and nonordinary statements together supply every test type used in the density argument; see also (Paškūnas and Tung 2021, Propositions 5.3–5.6) <a id="pm-cite-67"></a>[[PaskunasTung2021](#ref-PaskunasTung2021)].

A map from such a classical constituent into completed forms extends continuously from its universal unitary completion. After scaling lattices, it is a bounded map in the compact block category. Naturality of the center then identifies the central trace action on its image with the Galois parameter given by classical local-global compatibility. The normalizations give $\rho\otimes\varepsilon$, as stipulated above. The dense classical span proves equality of the two actions on the whole Banach space. Torsion-freeness of the integral lattice gives integral equality and shows that every other block projection vanishes.

*Integral finiteness and coefficient fibers.* It follows from integral Paškūnas–Tung finiteness that $L$ is finite over $R_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\mathrm{ps}}$. Exactness and the equivalence imply that the action of $\mathbb T$ on $L$ is faithful. The same finite generators generate $L$ over $\mathbb T$. Moreover, $$\mathbb T\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow
 \mathop{\mathrm{End}}_{R_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\mathrm{ps}}}(L);$$ the right side is finite over the noetherian local base. Thus $\mathbb T$ is finite over that base.

At $x$, the quotient of $L[1/2]$ by the Hecke maximal ideal is nonzero and finite-dimensional. Select a simple quotient after a finite coefficient extension. The commuting images of the factor endomorphism rings generate a finite-dimensional algebra. Their radicals kill the simple quotient; their central idempotents select semisimple factors; after splitting those factors, the quotient is a tensor product of simple modules for matrix algebras. The original integral actions preserve a lattice. All full finite lattices in this quotient are commensurable. Under the inverse compact equivalence, the tensor lattice corresponds to the completed tensor product of the individual Banach lattices. Dualizing the quotient therefore embeds the asserted Banach tensor product in the eigenspace.

For an absolutely irreducible regular de Rham parameter, the local factor has a nonzero locally algebraic vector, so their tensor does as well. For a generic reducible parameter, substituting $\gamma_i\varepsilon$ into the fiber formula of (Paškūnas and Tung 2021, Corollary 6.10) <a id="pm-cite-68"></a>[[PaskunasTung2021](#ref-PaskunasTung2021)] gives exactly the two displayed inducing orders. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### A lower bound on every Hecke component

We next obtain a dimension bound that will supply the initial support size for patching. For a finitely generated compact $k[[K]]$-module, its growth degree is the degree of growth of its length modulo powers of the augmentation ideal.

<a id="stmt-lem-simple-growth"></a>
<div id="lem:simple-growth" class="lemma">

**Lemma 18**. *Let $k$ be the characteristic-two residue coefficient field of Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a>. The compact dual of an absolutely irreducible admissible smooth $k$-representation of $\mathop{\mathrm{GL}}_2(\mathbb Q_2)$ with central character has growth degree at most one on a sufficiently deep determinant-one compact subgroup. Finite-length objects for $d$ factors have growth degree at most $d$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-simple-growth"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-simple-growth</summary>

*Proof.* Write $K_n=1+2^nM_2(\mathbb Z_2)$, $n\ge2$. Characters have bounded invariant dimension. For a principal series, the double cosets indexed by $\mathbb P^1(\mathbb Z/2^n\mathbb Z)$ give $O(2^n)$ invariants. The Steinberg case follows from the constants–principal-series–Steinberg exact sequence: its possible $H^1(K_n,k)$ correction is bounded, since $K_n$ has four generators.

For a supersingular $\pi$, let $$I_1=\begin{pmatrix}1+2\mathbb Z_2&\mathbb Z_2\\
                    2\mathbb Z_2&1+2\mathbb Z_2\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad \Pi=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\2&0\end{pmatrix}.$$ Thus $I_1$ is the standard pro-$2$ Iwahori subgroup. Let $M_\pi$ be the span of the translates of $\pi^{I_1}$ under $\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2^n&b\\0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)$, where $n\ge0$ and $b\in\mathbb Z_2$; this space is Iwahori-stable (Paškūnas 2010, Definition 4.5 and Lemma 4.6) <a id="pm-cite-69"></a>[[Paskunas2010](#ref-Paskunas2010)]. Use the exact sequence of Iwahori representations $$0\longrightarrow \pi^{I_1}\longrightarrow
 M_\pi\oplus\Pi M_\pi\longrightarrow\pi\longrightarrow0$$ from (Paškūnas 2010, Theorem 1.2, proved as Theorem 6.3) <a id="pm-cite-70"></a>[[Paskunas2010](#ref-Paskunas2010)]. That statement explicitly includes $p=2$. By (Paškūnas 2010, Proposition 4.7 and Lemma 4.10) <a id="pm-cite-71"></a>[[Paskunas2010](#ref-Paskunas2010)], $M_\pi$ is the sum of two injective envelopes for the upper unipotent group $\mathbb Z_2$. Each has $2^n$-dimensional invariants under $2^n\mathbb Z_2$. Conjugation by $\Pi$ shifts the congruence level by at most one. Since $\dim\pi^{I_1}=2$, the invariants sequence gives $$\dim_k\pi^{K_n}\le 2\cdot2^n+2\cdot2^{n+1}+8.$$ The last term bounds $H^1(K_n,\pi^{I_1})$. The classification of Barthel–Livné and Breuil (Barthel and Livné 1994, 1995) <a id="pm-cite-72"></a>[[BarthelLivne1994](#ref-BarthelLivne1994), [BarthelLivne1995](#ref-BarthelLivne1995)], in the form of (Breuil 2003, Corollary 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-73"></a>[[Breuil2003I](#ref-Breuil2003I)], shows that the cases above exhaust the irreducible admissible smooth representations with central character.

Let $I$ be the augmentation ideal of a fixed deep uniform compact group. In characteristic two, the operators $g^{2^n}-1=(g-1)^{2^n}$ belong to $I^{2^n}$. Thus the compact quotient modulo $I^{2^n}$ is bounded in dimension by the dual $K_{n+c}$-invariants, for a fixed shift $c$. Choosing $2^n$ comparable to an arbitrary exponent gives linear augmentation growth. The central character is trivial on a sufficiently deep scalar subgroup, and the product of that subgroup with a deep determinant-one subgroup has finite index. The same upper bound therefore holds on the latter. Finite extensions preserve upper growth bounds, and tensor products add them. This proves the product assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-hecke-dimension"></a>
<div id="prop:hecke-dimension" class="proposition">

**Proposition 19**. *For every irreducible component $C$ of $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathbb T$, $$\dim C\ge1+2d.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-hecke-dimension"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-hecke-dimension</summary>

*Proof.* Reducedness and prime avoidance give an element of $\mathbb T$ that vanishes on every component except $C$ and is nonzero on $C$. Its image on $M$ is a nonzero submodule $M'$ supported on $C$. The full $M$ is finite free over a sufficiently deep Iwasawa algebra $A=\mathcal O[[K]]$. The residual summand and $M'$ embed in this free module.

The maximal augmentation filtration, including $\varpi$, has polynomial associated graded ring of dimension $1+3d$, after taking $K$ sufficiently deep. A nonzero submodule of a finite free $A$-module has that full growth degree: it contains a cyclic copy of $A$, and Artin–Rees compares its induced filtration with its intrinsic filtration. Consequently $M'$ has growth degree $1+3d$.

Let $r=\dim C$, and choose $r$ parameters of the supporting Hecke quotient. Write $J$ for their ideal. Proposition <a href="#prop:block-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:block-comparison">17</a> and exactness of the block functor show that $M'/JM'$ is dual finite length. Lemma <a href="#lem:simple-growth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:simple-growth">18</a> bounds its growth degree by $d$. Each Hecke parameter is topologically nilpotent; on the finite quotient $M'/\mathfrak m_A M'$, some fixed power is zero. Hence there is $c>0$ such that $$J^{cn}M'\subseteq\mathfrak m_A^nM'
 \quad(n\ge1).$$ Filter $M'/\mathfrak m_A^nM'$ by $J$. The degree-$j$ layer is a quotient of at most $\binom{j+r-1}{r-1}$ copies of $$(M'/JM')/\mathfrak m_A^n(M'/JM').$$ Only $cn$ layers occur. Their total length is therefore $O(n^{r+d})$. Comparing with the degree $1+3d$ gives $1+3d\le r+d$, which is the claim. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### Auxiliary level and solvable base change

The dimension bound supplies the initial support size. Patching also requires that removing auxiliary level recover the original operators, not just modules of the same rank. The following exact statements connect completed forms to the finite stages of localized patching. An auxiliary prime has residue cardinality $q_v\equiv1\pmod{2^n}$. Inside its Iwahori group, take the kernel of the diagonal-ratio map to a cyclic quotient of residue units of order $2^n$. This subgroup contains the scalars. For $h$ auxiliary primes, the deck group is $\Delta=(\mathbb Z/2^n)^h$.

<a id="stmt-lem-auxiliary-level"></a>
<div id="lem:auxiliary-level" class="lemma">

**Lemma 20**. *Assume that $\chi$ is unramified at the auxiliary primes. Localize at a characteristic-two point of the completed Hecke spectrum at which their Frobenii have distinct eigenvalues. The point may be the generic point of an enhanced curve, rather than the original finite-field residual point. The following assertions hold for the localized completed-forms block modules, and continue to hold after the coefficient extensions, completions, and smooth framings used in Section <a href="#sec:propagation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:propagation">5</a>.*

1.  *The auxiliary-level module is flat over $\mathcal O[\Delta]$. Diamond augmentation is the Iwahori module; after localization at the indicated point it is the direct sum of $2^h$ copies of the old hyperspecial module, with its Galois-ring action factoring through the old unramified quotient.*

2.  *If $U_j$ is a diamond generator and $\tau_j$ is the corresponding tame inertia element, then $$U_j^2-T(\tau_j)U_j+1=0.$$ Congruent tame transformation characters give isomorphic residue modules with the same deformation actions.*

3.  *A quadratic character trivial above $2$ and at infinity, unramified away from the auxiliary primes, and factoring through the auxiliary character quotient, acts by twisting forms. It gives an actual semilinear isomorphism of the block module with its Galois twist, preserving the central character, the level, and the characteristic-two completion point.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-auxiliary-level"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-auxiliary-level</summary>

*Proof.* The finite disjoint-union function model used in Proposition <a href="#prop:completed-forms" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:completed-forms">16</a> is free under the deck action, since the ratio quotient kills scalars and the level was chosen to remove projective stabilizers. Its compact dual is therefore flat over $\mathcal O[\Delta]$, with augmentation equal to the Iwahori-level module. Apply the exact projective functor to the finite-presentation ideal tests for flatness. This proves flatness of the block module and commutation of reduction with that functor.

At one auxiliary prime, let $\alpha:M_{\mathrm{old}}^{\oplus2}\to M_{\mathrm{Iw}}$ be the two degeneracy maps and $\beta:M_{\mathrm{Iw}}\to M_{\mathrm{old}}^{\oplus2}$ their trace maps. Their composite $C=\beta\alpha$ has diagonal entries $q_v+1$ and off-diagonal entries given by the unramified Hecke operator, up to central units. Write $D=\det C$. On the Iwahori module, $D$ denotes the same scalar expression in the interpolated Frobenius trace and central operators. In particular this is one compatible scalar operator on both levels, not a determinant defined only on the old module. The standard degeneracy calculation expresses it, up to a central unit, as $$(q_v+1)^2-q_v\chi(\phi_v)^{-1}T(\phi_v)^2,$$ where $\phi_v$ is a Frobenius lift. At the chosen characteristic-two point, its value is a unit times the nonzero square of the Frobenius trace. Thus $D$ becomes invertible in this localization.

Before inverting $D$, we have the integral identity $$\alpha\operatorname{adj}(C)\beta
       =D\,\operatorname{id}_{M_{\mathrm{Iw}}}.$$ The local alternatives here are the Iwahori component of Henniart’s description (Breuil and Mézard 2002, Appendix A, Sections A.1.3 and A.2.3) <a id="pm-cite-74"></a>[[BreuilMezard2002](#ref-BreuilMezard2002)]. Indeed an Iwahori-spherical classical cuspidal local representation is unramified principal series or special. In the former case its two Iwahori vectors are spanned by the degeneracies, and the identity follows from the matrix adjugate identity. In the latter case the old space is zero and the Frobenius eigenvalue ratio is $q_v^{\pm1}$, so the displayed scalar expression for $D$ is zero as well. Classical density and torsion-freeness prove the identity on integral completed forms and their compact duals. Consequently $D^{-1}\operatorname{adj}(C)\beta$ is both a left and a right inverse to $\alpha$ after the selected localization. Iterating gives $2^h$ old copies.

The maps are Hecke equivariant. Interpolation identifies their Galois trace actions; at the absolutely irreducible enhanced curve, the framed representation action is consequently the actual old unramified action. Thus the augmentation factors through the old deformation quotient, with its original action, rather than only having the same multiplicity. The flatness and Iwahori augmentation established before localization remain valid at the selected point on both sides. The exact block functor and the finite-ideal tests used in Section <a href="#sec:propagation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:propagation">5</a> preserve them under the indicated flat coefficient extensions, localizations, completions, and smooth framings.

On a classical point with a diamond eigencharacter, local Langlands gives semisimple inertia characters equal to that character and its inverse, because $\chi$ is unramified. Their sum is $T(\tau_j)$. The displayed monic quadratic identity follows at every such point, and hence on the integral module by density. Fixed tame character transformations congruent modulo $\varpi$ identify their residue function spaces. The associated trace comparisons show that this identification also preserves the residue deformation actions.

For the last assertion, multiply a form by the quadratic character composed with reduced norm. The central character is unchanged because its value on a scalar norm is a square. On the ratio-defined auxiliary subgroup, the two residue diagonal entries have the same image in the character quotient; their product is therefore killed by a quadratic character. Thus the level is preserved. Triviality at $2$ and infinity preserves the local actions there, and the Galois parameter twists by the same character. Its residual reduction is one, so it fixes the characteristic-two point and its residual frames. This gives the asserted semilinear intertwiner.

Finally, adding a coefficient variable preserves flatness by the complete-module local criterion. Localization, completion of finite noetherian modules, and smooth framing extension preserve the stated flatness and module identities. The intertwiners are local and preserve maximal-ideal filtrations. Hence every assertion survives the operations used at the finite patching stages. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-forward-basechange"></a>
<div id="lem:forward-basechange" class="lemma">

**Lemma 21**. *Let $F'/F$ be a solvable totally real Galois extension split completely at $2$. The restriction to $F'$ of a pro-modular trace locus over $F$ is pro-modular over $F'$, at one common tame level for the whole restricted locus. If all its bad inertial types are killed, this level can be taken Iwahori at the bad non-dyadic primes and hyperspecial elsewhere away from $2$. For a general solvable totally real extension split completely at $2$, the restricted locus is potentially pro-modular.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-forward-basechange"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-forward-basechange</summary>

*Proof.* First suppose that $F'/F$ is Galois. Transfer the dense classical points by solvable base change, iterated through prime-degree cyclic extensions (Arthur and Clozel 1989, vol. 120, chap. III, Theorems 4.2 and 5.1) <a id="pm-cite-75"></a>[[ArthurClozel1989](#ref-ArthurClozel1989)]; for the original $\mathop{\mathrm{GL}}_2$ construction see (Langlands 1980, vol. 96, chap. 11, Lemma 11.3 and Proposition 11.4) <a id="pm-cite-76"></a>[[Langlands1980](#ref-Langlands1980)]. Apply the unitary formulation after a norm twist when necessary, then undo the twist. Use Jacquet–Langlands (Jacquet and Langlands 1970, Theorems 14.4 and 16.1) <a id="pm-cite-77"></a>[[JacquetLanglands1970](#ref-JacquetLanglands1970)]. A regular cuspidal packet stays cuspidal along each prime-degree cyclic step: a loss of cuspidality would make it induced from a character of a totally real quadratic field, whose parallel algebraic exponents contradict regularity. Local-global compatibility and the uniform inertia control of Lemma <a href="#lem:inertia-basechange" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:inertia-basechange">12</a> bound the conductors at a fixed finite set of places. The wild kernel is common and the rank is two, so the possible tame monodromy contribution is uniformly bounded. Thus a single tame level contains every transferred packet.

Their trace closure contains the restricted locus by density and continuity. Thus this locus is pro-modular over the specified field $F'$, at the common tame level just chosen. After the inertial types are killed, only unramified principal or special local factors remain at a bad non-dyadic prime, both with Iwahori invariants. The stated level follows.

For a general solvable extension, pass to its solvable Galois closure. It is still totally real and completely split at $2$. The Galois case then proves potential pro-modularity of the restricted locus. We use only this forward base-change assertion here; classical descent is applied later to an actual cuspidal packet. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:propagation"></a>
### Propagation from a characteristic-two curve

The input to this section is a pro-modular curve whose generic representation is not virtually solvable. We prove that completed forms have full support on the deformation germ at that curve. The residue field of the germ is a Laurent-series field; accordingly, the patching argument below uses finite jets, rather than compactness of a finite residue field.

The argument follows the auxiliary-level framework of Taylor–Wiles (Taylor and Wiles 1995) <a id="pm-cite-78"></a>[[TaylorWiles1995](#ref-TaylorWiles1995)], its formulation relative to local rings by Kisin (Kisin 2009b, sec. 3.3) <a id="pm-cite-79"></a>[[Kisin2009FiniteFlat](#ref-Kisin2009FiniteFlat)], and the characteristic-two determinant-twist construction of Khare–Wintenberger (Khare and Wintenberger 2009b, sec. 9.1.3) <a id="pm-cite-80"></a>[[KhareWintenberger2009II](#ref-KhareWintenberger2009II)]. Pan’s localized propagation argument is the global predecessor (Pan 2022) <a id="pm-cite-81"></a>[[Pan2022](#ref-Pan2022)]. Here the curve residue field requires explicit control of finite jets and fixed old-level quotients. We first construct finite auxiliary stages, then preserve their filtration in the limit, and finally use twists, sign intertwiners and diamond augmentation to recover support with the original deformation action.

<a id="stmt-thm-localized-propagation"></a>
<div id="thm:localized-propagation" class="theorem">

**Theorem 22** (Localized propagation). *Let $F$ be a totally real solvable field of even degree, completely split at $2$, and let $S$ contain its real and dyadic places and the allowed finite ramification. Fix the determinant $\chi=\det r=\chi_{\mathrm{fin}}\varepsilon^w$ of a target $r$ as in Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a>, a semisimple residual representation with determinant $\bar\chi$, and let $R_F^{\mathrm{ps},\chi}$ be the corresponding complete local global pseudodeformation ring. Let $C$ be a characteristic-two curve in $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}R_F^{\mathrm{ps},\chi}$. Suppose that $C$ is potentially pro-modular, that its generic representation is not virtually solvable, and that its local images at the non-dyadic finite places in $S$ are finite. Every irreducible closed locus of $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}R_F^{\mathrm{ps},\chi}$ containing $C$ is potentially pro-modular, with ramification allowed only at the specified set $S$ before base change.*

</div>

We will prove the theorem at the end of the section. First make a totally real solvable Galois base change, split completely above $2$, on which the curve is pro-modular. Lemma <a href="#lem:inertia-basechange" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:inertia-basechange">12</a>, applied both to the source deformation rings and to the Hecke algebras, permits a further such base change with the following properties: the curve representation is trivial at every bad finite place; the determinant $\chi$ is unramified there; the source has unipotent inertia there; and each bad residue cardinality is $1$ modulo $4$. By Lemma <a href="#lem:forward-basechange" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:forward-basechange">21</a>, the curve is then in the support of forms with Iwahori level at the bad places and hyperspecial level elsewhere away from $2$. The assertion about the source is set-theoretic, which is sufficient for the theorem.

Retain the notation $F,S$ after these base changes, and put $$d=[F:\mathbb Q],\qquad
 u=\#\{v\in S:v\nmid 2\infty\},\qquad s=2d+u.$$ Use Lemma <a href="#lem:curve-completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:curve-completion">7</a> to enhance the curve by $z$ and complete. Its coefficient ring and residue field are $$\mathcal L=\widehat{\mathcal O[[z]]_{(\varpi)}},\qquad K=k((t)),
 \qquad z\longmapsto t.$$ Finite extensions of these coefficients are allowed throughout. Write $\rho_*:G_{F,S}\to\mathop{\mathrm{GL}}_2(K)$ for the curve representation. It is absolutely irreducible, and remains so under the base changes just made. All local and global deformation rings in this section are completed at this same enhanced curve and have residue field $K$.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### Auxiliary primes and characters

Character twists will enlarge the support obtained from completed forms. Since twisting multiplies the determinant by the square of the character, we need a variable-determinant problem as well as the fixed-determinant one. In the former, the determinant is fixed at the dyadic and real places and on bad inertia, but is allowed to vary globally. Enlarge $\mathcal O$ so that $i^2=-1$ belongs to it. At a bad place, choose a tame inertia generator $\tau$ and an arithmetic Frobenius $\phi$ with $\phi\tau\phi^{-1}=\tau^{q_v}$. For $\eta\in\{1,i\}$ impose $$\det\rho(\tau)=1,\qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\rho(\tau)=\eta+\eta^{-1}.$$ The determinant of $\rho(\phi)$ is unrestricted in the variable problem. The local curve is trivial, so wild inertia and prime-to-two tame inertia disappear in these completed problems. Denote the completed product of the framed local rings at $S$ by $R_{\mathrm{loc},\eta}$. The $\eta=1$ condition contains the source with unipotent bad inertia. The $\eta=i$ condition provides an irreducible comparison model: Proposition <a href="#prop:local-geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:local-geometry">13</a> identifies its special fiber with that of the $1$-model and specifies the horizontal components through each generic special-fiber point. We will use this comparison to transfer support to every component of the $1$-model.

For a set $Q_n$ of auxiliary primes, let $R_{\eta,n}$ be the $S$-framed global ring with these local conditions and unrestricted additional ramification at $Q_n$. A superscript $f$ denotes the quotient imposing the global determinant $\chi$. Write $R_\eta$ and $R_\eta^f$ for the corresponding rings without $Q_n$. Thus forgetting the additional ramification gives quotient maps $$R_{\eta,n}\twoheadrightarrow R_\eta,
 \qquad R_{\eta,n}^f\twoheadrightarrow R_\eta^f.$$ The deformation interpretation over $\mathcal L$ and noetherianity follow from Lemma <a href="#lem:curve-completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:curve-completion">7</a> and the relative tangent and obstruction calculation of Proposition <a href="#prop:relative-presentation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:relative-presentation">9</a>.

<a id="stmt-lem-auxiliary-primes"></a>
<div id="lem:auxiliary-primes" class="lemma">

**Lemma 23** (Auxiliary-prime bound). *There is a sufficiently large integer $h$, independent of $n$, and sets $Q_n$ of $h$ primes outside $S$ such that:*

1.  *every prime in $Q_n$ splits in $F(\zeta_{2^n},2^{1/2^n})$ and has residue cardinality $1$ modulo $2^n$;*

2.  *its $\rho_*$-Frobenius is regular semisimple, with both eigenvalues in one fixed finite extension of $K$;*

<a id="eq:patching-presentation"></a>
3.  *there are surjections $$\label{eq:patching-presentation}
     B_\eta:=R_{\mathrm{loc},\eta}[[X_1,\ldots,X_g]]
     \twoheadrightarrow R_{\eta,n},\qquad g=2h+1.$$*

*The two sets of maps in <a href="#eq:patching-presentation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:patching-presentation">[eq:patching-presentation]</a>, for $\eta=1,i$, can be chosen to agree modulo $\varpi$. For each $\eta$, their composites with $R_{\eta,n}\twoheadrightarrow R_\eta$ can also be chosen independently of $n$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-auxiliary-primes"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-auxiliary-primes</summary>

*Proof.* Put $M=\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\rho_*$. The pairing $(A,B)\mapsto\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(AB)$ on $M_2(K)$ is perfect also in characteristic two. Thus the dual coefficient module is again $M$: the mod-two cyclotomic twist is trivial. The relative Selmer condition is zero at $S$ and full at $Q_n$. The dual condition therefore consists of classes unramified outside $S$ whose localizations at $Q_n$ vanish.

Set $$F_\infty=F(\zeta_{2^\infty},2^{1/2^\infty}),\qquad
 H=\ker(G_{F,S}\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Gal}}(F_\infty/F)).$$ The tower group is solvable. Nonvirtual solvability of $\rho_*$ therefore implies that the geometric reduced Zariski closure of $\rho_*(H)$ contains $\mathop{\mathrm{SL}}_2$; the same is true for every open subgroup of $H$. Indeed, take the closure over an algebraic closure of $K$. Its smooth connected derived group lies in $\mathop{\mathrm{SL}}_2$. If proper, it has dimension at most two and is solvable, forcing the identity component itself to be solvable, a contradiction (Milne 2022, Corollaries 6.19 and 17.27) <a id="pm-cite-82"></a>[[MilneAlgebraicGroups](#ref-MilneAlgebraicGroups)]. Consequently regular semisimple elements are dense in every open coset of $H$.

We give the detection argument explicitly. Let $c\in Z^1(G_{F,S},M)$ have zero diagonal evaluation at every regular semisimple $\sigma\in H$. Then the first-order representation $$\rho_c(\sigma)=(1+\epsilon c(\sigma))\rho_*(\sigma),
 \qquad \epsilon^2=0,$$ has the same trace as $\rho_*$ on those elements. Indeed, the quotient $$M/(\operatorname{Ad}(\rho_*(\sigma))-1)M$$ is the diagonal subspace in an eigenbasis, and zero evaluation kills both diagonal entries. Continuity and the density just observed give equality of traces on all of $H$.

Choose $h_1,\ldots,h_4\in H$ such that $A_j=\rho_*(h_j)$ form a basis of $M_2(K)$. The lifts $\rho_c(h_j)$ form a basis over $K[\epsilon]$. The Gram matrix $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(A_jA_k)$ is invertible, and equality of traces on products of two and three group elements shows that this Gram matrix and all multiplication structure constants are unchanged after lifting. The linear map taking $A_j$ to $\rho_c(h_j)$ is therefore an algebra automorphism of $M_2(K[\epsilon])$, reducing to the identity. Such an automorphism is conjugation by a matrix congruent to the identity: this follows, for example, by carrying a system of matrix units to its image and choosing the corresponding basis. Pairing with the four basis matrices then shows that this conjugation carries $\rho_*|_H$ to $\rho_c|_H$ on every element of $H$. Hence $[c]|_H=0$.

The invariants $M^H$ are the scalars. Inflation–restriction bounds the kernel of all these evaluations by $$H^1(\mathop{\mathrm{Gal}}(F_\infty/F),K).$$ This space has dimension at most two. To see the last assertion, the corresponding tower over $\mathbb Q$ is pro-two and unramified away from $2$. Its quadratic subfields are $\mathbb Q(i)$, $\mathbb Q(\sqrt2)$, and $\mathbb Q(\sqrt{-2})$. Its elementary two-character rank is therefore two. The field $F$ is disjoint from that tower: a nontrivial intersection would have a quadratic subfield, whereas none of these three fields has $2$ split completely. Thus the tower group over $F$ has the same two-character rank.

The ambient cohomology space is finite dimensional. A finite list of regular elements consequently detects its quotient by a subspace of dimension at most two. Extend $K$ once to split the eigenvalues of this list. Both regular split eigenvalues and the nonvanishing minors of the evaluation matrix persist on sufficiently small neighborhoods. For each $n$, Chebotarev approximation supplies Frobenius elements in these neighborhoods which are trivial at the required finite stage of the tower (Milne 2020b, V, Theorem 3.23) <a id="pm-cite-83"></a>[[MilneCFT](#ref-MilneCFT)]. All conditions concern finite quotients after the neighborhoods have been chosen. Extra such primes can be added, so the cardinality can be any fixed sufficiently large $h$. The dual Selmer dimension is then at most two.

The auxiliary evaluations have reduced the undetected dual Selmer space to at most two scalar tower directions. The next count turns this into the uniform presentation bound $g=2h+1$. For completeness, write $h^j_v=\dim_K H^j(G_{F_v},M)$. Local duality and Euler characteristics give $$h^1_v=
 \begin{cases}
 2h^0_v+4[F_v:\mathbb Q_2],&v\mid2,\\
 2h^0_v-4,&v\text{ real},\\
 2h^0_v,&v\nmid2\infty.
 \end{cases}$$ For trivial complex conjugation, the dimensions are $(h^0_v,h^1_v)=(4,4)$. For nontrivial unipotent complex conjugation they are $(2,0)$. Thus the dyadic contribution $4d$ cancels the real contribution $-4d$. The localization image in the direct sum of the local $H^1$ spaces is its own annihilator under Poitou–Tate. For a subspace of local conditions $L$, elementary linear algebra therefore gives the Selmer difference as $\dim L-\tfrac12\sum_v h^1_v$. Here $L$ is zero at $S$ and full at $Q_n$. At each auxiliary prime regularity gives $h^0_v=2$ and $h^1_v=4$. Consequently the identity is $$h^1_{\mathrm{Sel}}(M)-h^1_{\mathrm{Sel}^{\perp}}(M)
   =-\sum_{v\in S}h^0_v+2h.$$ The finite-coefficient formulas are (Milne 2006, I, Corollary 2.3, Theorems 2.8, 2.13, 4.10, and 5.1) <a id="pm-cite-84"></a>[[Milne2006](#ref-Milne2006)]; their passage to $K$ is the lattice-and-limit argument of Lemma <a href="#lem:curve-cohomology" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:curve-cohomology">8</a>. With independent $S$-frames the relative tangent dimension is $$h^1_{\mathrm{Sel}}(M)+\sum_{v\in S}h^0_v-h^0(G_{F,S},M)
 =h^1_{\mathrm{Sel}^{\perp}}(M)+2h-1
 \leq 2h+1.$$ *Generators with a fixed old composite.* To obtain the last assertion as well as the variable bound, write $A_\eta=R_{\mathrm{loc},\eta}$ and $I_{\eta,n}=\ker(R_{\eta,n}\to R_\eta)$. The relative cotangent spaces are $$Q_{\eta,n}=\mathfrak m_{\eta,n}/
  (\mathfrak m_{\eta,n}^2+\mathfrak m_{A_\eta}R_{\eta,n}),
 \qquad
 Q_\eta=\mathfrak m_\eta/
  (\mathfrak m_\eta^2+\mathfrak m_{A_\eta}R_\eta).$$ The first maps onto the second. Its kernel is the image of the actual ideal $I_{\eta,n}$: if a representative maps into $\mathfrak m_\eta^2+\mathfrak m_{A_\eta}R_\eta$, lift a decomposition of its image into $\mathfrak m_{\eta,n}^2+\mathfrak m_{A_\eta}R_{\eta,n}$ and subtract it. This leaves a representative in $I_{\eta,n}$ of the same relative cotangent class.

Choose a basis of the old space $Q_\eta$ with fixed representatives $r_{\eta,1},\ldots,r_{\eta,q}$ in $R_\eta$. Choose their reductions first in the common special-fiber diagram and then lift to both values of $\eta$. Their relative cotangent spaces agree modulo $\varpi$, so the number $q$ is the same. For each $n$, lift these fixed old representatives to $R_{\eta,n}$ and complete their independent cotangent classes to a basis using representatives in $I_{\eta,n}$. The tangent bound just proved shows that at most $g=2h+1$ elements are needed; pad the list with zeros to length $g$.

These choices can have identical reductions for $\eta=1,i$. First choose the lifts and the kernel-complement elements in the common special-fiber diagram. Lift an element arbitrarily to $R_{\eta,n}$. If its image in $R_\eta$ differs from the required fixed old value by $\varpi a$, subtract $\varpi$ times any preimage of $a$ in $R_{\eta,n}$. This preserves the chosen special-fiber reduction and makes the old image exact. For a kernel-complement element the required old image is zero. No flatness of the old global ring over the coefficient DVR is used.

Complete relative Nakayama now gives <a href="#eq:patching-presentation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:patching-presentation">[eq:patching-presentation]</a>. Every composite to $R_\eta$ sends the first $q$ variables to the fixed $r_{\eta,j}$ and all remaining variables to zero, independently of $n$. The two presentations still agree modulo $\varpi$. The variable count, and hence every later ambient dimension, is unchanged. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-twist-rank"></a>
<div id="lem:twist-rank" class="lemma">

**Lemma 24** (Rank of auxiliary character twists). *Increase $h$ so that $b=h-2d+2>0$. There are quotients $$A_n=(\mathbb Z/2^{a_n}\mathbb Z)^b,\qquad a_n\longrightarrow\infty,$$ of the ray class groups defining characters trivial at every dyadic and real place and unramified outside $Q_n$. At $Q_n$ they use only the residue-unit quotients of order $2^n$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-twist-rank"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-twist-rank</summary>

*Proof.* Let $S_2$ be the dyadic places. Take the ordinary ray class group of modulus $\prod_{q\in Q_n}q$, and quotient by the dyadic ideal classes. Omitting real places from the modulus requires them to split; at the dyadic places, both the units and uniformizers are trivial in this quotient. The ray class exact sequence is $$\mathcal O_{F,S_2}^{\times}\longrightarrow
 \prod_{q\in Q_n}k(q)^\times\longrightarrow
 \Gamma_n\longrightarrow\operatorname{Cl}_{S_2}(F)
 \longrightarrow0 .$$ This is the usual ideal-theoretic ray sequence, with the primes in $S_2$ inverted (Milne 2020b, V, Theorem 1.7) <a id="pm-cite-85"></a>[[MilneCFT](#ref-MilneCFT)]. The group of dyadic $S$-units has free rank $$(d-1)+d=2d-1.$$ Here we use the $S$-unit theorem (Milne 2020a, Theorem 5.11) <a id="pm-cite-86"></a>[[MilneANT](#ref-MilneANT)]. The direction generated by $2$ is primitive in its free part: its valuation at each dyadic prime is one, since $2$ splits completely in $F$. Since $F$ is totally real, generators for these units may therefore be chosen as $-1,2$ and $2d-2$ further units. For $n\geq2$, put $a_n=n-1$ and take the cyclic residue-unit quotient of order $2^{a_n}$ at each auxiliary prime. Both $-1$ and $2$ vanish in these quotients: the first is a $2^{a_n}$th power because $\mu_{2^{a_n+1}}$ is present, and the second is such a power by the imposed Kummer splitting. The elementary divisor theorem shows that quotienting $(\mathbb Z/2^{a_n}\mathbb Z)^h$ by the images of the remaining $2d-2$ generators leaves a quotient $(\mathbb Z/2^{a_n}\mathbb Z)^{h-2d+2}$. The image of the local units in $\Gamma_n$ thus has such a quotient. A finite abelian group containing a subgroup with this quotient also has this quotient: in invariant-factor form, the subgroup forces at least this many factors of order at least $2^{a_n}$ in the containing group. Apply this to $\Gamma_n$. Class field theory turns its quotient into the required characters (Milne 2020b, V, Theorems 5.3 and 5.5) <a id="pm-cite-87"></a>[[MilneCFT](#ref-MilneCFT)]. Discarding the first finitely many indices is harmless. ◻

</details>

The resulting $b=h-2d+2$ character parameters will supply the support dimensions missing from the fixed-determinant estimate. Their vanishing at the old local conditions is what permits twisting without changing those conditions. Let $H_n$ denote the formal character group of $A_n$, completed at the trivial character and $\varpi$. Its coordinate ring is $$\mathcal L[[l_1,\ldots,l_b]]/
 \bigl((1+l_j)^{2^{a_n}}-1:1\leq j\leq b\bigr).$$ Its tautological Galois character will be denoted $\theta_n$. Twisting a fixed-determinant representation by $\theta_n$ preserves the prescribed determinants at the dyadic and real places and on bad inertia. It therefore defines a map from the variable global problem to the fixed global problem with these character parameters.

<a id="eq:patching-ambient-dimension"></a>
The local product $R_{\mathrm{loc},\eta}$ in <a href="#eq:patching-presentation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:patching-presentation">[eq:patching-presentation]</a> is obtained from the enhanced real–dyadic base $A^{\mathrm{rd}}$ of Proposition <a href="#prop:local-geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:local-geometry">13</a> by adjoining the $u$ raw tame factors of <a href="#eq:tame-model" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tame-model">[eq:tame-model]</a>, with $\det\phi_j$ unrestricted. Adjoining the $g=2h+1$ presentation variables gives the rings $B_\eta$ of that proposition. They therefore have dimension $$\label{eq:patching-ambient-dimension}
 e=1+8d+4u+g=8d+4u+2h+2.$$ Their special fibers are identified. The underlying spectrum of $B_i$ is irreducible and nonvertical. All components of $B_1$ are horizontal of dimension $e$, and correspond bijectively to the top-dimensional components of its special fiber, of dimension $e-1$. The generic point of each such special-fiber component lies on just its corresponding component of $B_1$. These precise component assertions, and not only the dimension in <a href="#eq:patching-ambient-dimension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:patching-ambient-dimension">[eq:patching-ambient-dimension]</a>, will be used below.

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### The modules and their finite-level actions

Let $N_{\eta,n}$ be the block module of completed forms at the auxiliary ratio level $U_1(Q_n)$, with tame type $(\eta,\eta^{-1})$ at the bad places. Add $z$, make the enhanced completion, and pull back to the independent $S$-frame space. Define $N_\eta$ in the same way at old level. The modules are finite over $R_{\eta,n}^f$ and $R_\eta^f$, respectively, by Propositions <a href="#prop:completed-forms" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:completed-forms">16</a> and <a href="#prop:block-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:block-comparison">17</a>. We also regard them as modules for the variable-determinant rings through their fixed-determinant quotients.

<a id="eq:auxiliary-flatness"></a>
<a id="eq:old-level-action"></a>
<a id="eq:old-residue-dimension"></a>
Choose the quotient of the diamond group $\Delta_n=(\mathbb Z/2^n\mathbb Z)^h$, with generators $U_1,\ldots,U_h$, and write $V_j=U_j-1$. Lemma <a href="#lem:auxiliary-level" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:auxiliary-level">20</a> gives $$\begin{aligned}
 &N_{\eta,n}\text{ is flat over }\mathcal L[\Delta_n],
                                                        \label{eq:auxiliary-flatness}\\
 &N_{\eta,n}/(V_1,\ldots,V_h)N_{\eta,n}
       \simeq N_\eta^{\oplus 2^h},                       \label{eq:old-level-action}\\
 &\overline N:=N_1/\varpi N_1\simeq N_i/\varpi N_i\ne0,
 \qquad \dim\mathop{\mathrm{Supp}}\overline N\geq 2d+4s-1.             \label{eq:old-residue-dimension}
\end{aligned}$$ The isomorphism <a href="#eq:old-level-action" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:old-level-action">[eq:old-level-action]</a> is an isomorphism of $R_{\eta,n}$-modules through $R_{\eta,n}\twoheadrightarrow R_\eta$. In particular, it identifies the actual Galois-ring actions. The $n$-level modules and their ring and diamond actions for the two values of $\eta$ likewise agree modulo $\varpi$.

We recall why the operations just performed preserve the assertions. Flatness over $\mathcal O[\Delta_n]$ holds in the definite function-space model and passes through the exact block functor.
