The $z$-adic flatness criterion applies after adjoining $z$, and base change from $\mathcal O[[z]]$ to $\mathcal L$ preserves flatness. The subsequent localizations, noetherian completions, and smooth frame extensions are flat maps of the commuting deformation rings. Tensoring a finite-ideal flatness test over $\mathcal L[\Delta_n]$ with these flat extensions preserves injectivity, proving <a href="#eq:auxiliary-flatness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:auxiliary-flatness">[eq:auxiliary-flatness]</a> after all the stated operations. The degeneracy maps giving <a href="#eq:old-level-action" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:old-level-action">[eq:old-level-action]</a> are Hecke equivariant. At the regular Frobenius curve their composite is invertible, and the special contribution is absent. The augmented Galois action is consequently unramified at $Q_n$ and factors through the old quotient; the classical comparison identifies it with the old action. Finally, Proposition <a href="#prop:hecke-dimension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:hecke-dimension">19</a> gives dimension at least $1+2d$ on each Hecke component. Enhanced completion preserves this dimension, independent $S$-frames add $4s-1$, and reduction by $\varpi$ loses at most one. This proves the bound in <a href="#eq:old-residue-dimension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:old-residue-dimension">[eq:old-residue-dimension]</a>. Nonvanishing follows from the pro-modular curve and the faithfulness of the old block module.

<a id="eq:diamond-quadratic"></a>
For chosen tame inertia elements $\tau_{j,n}$ at the auxiliary primes, the commuting diamond actions also satisfy $$\label{eq:diamond-quadratic}
 U_j^2-T(\tau_{j,n})U_j+1=0.$$ Here $T$ denotes the trace of the Galois deformation acting on the module. This is an identity of operators, as supplied by Lemma <a href="#lem:auxiliary-level" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:auxiliary-level">20</a>.

We will need actual twist isomorphisms, rather than only a numerical invariance of support. For every sign tuple $\sigma\in\{1,-1\}^b$, specialize $\theta_n$ to the quadratic character $\theta_{\sigma,n}$. Multiplication of forms by $\theta_{\sigma,n}\circ\operatorname{Nrd}$ gives a semilinear isomorphism of $N_{\eta,n}$ for the corresponding automorphism of $R_{\eta,n}^f$. The central character is unchanged because this character is quadratic. It is trivial above $2$ and at infinity and unramified on bad inertia. It preserves the ratio level: on that level the two diagonal character values agree, so their product is one. Its reduction is one, hence the enhanced curve and its residual frames are fixed. These observations prove that the isomorphisms act on precisely the completed modules under consideration.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### Exact patching with finite jets

Fix a nonprincipal ultrafilter $\mathfrak U$ on the positive integers. For a fixed complete noetherian local ring $(B,\mathfrak m)$, define $$B^*=\varprojlim_j\prod_{\mathfrak U} B/\mathfrak m^j.$$ Its residue field is the ultrapower $K^*$ of the residue field $K$. This deliberate auxiliary scalar extension is separate from the finite coefficient extensions used to define the original curves and representations. For a fixed finite $B$-module $M$, use the same definition with $M/\mathfrak m^jM$. For varying quotients of $B$ and varying finite modules, a subscript $\infty$ will denote this construction using the maximal-ideal filtration induced by $B$.

<a id="stmt-lem-jet-exactness"></a>
<div id="lem:jet-exactness" class="lemma">

**Lemma 25** (Finite jets and uniform exactness). *The following assertions hold.*

1.  *The ring $B^*$ is complete noetherian local, and $B\to B^*$ is faithfully flat. For fixed finite modules the construction is exact and equals extension of scalars. It preserves their support dimensions. If $B$ is a domain, so is $B^*$.*

2.  *For varying quotients of $B$ and modules generated by a bounded number of elements, the patched objects are finite over $B^*$. Surjections and cokernels of a fixed finite number of compatible operators commute with patching.*

<a id="eq:uniform-artin-rees"></a>
3.  *Let $(D,\mathfrak a)$ be a fixed noetherian local ring and $C$ a fixed finite $D$-module. For arbitrary finite-module extensions $$0\longrightarrow P_n\longrightarrow H_n\longrightarrow C
     \longrightarrow0$$ there is a constant $c$, independent of the extension, such that $$\label{eq:uniform-artin-rees}
     P_n\cap\mathfrak a^jH_n\subseteq\mathfrak a^{j-c}P_n
     \quad(j\geq c).$$ Thus a filtration of fixed length with fixed successive quotients patches exactly whenever its ambient jet filtration is uniformly cofinal with the $\mathfrak a$-adic filtrations on its pieces.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-jet-exactness"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-jet-exactness</summary>

*Proof.* At each depth, taking an ultrapower extends the scalars in each finite-dimensional associated graded piece from $K$ to $K^*$. Thus the graded ring of $B^*$ is generated by the images of any fixed finite set of generators of $\mathfrak m$. Successive expansion in these generators, followed by completeness, identifies the jet filtration with powers of the ideal they generate. The complete filtered-ring argument then proves noetherianity: initial ideals are finitely generated in the noetherian associated graded ring, and successive approximation lifts those generators.

For an exact sequence of fixed finite modules, Artin–Rees makes the induced and intrinsic filtrations on its submodule cofinal. Use the induced filtration to obtain exact sequences of jets; cofinality identifies their inverse limits with the construction using intrinsic filtrations. The resulting limit is exact. A fixed finite presentation identifies $M^*$ with $B^*\otimes_B M$. This gives flatness by the finite-ideal criterion, and the nonzero residue extension gives faithful flatness. Lengths of the graded pieces are unchanged after residue-field extension, so their Hilbert growth, and hence the support dimension, is unchanged.

Suppose next that $B$ is a domain and is not a field. Choose a discrete valuation $v$ of its fraction field centered at $\mathfrak m$, for example one supplied by the normalized blowup of $\mathfrak m$. It is positive on $\mathfrak m$. The ideals $$I_a=\{x\in B:v(x)\geq a\}$$ have zero intersection. Chevalley’s lemma for a complete local ring implies that for every $q$ there is an $a(q)$ with $I_{a(q)}\subseteq\mathfrak m^q$. Thus being nonzero modulo a fixed $\mathfrak m^q$ bounds $v(x)$ from above, uniformly in $x$. If $c_0>0$ is the minimum valuation of fixed generators of $\mathfrak m$, then $v(\mathfrak m^r)\geq rc_0$. For two fixed nonzero jets, these inequalities give a depth at which the product of any representatives of the two jets is nonzero. Applied on a set belonging to $\mathfrak U$, they show that the product of two nonzero elements of $B^*$ cannot vanish. The assertion is immediate when $B$ is a field.

For the varying construction, choose a uniform number of generators at each level. Each jet ultraproduct is then a quotient of a fixed finite free module over the corresponding jet of $B^*$ and has finite length. Images of kernels at any fixed depth form a descending chain in a finite-length module, hence stabilize. The Mittag–Leffler argument gives surjectivity on inverse limits and a finite generating set over $B^*$. The same argument applied to a right-exact sequence proves the cokernel assertion. This does not assert exactness for arbitrary varying submodules.

For the uniform assertion, choose one finite free presentation $F\twoheadrightarrow C$, with kernel $L$, and lift it to $f_n:F\to H_n$. Then $H_n=P_n+f_n(F)$. If $x\in P_n\cap\mathfrak a^jH_n$, write $$x=p+f_n(y),\qquad p\in\mathfrak a^jP_n,
 \quad y\in\mathfrak a^jF.$$ Since $x$ maps to zero in $C$, we have $y\in L$. Artin–Rees for the one fixed inclusion $L\subset F$ gives $y\in\mathfrak a^{j-c}L$, with $c$ independent of $n$. As $f_n(L)\subset P_n$, this proves <a href="#eq:uniform-artin-rees" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:uniform-artin-rees">[eq:uniform-artin-rees]</a>. Applying this successively to the fixed quotients of a finite filtration gives uniform strictness for each inclusion. Uniform cofinality transfers these bounds to the ambient jets, proving the final statement. ◻

</details>

We now use the jet lemma in three specific ways: scalar extension of fixed modules, bounded-generation patching of the auxiliary modules, and uniform strictness of filtrations with fixed old quotients. It is the fixed quotient in the third assertion, not arbitrary varying-module exactness, that preserves the regular sequence below.

The domain assertion in Lemma <a href="#lem:jet-exactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:jet-exactness">25</a> can be applied to each quotient by a minimal prime. Exactness and nilpotence of the nilradical then show that a fixed finite list of irreducible components is preserved by starring. The same applies to the special fiber and to inclusions between its components and the ambient components. In particular all the geometric assertions following <a href="#eq:patching-ambient-dimension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:patching-ambient-dimension">[eq:patching-ambient-dimension]</a> remain valid for $B_\eta^*$. This observation does not require $B_i$ itself to be reduced.

Apply the construction to the surjections $B_\eta\twoheadrightarrow R_{\eta,n}$ and their fixed-determinant quotients. The module generator numbers are uniformly bounded. Indeed modulo the maximal ideal of $R_{\eta,n}$, <a href="#eq:diamond-quadratic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:diamond-quadratic">[eq:diamond-quadratic]</a> gives $V_j^2=0$. The quotient by all the $V_j$ is the fixed old module in <a href="#eq:old-level-action" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:old-level-action">[eq:old-level-action]</a>. The monomials $V_1^{e_1}\cdots V_h^{e_h}$ with $0\leq e_j\leq1$ therefore give a uniform bound, and Nakayama supplies the required generators. We obtain finite $N_{\eta,\infty}$ and surjections $$B_\eta^*\twoheadrightarrow R_{\eta,\infty}
 \twoheadrightarrow R_{\eta,\infty}^f
 \twoheadrightarrow(R_\eta^f)^*.$$ The commuting $U_j$ patch as well. They satisfy the patched versions of <a href="#eq:diamond-quadratic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:diamond-quadratic">[eq:diamond-quadratic]</a>; the joint action algebra is consequently module finite over $R_{\eta,\infty}$. Modulo its original maximal ideal all the $V_j$ are nilpotent, so adjoining their actions does not change the complete separated module topology.

<a id="stmt-prop-patched-regular-sequence"></a>
<div id="prop:patched-regular-sequence" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:patched-dimension"></a>
<a id="eq:patched-augmentation"></a>
**Proposition 26** (The patched regular sequence). *On $N_{\eta,\infty}$ the sequence $\varpi,V_1,\ldots,V_h$ is regular. Moreover $$\begin{aligned}
 \dim\mathop{\mathrm{Supp}}_{R_{\eta,\infty}}N_{\eta,\infty}
       &\geq2d+4s+h,                                  \label{eq:patched-dimension}\\
 N_{\eta,\infty}/(V_1,\ldots,V_h)N_{\eta,\infty}
       &\simeq (N_\eta^{\oplus2^h})^*.                \label{eq:patched-augmentation}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-patched-regular-sequence"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-patched-regular-sequence</summary>

*Proof.* *Polynomial layers with a fixed old action.* Fix $\eta$, and write $\mathfrak m$ for the maximal ideal of $B_\eta$. The presentations of Lemma <a href="#lem:auxiliary-primes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:auxiliary-primes">23</a> give surjections $B_\eta\to R_{\eta,n}$ whose composites to $R_\eta$ are independent of $n$. Regard $$C=\overline N^{\oplus2^h}$$ as a fixed finite $B_\eta$-module through this common composite. Let $J=(\varpi,V_1,\ldots,V_h)$ denote the ideal of commuting operators on the auxiliary modules and on their patched limit.

<a id="eq:finite-polynomial-layers"></a>
Fix a truncation length $r$. For all sufficiently large $n$, the relations $(1+V_j)^{2^n}-1$ have $J$-order greater than $r$. Flatness in <a href="#eq:auxiliary-flatness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:auxiliary-flatness">[eq:auxiliary-flatness]</a>, together with the actual old-action identification <a href="#eq:old-level-action" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:old-level-action">[eq:old-level-action]</a>, gives multiplication isomorphisms $$\label{eq:finite-polynomial-layers}
 \operatorname{gr}_J^{<r} N_{\eta,n}
   \simeq C\otimes_K K[T_0,T_1,\ldots,T_h]_{<r},$$ where $T_0,T_j$ represent $\varpi,V_j$. These are isomorphisms of $B_\eta$-modules. Indeed the Galois action commutes with the $J$-operators, so its action on each layer is obtained from its action on $N_{\eta,n}/JN_{\eta,n}=C$. The fixed old composite therefore makes every degree a fixed finite direct sum of $C$. Only the extensions joining these layers vary with $n$.

*Exactness at each fixed truncation length.* Put $H_n=N_{\eta,n}/J^rN_{\eta,n}$ and $F_j=J^jH_n$ for $0\leq j\leq r$. All these modules are finite over $B_\eta$. Their successive quotients are the fixed modules in <a href="#eq:finite-polynomial-layers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-polynomial-layers">[eq:finite-polynomial-layers]</a>, so their numbers of generators are bounded independently of $n$ for this fixed $r$. If $\mathfrak q_n$ is the maximal ideal of $R_{\eta,n}$, the surjection from $B_\eta$ gives $$\mathfrak m R_{\eta,n}=\mathfrak q_n.$$ Thus the $\mathfrak m$-adic filtrations are precisely the maximal-ideal filtrations used for patching these modules.

Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:jet-exactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:jet-exactness">25</a>(3) over the one fixed ring $B_\eta$ to $$0\longrightarrow F_{j+1}\longrightarrow F_j
 \longrightarrow F_j/F_{j+1}\longrightarrow0.$$ Its fixed-quotient Artin–Rees bound makes each inclusion uniformly strict. Composing the finitely many bounds compares the induced filtration from $H_n$ with the intrinsic filtration on every $F_j$. The constants may depend on $r$, but are independent of $n$. Consequently this finite filtration patches exactly. No exactness for arbitrary varying submodules is required.

<a id="eq:patched-polynomial-layers"></a>
The ideal $J^r$ is generated by a fixed finite number of monomials in its commuting generators. The cokernel assertion of Lemma <a href="#lem:jet-exactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:jet-exactness">25</a>(2) therefore identifies the patched $H_n$ with $N_{\eta,\infty}/J^rN_{\eta,\infty}$; the same argument identifies its quotients by $J^j$. These identifications respect the multiplication maps and are compatible as $r$ varies. We obtain $$\label{eq:patched-polynomial-layers}
 \operatorname{gr}_J N_{\eta,\infty}
       \simeq C^*\otimes_{K^*}K^*[T_0,T_1,\ldots,T_h].$$ Here $C^*$ is the scalar extension of the fixed $B_\eta$-module $C$ from Lemma <a href="#lem:jet-exactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:jet-exactness">25</a>(1).

*Regularity, dimension, and augmentation.* The finite joint action algebra generated by the $U_j$ is complete local: modulo the deformation-ring maximal ideal, every $V_j$ is nilpotent. Its module topology agrees with the one used for patching. Since $J$ lies in its maximal ideal, the finite module $N_{\eta,\infty}$ is $J$-adically separated and complete. A nonzero element killed by $\varpi$ would therefore have a first nonzero $J$-initial form, contradicting injectivity of $T_0$ in <a href="#eq:patched-polynomial-layers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:patched-polynomial-layers">[eq:patched-polynomial-layers]</a>. The same initial-form argument gives $$\varpi N_{\eta,\infty}\cap J^mN_{\eta,\infty}
       =\varpi J^{m-1}N_{\eta,\infty}\qquad(m\geq1).$$ Hence the associated graded module after quotienting by $\varpi$ is obtained by setting $T_0=0$. Repeating the argument for $V_1,\ldots,V_h$ proves that the stated sequence is regular.

The final quotient by this regular sequence is $C^*$. Its dimension is at least $2d+4s-1$ by <a href="#eq:old-residue-dimension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:old-residue-dimension">[eq:old-residue-dimension]</a> and Lemma <a href="#lem:jet-exactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:jet-exactness">25</a>(1). Each member of the regular sequence raises support dimension by one on passing back to the original module. Dimensions over the finite joint action algebra and over $R_{\eta,\infty}$ agree, giving <a href="#eq:patched-dimension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:patched-dimension">[eq:patched-dimension]</a>. Finally, apply cokernel patching to the diamond augmentation <a href="#eq:old-level-action" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:old-level-action">[eq:old-level-action]</a>, without reducing by $\varpi$. The old $B_\eta$-action is fixed by the same common composite, so this gives <a href="#eq:patched-augmentation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:patched-augmentation">[eq:patched-augmentation]</a> with that actual old deformation action. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### Twisting and full support

<a id="stmt-prop-patched-support"></a>
<div id="prop:patched-support" class="proposition">

**Proposition 27** (Full support after patching). *The untwisted patched module satisfies $$\mathop{\mathrm{Supp}}_{R_{1,\infty}}N_{1,\infty}
       \supseteq\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}R_{1,\infty}^f.$$ Consequently the old module has full support: $\mathop{\mathrm{Supp}}_{R_1^f}N_1=\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}R_1^f$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-patched-support"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-patched-support</summary>

<a id="eq:formal-twisting-map"></a>
*Proof.* *Finite twisting and its dimension contribution.* The finite characters of Lemma <a href="#lem:twist-rank" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:twist-rank">24</a> give a local ring map after patching, $$\label{eq:formal-twisting-map}
 \Theta_\eta:R_{\eta,\infty}\longrightarrow
          R_{\eta,\infty}^f[[l_1,\ldots,l_b]].$$ Here one takes bounded total jets in the ring and character variables. At each such depth the equations $(1+l_j)^{2^{a_n}}-1$ disappear for large $n$. Coefficientwise patching of the finitely many monomials at each depth identifies the target with the displayed power-series ring. This explains both existence of the map and its target.

The map <a href="#eq:formal-twisting-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:formal-twisting-map">[eq:formal-twisting-map]</a> is finite. Since $A_n$ is a character-group quotient, choose, for each $n$ and each $j$, a Galois element detecting exactly its $j$th coordinate. Its determinant divided by $\chi$ maps under twisting to $(1+l_j)^2$. Although these Galois elements may vary with $n$, their determinant functions determine elements in every patched jet, and hence in $R_{\eta,\infty}$. The resulting elements have residue one. On the closed fiber of <a href="#eq:formal-twisting-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:formal-twisting-map">[eq:formal-twisting-map]</a> they impose $l_j^2=0$. At $l_1=\cdots=l_b=0$ the map is the fixed-determinant surjection. Its closed fiber is therefore an Artinian local algebra, of length at most $2^b$, with the same residue field. Complete Nakayama proves finiteness.

Let $T_\eta$ denote $N_{\eta,\infty}[[l_1,\ldots,l_b]]$, restricted along <a href="#eq:formal-twisting-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:formal-twisting-map">[eq:formal-twisting-map]</a> and then regarded as a finite $B_\eta^*$-module. Its support is the closed image of the power-series-extended support. Finiteness of the map preserves its dimension, and <a href="#eq:patched-dimension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:patched-dimension">[eq:patched-dimension]</a> gives $$\dim\mathop{\mathrm{Supp}}_{B_\eta^*}T_\eta
 \geq 2d+4s+h+b
 =8d+4u+2h+2=e.$$ Furthermore $\varpi$ is regular on $T_\eta$, since it is regular before power-series extension and restriction of scalars. No minimal support prime of $T_\eta$ is vertical.

*Full support on the $i$-model and transfer to the $1$-model.* The dimension estimate now reaches the entire ambient dimension after twisting. The spectrum of $B_i^*$ is irreducible of dimension $e$. It follows that $T_i$ has full support. On reducing modulo $\varpi$, the finite-stage modules, actions, and twisting maps for $\eta=1,i$ are identical. Reduction is a cokernel, and power-series extension commutes with it and with the jets. Thus $T_1/\varpi T_1$ and $T_i/\varpi T_i$ are the same module over the identified special fibers. For any finite module $T$, local Nakayama gives $$\mathop{\mathrm{Supp}}(T/\varpi T)=\mathop{\mathrm{Supp}}(T)\cap V(\varpi).$$ It follows that $\mathop{\mathrm{Supp}}T_1$ contains the generic point of every top-dimensional special-fiber component of $B_1^*$.

We give the component argument explicitly. Let $\mathfrak q$ be one of these generic points, and let $\mathfrak p$ be the unique minimal prime of $B_1^*$ below it. The local geometry gives $\dim B_1^*/\mathfrak p=e$ and $\dim B_1^*/\mathfrak q=e-1$; equivalently $\mathfrak q/\mathfrak p$ has height one. The latter also follows from the principal ideal theorem, since it is a minimal prime of the divisor $\varpi$ on this component. Choose a minimal support prime $\mathfrak a\subseteq\mathfrak q$ of $T_1$. The uniqueness assertion forces $\mathfrak p\subseteq\mathfrak a$. The prime $\mathfrak a$ is horizontal, so it is not $\mathfrak q$; there is no prime strictly between $\mathfrak p$ and $\mathfrak q$. Thus $\mathfrak a=\mathfrak p$. Every ambient component is obtained this way, and $T_1$ has full support.

*Removing twists by actual sign intertwiners.* It remains to remove the formal twists. For each fixed sign tuple $\sigma\in\{1,-1\}^b$, the finite-level quadratic twists described above give local ring automorphisms and semilinear module isomorphisms. They preserve maximal-ideal filtrations, and therefore patch, together with their inverses. In particular the support of $N_{1,\infty}$ is stable under the resulting sign automorphisms of $R_{1,\infty}^f$. Evaluation of <a href="#eq:formal-twisting-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:formal-twisting-map">[eq:formal-twisting-map]</a> at $l_j=\sigma_j-1$ is the fixed-determinant quotient followed by this patched sign twist, by its finite-level construction. The substitution is well-defined on formal series, since its values are $0$ or $-2$.

Let $x\in\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}R_{1,\infty}^f$. Full support of $T_1$ supplies a point $y$ in the support of $N_{1,\infty}[[l_1,\ldots,l_b]]$ mapping to $x$. The determinant calculation, since $x$ has fixed determinant, gives in the residue field $\kappa(y)$ $$(1+l_j)^2=1,\qquad\text{or equivalently}\qquad l_j(l_j+2)=0.$$ For every $j$, primality therefore chooses $l_j=0$ or $-2$. Choose the corresponding sign tuple. Quotienting by these sign equations leaves a nonzero localized module, by Nakayama, and identifies the quotient with $N_{1,\infty}$ at a point $x'$ of its support. Its image $x$ is the sign transform of $x'$. Sign stability puts $x$ in the untwisted support. In characteristic two the two signs coincide, with the same conclusion. This proves the first assertion.

*Diamond augmentation and the old action.* Finally consider the old quotient $(R_1^f)^*$. Let $\mathfrak p$ be any of its primes, viewed in $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}R_{1,\infty}$. It belongs to the support just proved. In the finite joint action algebra generated by the $U_j$, there is a support prime $\mathfrak P$ above $\mathfrak p$. On the old quotient each $\tau_{j,n}$ is trivial, so <a href="#eq:diamond-quadratic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:diamond-quadratic">[eq:diamond-quadratic]</a> becomes $$U_j^2-2U_j+1=V_j^2=0.$$ Hence $V_j\in\mathfrak P$ for all $j$. The nonzero finite module localized at $\mathfrak P$ remains nonzero after quotienting by all the $V_j$, by Nakayama. Equation <a href="#eq:patched-augmentation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:patched-augmentation">[eq:patched-augmentation]</a> shows that $(N_1^{\oplus2^h})^*$ has support at every prime of $(R_1^f)^*$. Faithful flatness in Lemma <a href="#lem:jet-exactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:jet-exactness">25</a> now gives $\mathop{\mathrm{Supp}}_{R_1^f}N_1=\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}R_1^f$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-patched-support-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-patched-support</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:localized-propagation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:localized-propagation">22</a>.* We have proved full support on the enhanced completed global deformation germ with independent $S$-frames, global determinant $\chi$, and unipotent bad inertia. After the initial base changes, the source locus has exactly these local conditions. At its absolutely irreducible curve, the representation and pseudo-deformation problems agree after a smooth choice of frames. Adding $z$, passing to a finite curve-field extension, and taking this completed localization are faithfully flat on the indicated germ, by Lemma <a href="#lem:curve-completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:curve-completion">7</a>. Consequently the closed Hecke support contains that source germ.

To pass from the germ to the asserted irreducible closed locus, work on its reduced irreducible coordinate ring. The image of any equation of the Hecke support is nilpotent in the completed source germ, by the set-theoretic containment just proved. Faithful flatness descends this nilpotence to the localization at the enhanced curve. That localization is reduced, so the image is zero there. It therefore vanishes on the entire irreducible locus: localization of a domain is injective. Thus the locus is pro-modular over the field reached by the allowed solvable base changes. This is precisely potential pro-modularity and proves the theorem. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:fields"></a>
### Fields with controlled Selmer groups

The connectedness argument needs large totally real fields, very few allowed non-dyadic primes, and a strict Selmer bound at a small proportion of the dyadic places. We construct these fields here. The original allowed set, including the primes needed for the modular seed, is fixed before this construction.

Let $\Sigma$ be a finite set of odd rational primes, and let $\bar r$ be a semisimple representation of $G_{\mathbb Q}$ over a sufficiently large finite field $k$ of characteristic two, unramified outside $\Sigma\cup\{2\}$. If $\bar r$ is reducible, write its two characters as $\bar\chi_1,\bar\chi_2$ and put $\alpha=\bar\chi_1/\bar\chi_2$. Thus $\alpha$ has odd order, is even, and is unramified at $2$. The last assertion follows from $\mathbb Z_2^\times$ being a pro-$2$ group. Write $\alpha_2$ for its restriction to $G_{\mathbb Q_2}$. For a number field $E$ and a finite set $S_E$ containing the dyadic and real places, all Selmer groups below are subspaces of $H^1(G_{E,S_E},k(\beta))$; at places outside $S_E$ their local condition is unramified.

<a id="stmt-prop-field-preparation"></a>
<div id="prop:field-preparation" class="proposition">

**Proposition 28** (Field preparation). *There is a constant $U$, depending only on the fixed residual data and $\Sigma$, with the following property. One can construct abelian, totally real $2$-extensions $$\mathbb Q\subset A\subset F$$ in which $2$ splits completely, together with allowed sets $S_A,S_F$, such that the following assertions hold.*

1.  *The allowed sets contain the real and dyadic places and all places above $\Sigma$. Their numbers $u_A,u_F$ of non-dyadic finite places are at most $U$. The degrees $d_A=[A:\mathbb Q]$ and $[F:A]$ can be made arbitrarily large, with $d=[F:\mathbb Q]=[F:A]d_A$.*

2.  *If $\bar r$ is absolutely irreducible, its restriction to $G_F$ is absolutely irreducible. In this case there are no marked places.*

3.  *In the reducible case, the characters remain distinct on $G_F$ if they were distinct on $G_{\mathbb Q}$. If $\alpha_2\ne1$, there are again no marked places. If $\alpha_2=1$, there is a dyadic place $v_0$ of $A$ and the marked set $M$ consists of all its extensions to $F$. Set $m=|M|$; thus $m=[F:A]$ and $m/d=1/d_A$ in the marked case, whereas $m=0$ otherwise.*

<a id="eq:dual-vanishing"></a>
4.  *In the reducible case, for $\beta=\alpha,\alpha^{-1}$ the following group is zero: $$\label{eq:dual-vanishing}
     \left\{c\in H^1(G_{F,S_F},k(\beta)):
           \operatorname{loc}_v(c)=0\text{ for every }v\in S_F\setminus M
     \right\}=0.$$ Here the zero conditions include all real places. The condition at a marked place is unrestricted.*

5.  *In the reducible case, restriction preserves residual extension classes: for either ratio $\beta$, the map $$H^1(G_{A,S_A},k(\beta))\longrightarrow H^1(G_{F,S_F},k(\beta))$$ is injective.*

<a id="eq:strict-selmer"></a>
*In the reducible case let $H^1_{\mathrm{str}}(F,k(\beta))$ denote the Selmer group with zero condition at $M$, full condition at $S_F\setminus M$ (including the real places), and unramified condition elsewhere. Then $$\label{eq:strict-selmer}
 \dim_k H^1_{\mathrm{str}}(F,k(\beta))\le
 \begin{cases}
 d+u_F,&\alpha_2\ne1,\\
 2d-3m+u_F,&\alpha_2=1.
 \end{cases}$$*

</div>

The field construction has three cases. For absolutely irreducible residue, a cyclic extension disjoint from the residual splitting field will suffice. For scalar residue, a narrow-class-group argument will give the required vanishing. For distinct residual characters, we will choose conductor primes successively so that changing the local Selmer conditions removes classes without introducing new ones. In each case we must bound the number of allowed primes independently of the degrees of the fields; this is what permits the strict dimension inequalities used in Section <a href="#sec:connectedness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:connectedness">7</a>.

We use class field theory with real places in the modulus (Milne 2020b, chap. V, Theorems 1.7, 3.5, and 3.6) <a id="pm-cite-88"></a>[[MilneCFT](#ref-MilneCFT)], the Kummer identification, and inflation–restriction. Local and global duality give the following Selmer dimension formula for a finite coefficient module $V$ and linear local conditions $\mathcal L$: <a id="eq:field-selmer-formula"></a>$$\label{eq:field-selmer-formula}
 \begin{aligned}
 \dim H^1_{\mathcal L}(V)-\dim H^1_{\mathcal L^\perp}(V^\vee(1))
 &=h^0(V)-h^0(V^\vee(1))\\
 &\quad+\sum_v\bigl(\dim\mathcal L_v-h^0_v(V)\bigr).
 \end{aligned}$$ This is obtained by taking dimensions in Poitou–Tate and using the global Euler formula (Milne 2006, chap. I, Theorems 4.10 and 5.1, Remark 5.2(a)) <a id="pm-cite-89"></a>[[Milne2006](#ref-Milne2006)]. The real places are retained; their $h^0_v$ in this formula is ordinary invariant dimension. Our coefficient characters are trivial at real places, so local $H^1$ there has dimension one.

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### Cyclic fields and a scalar genus argument

<a id="eq:conductor-power"></a>
Fix integers $n>a\ge1$. If an odd prime $r$ satisfies $$\label{eq:conductor-power}
 r\equiv1\pmod{2^{n+1}},\qquad
               2\in(\mathbb F_r^\times)^{2^n},$$ the degree-$2^n$ cyclic subfield $L(r,n)$ of $\mathbb Q(\zeta_r)$ is real and has $2$ split completely. It is totally ramified at $r$ and unramified at other finite primes. For an odd prime $\ell\ne r$, the Frobenius of $\ell$ generates its Galois group precisely when $\ell$ is a nonsquare modulo $r$. Indeed, both assertions are read in the cyclic quotient of $\mathbb F_r^\times$ of order $2^n$; an element generates that quotient if and only if its exponent in $\mathbb F_r^\times$ is odd.

The power conditions in <a href="#eq:conductor-power" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conductor-power">[eq:conductor-power]</a> say that $r$ splits in the Galois $2$-extension $$E_n=\mathbb Q(\zeta_{2^{n+1}},2^{1/2^n}).$$ This extension is unramified at odd primes. Consequently, independent square-root conditions at specified odd primes are compatible with splitting in $E_n$: any nontrivial product of their squareclasses is ramified at an odd prime, whereas every quadratic subfield of $E_n$ is unramified there. Chebotarev (Milne 2020b, chap. V, Theorem 3.23) <a id="pm-cite-90"></a>[[MilneCFT](#ref-MilneCFT)] therefore supplies $r$ satisfying <a href="#eq:conductor-power" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conductor-power">[eq:conductor-power]</a> with every $\ell\in\Sigma$ nonsquare modulo $r$. One can avoid any further fixed finite set of primes.

This already proves the field assertion for absolutely irreducible $\bar r$: take $F=L(r,n)$ and its degree-$2^a$ subfield for $A$. Choose $r$ unramified in the residual splitting field. Every nontrivial subextension of $F/\mathbb Q$ is ramified at $r$, so $F$ is disjoint from that splitting field. The residual image is therefore unchanged. Each $\ell\in\Sigma$ has one prime above it at every cyclic layer. The prime $r$ need not be allowed, since restricting a representation does not introduce ramification at a prime where it was unramified.

This completes the construction for absolutely irreducible residue: the residual image is unchanged and the number of allowed primes is bounded independently of the cyclic degree. For scalar residue we also need the Selmer vanishing in <a href="#eq:dual-vanishing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dual-vanishing">[eq:dual-vanishing]</a>. We obtain it from a different fixed base field and the following elementary form of the one-prime genus argument.

<a id="stmt-lem-one-prime-genus"></a>
<div id="lem:one-prime-genus" class="lemma">

**Lemma 29**. *Suppose that $K/E$ is a quadratic extension of totally real fields and that exactly one finite prime of $E$ ramifies. If the narrow class number of $E$ is odd, the narrow class number of $K$ is odd. For every totally real field with odd narrow class number, each totally positive unit is a square.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-one-prime-genus"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-one-prime-genus</summary>

*Proof.* If the narrow class number of $K$ were even, the nonzero $\mathbb F_2$-space $\operatorname{Cl}^+(K)/2$ would have a nonzero invariant linear functional for $\mathop{\mathrm{Gal}}(K/E)$. Here we use the elementary fact that a nonzero module of a finite $2$-group in characteristic two has nonzero invariants, applied to the dual module. Class field theory would give a quadratic extension $H/K$, unramified at every finite prime, which is Galois over $E$. The group $G=\mathop{\mathrm{Gal}}(H/E)$ has order four. Above the unique finite prime ramified in $K/E$, its inertia group $I$ has order two and maps isomorphically to $\mathop{\mathrm{Gal}}(K/E)$. It thus has trivial intersection with $\mathop{\mathrm{Gal}}(H/K)$. This excludes $G\simeq\mathbb Z/4\mathbb Z$, whose only subgroup of order two is $\mathop{\mathrm{Gal}}(H/K)$. Hence $G$ is the Klein four group. All inertia groups above that prime equal $I$, since $G$ is abelian, and all other finite inertia groups are trivial. The quadratic extension $H^I/E$ is consequently unramified at all finite primes, contrary to oddness of the narrow class number of $E$. Ramification at infinity is allowed by the narrow class field and causes no exception to this argument.

For the last assertion, the signature cokernel of the units is a $2$-group embedded in the narrow class group, so it is zero. For a totally real field of degree $e$, Dirichlet’s unit theorem (Milne 2020a, Theorem 5.1) <a id="pm-cite-91"></a>[[MilneANT](#ref-MilneANT)] gives $|\mathcal O_E^\times/(\mathcal O_E^\times)^2|=2^e$. The signature map from this group onto $\{\pm1\}^e$ is therefore an isomorphism. Its kernel is the group of totally positive units modulo squares. ◻

</details>

Now suppose $\alpha=1$. Put $$B=\mathbb Q(\sqrt{17}),\qquad
 \pi=\frac{5+\sqrt{17}}2.$$ The Minkowski bound (Milne 2020a, Theorem 4.3) <a id="pm-cite-92"></a>[[MilneANT](#ref-MilneANT)], here $\sqrt{17}/2<3$, says that every ideal class of $B$ has a representative of norm one or two. Both primes above two are principal, generated by $\pi$ and its conjugate, so the ordinary class number is one. The units $-1$ and $4+\sqrt{17}$ realize every signature; the latter has norm $-1$. Thus the narrow class number is also one.

Choose $r$ as above, additionally inert in $B$, and put $$F=B L(r,n),\qquad A=B L(r,n)_a,$$ where the subscript denotes the degree-$2^a$ layer. Inertness in $B$ is the extra condition that $17$ be a nonsquare modulo $r$. It is compatible with all the preceding conditions, including when $17\in\Sigma$, in which case it repeats an existing condition. Each quadratic step of $B L(r,n)/B$ has exactly one finite ramified prime, the prime over $r$. Lemma <a href="#lem:one-prime-genus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:one-prime-genus">29</a> implies that $F$ has odd narrow class number and that its totally positive units are squares. Take $S_A,S_F$ to contain only the prescribed places above $\Sigma$, two, and infinity; in particular $r$ is not allowed. There are at most two primes above any $\ell\in\Sigma$.

Let $w_1$ be the dyadic prime of $B$ generated by $\pi$, and $w_2$ the other dyadic prime. Both embeddings of $\pi$ into $\mathbb R$ are positive. At $w_2$ it is a unit congruent to seven modulo eight: the odd root of $X^2-5X+2$ is seven modulo eight. Thus $\pi$ is not a square in $B_{w_2}=\mathbb Q_2$.

<a id="eq:scalar-parity"></a>
Choose any $v_0$ of $A$ above $w_1$. We prove a stronger version of <a href="#eq:dual-vanishing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dual-vanishing">[eq:dual-vanishing]</a>, allowing arbitrary localization at every place of $F$ above $w_1$. It suffices to work with $\mathbb F_2$ coefficients. By Kummer theory a class in this enlarged group is represented by a totally positive $x\in F^\times$ modulo squares. Its valuations are even outside the set $T$ of dyadic places above $w_1$, and it is locally a square at all places above $w_2$. The valuation-parity map $$\label{eq:scalar-parity}
             [x]\longmapsto (v(x)\bmod2)_{v\in T}
                 \quad\hbox{into }\mathbb F_2^T$$ is injective even on the larger space of totally positive squareclasses with even valuations outside $T$. Indeed, zero parity gives $(x)=\mathfrak a^2$; odd ordinary class number makes $\mathfrak a$ principal, and division by a square leaves a totally positive unit, which is a square by Lemma <a href="#lem:one-prime-genus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:one-prime-genus">29</a>.

Our Selmer subspace is stable under the $2$-group $\mathop{\mathrm{Gal}}(F/B)$. If nonzero, it has a nonzero invariant vector. The action on $T$ is transitive, so injectivity of <a href="#eq:scalar-parity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalar-parity">[eq:scalar-parity]</a> forces the parity vector of that invariant to be the all-ones vector. The unique totally positive squareclass with this parity is $[\pi]$: it has valuation one at every place above $w_1$, and the injectivity just proved gives uniqueness. But $\pi$ is nonsquare at every place above $w_2$, since these completions are still $\mathbb Q_2$. This contradiction proves the required vanishing, also after extending coefficients from $\mathbb F_2$ to $k$.

Finally, the kernel of restriction of scalar $H^1$ from $A$ to $F$ consists of characters factoring through the cyclic group $\mathop{\mathrm{Gal}}(F/A)$. It is generated over $k$ by the unique nonzero $\mathbb F_2$-valued quadratic character, and every nonzero multiple is ramified above $r$. As $r\notin S_A$, these characters are excluded. This proves the restriction assertion in the scalar case.

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
#### Switching local conditions

The scalar case is now complete. With distinct residual characters, we will replace the class-group argument by successive changes of local conditions. The next lemma explains why a switch removes classes when the opposite Selmer group supplies enough local evaluations.

For the rest of the construction assume $\alpha\ne1$. Put $k_0=\mathbb F_2(\alpha)$ and $q=|k_0|\ge4$. Both $\alpha$ and $\alpha^{-1}$ are defined over $k_0$; the desired conclusions over $k$ follow by extension of scalars, since the continuous cochain complexes and all the specified local conditions commute with this finite scalar extension. Write $K_\alpha$ for the cyclic, totally real, odd-degree field cut out by $\alpha$.

This construction uses the viewpoint of Ramakrishna’s auxiliary-prime switching strategy (Ramakrishna 2002) <a id="pm-cite-93"></a>[[Ramakrishna2002](#ref-Ramakrishna2002)]; see also the exposition in Tang (Tang 2019, sec. 3A2, proof of Theorem 3.4) <a id="pm-cite-94"></a>[[Tang2019](#ref-Tang2019)]. The rank-one characteristic-two variant needed here is proved below, rather than obtained by applying either reference’s lifting theorem.

We first record the exact local switch used below. At an odd place where the coefficient character is trivial and the residue cardinality is one modulo four, local $H^1$ is two-dimensional. Its unramified line $L_{\mathrm{ur}}$ is self-orthogonal. Any line generated by a ramified quadratic character is transverse to it and is also self-orthogonal, for the pairing with the opposite coefficient. Indeed, the mod-two Hilbert pairing is alternating when $-1$ is a local square, and a line in a two-dimensional nondegenerate alternating space is its own annihilator.

<a id="stmt-lem-selmer-switch"></a>
<div id="lem:selmer-switch" class="lemma">

**Lemma 30** (Local switching). *Let $X$ be a Selmer group over a number field $E$ with one-dimensional coefficient $k_0(\beta)$, and let $Y$ be the Selmer group with coefficient $k_0(\beta^{-1})$ and orthogonal local conditions. Let $T$ be a finite set of odd places of $E$ where the coefficient character is trivial and the residue cardinality is one modulo four. Suppose that $X$ has the unramified local condition at every place in $T$, and that evaluation of $Y$ on unramified Frobenius gives a surjection $$Y\longrightarrow k_0^T.$$ Replacing the unramified lines at $T$ by arbitrary transverse ramified lines changes $X$ to $$\ker\bigl(X\longrightarrow k_0^T\bigr).$$ In particular the switch introduces no new classes, and it strictly decreases $X$ if its own evaluation is nonzero.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-selmer-switch"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-selmer-switch</summary>

*Proof.* Let $X^{\mathrm{rel}}$ have full condition at $T$ and the old conditions elsewhere. The relevant part of the Poitou–Tate comparison sequence is $$0\longrightarrow X\longrightarrow X^{\mathrm{rel}}
 \longrightarrow\bigoplus_{v\in T} H^1_v/L_{\mathrm{ur},v}
 \longrightarrow Y^\vee.$$ The last arrow is the transpose of localization on $Y$ under the perfect pairings $H^1_v/L_{\mathrm{ur},v}\times L_{\mathrm{ur},v}\to k_0$. It is injective by the hypothesis, so $X^{\mathrm{rel}}=X$. The new Selmer group is a subspace of this unchanged relaxed group. Its localizations must belong both to the old unramified line and the new transverse line, hence must be zero. This gives the asserted kernel exactly. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
#### Frobenius evaluations with splitting conditions

Take $B=\mathbb Q$ if $\alpha_2\ne1$. If $\alpha_2=1$, choose a prime $q_0\notin\Sigma\cup\{2\}$ with $$q_0\equiv1\pmod8,\qquad
           \alpha(\operatorname{Frob}_{q_0})\ne1,$$ and take $B=\mathbb Q(\sqrt{q_0})$. Such a prime exists by Chebotarev, since the odd-degree extension $K_\alpha$ is disjoint from $\mathbb Q(\zeta_8)$. In this second case let $w_1,w_2$ be the two dyadic places of $B$ and let $\sigma$ be its nontrivial automorphism.

All later conductor primes will satisfy <a href="#eq:conductor-power" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conductor-power">[eq:conductor-power]</a>, split in $K_\alpha$, and avoid the original primes and $q_0$. The first conductor $r_1$ also splits in $B$, with every $\ell\in\Sigma$ nonsquare modulo $r_1$. Compatibility follows from the independence of the odd squareclasses just used and from the coprimality of $[K_\alpha:\mathbb Q]$ with every auxiliary $2$-degree. For each subsequent conductor $r$, require every $\ell\in\Sigma$ and every earlier conductor to be a square modulo $r$. These conditions say that $r$ splits in a finite Galois $2$-extension $E_2$ obtained by adjoining $E_n$ and those square roots. In the quadratic-$B$ case, $E_2$ is unramified at $q_0$. Consequently $B\cap E_2=\mathbb Q$; the same is true of $B\cap K_\alpha E_2$. We can therefore impose either splitting or inertness in $B$ at a new conductor. In particular, $B$ is not included among the fields required to split at the final inert step.

<a id="stmt-lem-field-evaluations"></a>
<div id="lem:field-evaluations" class="lemma">

**Lemma 31** (Evaluation freedom). *Fix finitely many cohomology classes over $B$ with coefficients $k_0(\beta)$, where $\beta=\alpha$ or $\alpha^{-1}$, and impose the preceding splitting conditions in $K_\alpha E_2$. Evaluations use chosen places above the rational prime and chosen Frobenius representatives in a common finite quotient through which the coefficient actions and the finitely many cocycles factor. Numerical prescriptions refer to these choices.*

1.  *At primes split also in $B$, a Frobenius evaluation on any $k_0$-linearly independent collection of classes can be prescribed arbitrarily. If $B$ is quadratic, the pair of evaluations on a space $W$ at the two places above such a prime is given by one functional restricted to $W$ and $\sigma W$. It is uniformly distributed as two independent functionals on $W$ whenever $W\cap\sigma W=0$.*

2.  *In the quadratic case, for primes inert in $B$ the evaluations on $W$ are uniformly distributed in $W^\vee$ whenever $1+\sigma$ is injective on $W$. The evaluations on $c$ and $\sigma c$ at the single place above such a prime are equal.*

3.  *Collections for different Frobenius orbits of coefficient characters can be prescribed independently. If $\alpha^{-1}=\alpha^{2^j}$, coefficient Frobenius identifies the two collections semilinearly, and preserves all zero and rank conditions used here.*

*Every compatible prescription is realized, for suitable choices of these places and representatives, at infinitely many rational primes. Uniform distribution refers to the finite evaluation quotients used for this choice; no analytic density estimate is required. Chebotarev determines simultaneous conjugacy classes. The applications below use only rank and nonvanishing conditions, unchanged by the resulting coefficient scalings and interchange of the two split places.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-field-evaluations"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-field-evaluations</summary>

*Proof.* Set $N=G_{B K_\alpha E_2}$. The finite quotient $$\mathop{\mathrm{Gal}}(B K_\alpha E_2/B)=C\times P$$ has $C=\mathop{\mathrm{Gal}}(K_\alpha/\mathbb Q)$ of odd order and $P$ a $2$-group. On $k_0(\beta)$ the group $C$ acts nontrivially. Averaging over $C$ and using $k_0(\beta)^C=0$ gives $H^i(C\times P,k_0(\beta))=0$ for all $i\ge0$. Inflation–restriction thus makes restriction to $N$ injective. On $N$ the coefficients are trivial, so cocycles are additive homomorphisms and coboundaries are zero.

For independent classes $c_1,\ldots,c_t$, their joint evaluation map $N\to k_0^t$ has additive image stable under multiplication by $\beta(C)$. These scalars generate $k_0$ over $\mathbb F_2$, so its image is a $k_0$-subspace. If it were proper, a nonzero linear functional annihilating it would give a nontrivial linear combination of the $c_i$ restricting to zero on $N$, a contradiction. Its image is therefore all of $k_0^t$. The paired assertion follows by applying this to $W+\sigma W$; trivial intersection makes its two summands independent. The conjugation formula for cocycles identifies these with the evaluations at the two conjugate places.

<a id="eq:inert-evaluation"></a>
For the inert assertion choose $g\in G_{\mathbb Q}$ acting nontrivially on $B$ and trivially on $K_\alpha E_2$. Then $g^2\in N$, and $g$ represents $\sigma$ on cohomology. Since $\beta(g)=1$, for $h\in N$ the cocycle identity gives $$\label{eq:inert-evaluation}
 c((gh)^2)=c(g^2)+\operatorname{ev}_h((1+\sigma)c).$$ The first term is a fixed affine offset. The proven evaluation surjectivity on $(1+\sigma)W$ shows that this expression is uniform in $W^\vee$ if $1+\sigma$ is injective on $W$. Also $c(g^2)=(\sigma c)(g^2)$ and $(1+\sigma)\sigma c=(1+\sigma)c$ in cohomology. Evaluations on $N$ do not see coboundaries, so <a href="#eq:inert-evaluation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:inert-evaluation">[eq:inert-evaluation]</a> gives equality for $c$ and $\sigma c$, including the affine offset.

For different Frobenius orbits, regard these evaluation images as $\mathbb F_2[C]$-modules. They have different simple isotypes; semisimplicity of $\mathbb F_2[C]$ makes their joint image the product of the individual images. In the remaining case the automorphism $x\mapsto x^{2^j}$ of $k_0$ carries cocycles with coefficient $\alpha$ to those with coefficient $\alpha^{-1}$. It commutes with restriction, conjugation, and evaluation. Every ramified line below is generated by an $\mathbb F_2$-valued quadratic character, so these lines, as well as the zero, full, and unramified conditions, are preserved. All evaluation matrices are entrywise Frobenius transforms and have the same ranks.

The finite collection of cocycles factors through a finite quotient after adjoining their coefficient fields. Chebotarev realizes the conjugacy class of the chosen element of this quotient. Choosing a place in the corresponding finite Galois extension above the rational prime then chooses a Frobenius representative; one may choose the prescribed representative in that class. Conjugacy scales coefficient evaluations and may interchange the two split places, which preserves every rank and nonvanishing condition in use. Removing finitely many primes leaves infinitely many choices. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
#### The distinct-character construction

Given the chosen conductors $r_1,\ldots,r_t$, take a diagonal cyclic quotient of the product of the degree-$2^n$ fields $L(r_j,n)$: if $\gamma_j:G_{\mathbb Q}\to\mathbb Z/2^n\mathbb Z$ defines its $j$th factor, use $\gamma=\sum_j c_j\gamma_j$ with every $c_j$ odd. Let $L$ be the field of $\gamma$, let $L_a$ be its degree-$2^a$ layer, and write $$F=BL,\qquad A=BL_a,\qquad \delta=\gamma\bmod2.$$ When $B$ is quadratic, choose any $v_0$ of $A$ above $w_1$ and mark all its extensions to $F$. The character $\delta$ is the quadratic sum of the conductor characters. Every $r_j$ is totally ramified in $L$, and $2$ is split. At each original $\ell\in\Sigma$, the first summand of $\gamma(\operatorname{Frob}_\ell)$ is odd and all later summands are even, so its Frobenius has order $2^n$. At a previous conductor, adjoining a new conductor does not change the local quadratic line $k_0\delta$: the new quadratic character is unramified and trivial there. This follows from the imposed square conditions and quadratic reciprocity, since all conductors are one modulo four.

The allowed sets now include primes above all the $r_j$, in addition to the original allowed places. They do not need to include $q_0$. For $\beta=\alpha,\alpha^{-1}$ define $X_B^\beta$ as follows:

- its real conditions are zero;

- if $B=\mathbb Q$, its dyadic condition is zero; if $B$ is quadratic, its dyadic conditions are full at $w_1$ and zero at $w_2$;

- its other conditions are unramified, except above the $r_j$, where they are the lines $k_0\delta$.

At a conductor the coefficient is trivial, since $r_j$ splits in $K_\alpha$. These definitions therefore make sense, including at a possible inert conductor.

The remaining objective is to kill both spaces $X_B^\beta$ while keeping the number of conductors bounded. First we explain why this vanishing gives the desired Selmer condition over the large field $F$; we then construct the switches that achieve it.

Killing both spaces $X_B^\beta$ suffices for <a href="#eq:dual-vanishing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dual-vanishing">[eq:dual-vanishing]</a>. To see this, in the quadratic case first enlarge the possible marked set to all places above $w_1$. The resulting Selmer space over $F$ is stable under the cyclic $2$-group $\mathop{\mathrm{Gal}}(F/B)$. If it were nonzero it would have a nonzero invariant class. Since $k_0(\beta)^{G_F}=0$, inflation–restriction identifies these invariant classes with classes over $B$. At two and infinity the local conditions descend unchanged, because these places split completely. Away from the conductors, $F/B$ is unramified, so a class whose restriction is locally unramified has unramified localization downstairs. This applies both outside the allowed set and where localization was required to vanish. At a conductor the local extension is cyclic and totally ramified of degree $2^n$, and the kernel of local restriction is precisely the line of its quadratic character, namely $k_0\delta$. Thus the descended class belongs to $X_B^\beta$, giving the asserted sufficiency. For $B=\mathbb Q$ the same argument has no enlarged marked set.

*The case $B=\mathbb Q$.* Suppose first that $B=\mathbb Q$, so $\alpha_2\ne1$. For $X_B^\beta$ the Selmer dimension formula gives <a id="eq:q-selmer-difference"></a>$$\label{eq:q-selmer-difference}
      \dim X_B^\beta-\dim (X_B^\beta)^{\mathrm{dual}}=-1.$$ Here the dual group has coefficient $\beta^{-1}$ and orthogonal conditions. The real zero condition contributes $-1$; the dyadic zero condition contributes zero, since its local invariants vanish; all other local conditions contribute zero. Both global invariant terms vanish. Each opposite dual is therefore nonzero.

Append a conductor split in $K_\alpha E_2$ at which every opposite dual has nonzero evaluation, and every nonzero $X_B^\beta$ also has nonzero evaluation. Such a choice exists by Lemma <a href="#lem:field-evaluations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:field-evaluations">31</a>: per coefficient isotype there are at most two required nonvanishings, and the failure probability is at most $2/q<1$. Different isotypes can be prescribed separately; Frobenius-conjugate inverse characters impose identical conditions. Lemma <a href="#lem:selmer-switch" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:selmer-switch">30</a> says that neither space gains classes and that each nonzero space decreases. Repeating kills both spaces.

*Preparing the quadratic case.* It remains to carry out this argument when $B$ is quadratic, so $\alpha_2=1$. We first impose an auxiliary vanishing over $\mathbb Q$. Let $X_0^\beta$ have zero dyadic condition, full real condition, and the same non-dyadic conditions as $X_B^\beta$ (unramified away from the conductors, and $k_0\delta$ at them). Its dimension minus that of its opposite dual is again $-1$: the dyadic contribution is $-1$, the real contribution is zero, and all other contributions are zero. Restriction $H^1(G_{\mathbb Q},k_0(\beta))\to H^1(G_B,k_0(\beta))$ is injective, since $H^0(G_B,k_0(\beta))=0$. Rational classes and their opposite duals can therefore be included in the evaluation spaces over $B$ in Lemma <a href="#lem:field-evaluations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:field-evaluations">31</a>. Use the preceding one-coordinate switching argument to kill both $X_0^\beta$, requiring all the added conductors to split also in $B$. Lemma <a href="#lem:field-evaluations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:field-evaluations">31</a> permits this extra splitting. Keep all the resulting local switches.

<a id="eq:b-selmer-comparison"></a>
Let $Y_B^\beta$ be the group with coefficient $\beta$ whose conditions are dual to those of $X_B^{\beta^{-1}}$. Thus its real conditions are full, and its dyadic conditions are zero at $w_1$ and full at $w_2$. Its other conditions are the same as those of $X_B^\beta$. The dimension formula and conjugation give $$\label{eq:b-selmer-comparison}
 \dim Y_B^\beta=\dim X_B^{\beta^{-1}}+1,
       \qquad \sigma X_B^\beta\subset Y_B^\beta.$$ Indeed, for $X_B$ the two dyadic contributions are $2$ and $-1$, the two real contributions are $-1$ each, and the remaining contributions vanish. The inclusion follows from the local conditions: conjugation exchanges the dyadic conditions and the real zero conditions are contained in the full ones.

The following table summarizes the four groups used in the quadratic case. Every row has coefficient $k_0(\beta)$, and “full” means the full local $H^1$. The two entries for $B$ are ordered as $(w_1,w_2)$; the single entry for $\mathbb Q$ is the condition at $2$.

<div class="center">

| Group                                | Base field  |   Real places   |    Dyadic places    |
|:-------------------------------------|:------------|:---------------:|:-------------------:|
| $X_B^\beta$                          | $B$         |       $0$       | $(\mathrm{full},0)$ |
| $Y_B^\beta$                          | $B$         | $\mathrm{full}$ | $(0,\mathrm{full})$ |
| $X_0^\beta$                          | $\mathbb Q$ | $\mathrm{full}$ |         $0$         |
| $(X_0^{\beta^{-1}})^{\mathrm{dual}}$ | $\mathbb Q$ |       $0$       |   $\mathrm{full}$   |

</div>

At every selected conductor all four groups use the line $k_0\delta$, and at every other non-dyadic finite place they use the unramified condition. The rational opposite dual in the last row will keep $X_0=0$ while the paired conditions over $B$ are switched.

<a id="stmt-lem-disjoint-conjugate-selmer"></a>
<div id="lem:disjoint-conjugate-selmer" class="lemma">

**Lemma 32**. *As long as all conductors used so far split in $B$ and both $X_0^\beta$ are zero, one has $$W\cap\sigma W=0
        \quad\text{for }W=X_B^\beta\text{ and }W=Y_B^\beta.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-disjoint-conjugate-selmer"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-disjoint-conjugate-selmer</summary>

*Proof.* The intersection is stable under $\sigma$. If nonzero it has a nonzero invariant class, which descends to $\mathbb Q$ because $k_0(\beta)^{G_B}=0$. Its dyadic localization is zero: intersecting either pair of conjugate dyadic conditions imposes zero at both $w_1$ and $w_2$. At infinity it satisfies at least the full condition defining $X_0^\beta$; this observation also covers $W=Y_B^\beta$. At every conductor, splitting in $B$ makes descent of the rational line $k_0\delta$ immediate. At other primes unramified in $B/\mathbb Q$, the unramified condition descends by restriction to inertia. There is no condition to check at $q_0$: the coefficient is unramified with nontrivial Frobenius there, so local $H^0$ and $H^2$ vanish and the local Euler formula gives $H^1=0$. Thus the nonzero descended class lies in $X_0^\beta$, a contradiction. ◻

</details>

We have arranged $X_0^\beta=0$ for both coefficient characters. The next split switches must preserve these vanishings while decreasing the nonzero spaces $X_B^\beta$. The preceding lemma, together with Lemma <a href="#lem:field-evaluations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:field-evaluations">31</a>, supplies the freedom to choose paired evaluations needed for each new split conductor.

*Split switches.* Now suppose both $X_B$ spaces are nonzero. Include the restrictions of the rational opposite-dual classes in the evaluation spaces just described. Choose a new split conductor with the following three properties, for each coefficient isotype:

1.  the paired evaluation on $Y_B^\beta$ has rank two;

2.  the paired evaluation on $X_B^\beta$ is nonzero;

3.  the evaluation on the opposite dual of the zero space $X_0^{\beta^{-1}}$ is nonzero.

The third dual has dimension one, by its dimension difference. The first space has dimension at least two by <a href="#eq:b-selmer-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:b-selmer-comparison">[eq:b-selmer-comparison]</a>. Lemmas <a href="#lem:field-evaluations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:field-evaluations">31</a> and <a href="#lem:disjoint-conjugate-selmer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:disjoint-conjugate-selmer">32</a> make the paired evaluations on each of the first two spaces independent uniform functionals. For $t=\dim Y_B^\beta\ge2$, the rank failure probability is $$1-(1-q^{-t})(1-q^{1-t})\le q^{-1}+q^{-2}.$$ The second failure probability is at most $q^{-2}$, and the third is $q^{-1}$. Their sum satisfies <a id="eq:split-switch-probability"></a>$$\label{eq:split-switch-probability}
            (q^{-1}+q^{-2})+q^{-2}+q^{-1}<1.$$ No independence between these three failure events is needed for this union bound. Different character isotypes are independent; conjugate inverse characters give identical requirements under coefficient Frobenius. Hence an admissible conductor exists.

Switching at its two places makes both $X_B$ spaces smaller and introduces no new classes. The third requirement has a separate purpose: Lemma <a href="#lem:selmer-switch" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:selmer-switch">30</a>, now over $\mathbb Q$, preserves $X_0^\beta=0$. Thus Lemma <a href="#lem:disjoint-conjugate-selmer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:disjoint-conjugate-selmer">32</a> remains applicable at the next split step. Continue until both $X_B$ spaces vanish or exactly one remains.

<a id="eq:one-survivor"></a>
*The final inert switch.* In the latter situation write $X_B^\beta\ne0$ and $X_B^{\beta^{-1}}=0$. Formula <a href="#eq:b-selmer-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:b-selmer-comparison">[eq:b-selmer-comparison]</a> implies $$\label{eq:one-survivor}
 \dim X_B^\beta=\dim Y_B^\beta=1,
            \qquad Y_B^\beta=\sigma X_B^\beta.$$ If the inverse characters were Frobenius conjugate, their $X_B$ spaces would have equal dimension, so this case could not arise.

Choose one final conductor inert in $B$, still split in $K_\alpha E_2$. Immediately before this step Lemma <a href="#lem:disjoint-conjugate-selmer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:disjoint-conjugate-selmer">32</a> holds, so $1+\sigma$ is injective on each relevant $X_B$ and $Y_B$: its kernel would be a fixed vector in $W\cap\sigma W$. At this conductor require nonzero evaluation on both opposite duals $Y_B^\alpha,Y_B^{\alpha^{-1}}$ and on the remaining $X_B^\beta$. There is no third independent demand in the surviving isotype. By <a href="#eq:one-survivor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:one-survivor">[eq:one-survivor]</a> and the exact equality in Lemma <a href="#lem:field-evaluations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:field-evaluations">31</a>, the evaluations on $Y_B^\beta$ and $X_B^\beta$ coincide. The affine offset in <a href="#eq:inert-evaluation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:inert-evaluation">[eq:inert-evaluation]</a> does not change this equality. Uniform evaluation and the union bound give the conservative failure bound $2/q<1$ per isotype. The switch at the single place over this inert prime therefore kills the last $X_B$ space, while the opposite zero space stays zero. This is the final step: preservation of $X_0=0$ after it is neither required nor asserted. Its pre-step vanishing was used precisely to justify injectivity of $1+\sigma$.

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
#### Uniformity, restriction, and the numerical bounds

The constructions now give the required dual Selmer vanishing. To finish Proposition <a href="#prop:field-preparation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:field-preparation">28</a>, we bound the number of conductors, check preservation of residual data, and convert that vanishing into the strict Selmer bounds. The conductor count is the point that allows both cyclic exponents to grow.

The number of conductors in the preceding argument is bounded independently of $n$ and $a$. Here is an explicit accounting which also avoids making any uniformity assumption about their sizes. Let $b=[B:\mathbb Q]\le2$, and let $$C_E=\sum_{\beta\in\{\alpha,\alpha^{-1}\}}
    \dim_{k_0}H^1(G_{E,T_E},k_0(\beta)),\qquad E=\mathbb Q,B,$$ where $T_E$ is the fixed set above $\Sigma$, two, and infinity. In the quadratic case one may include $q_0$ in $T_B$ without change to the relevant local cohomology. Count both characters in this sum even if they are Frobenius conjugate; the resulting bound is only larger. Allowing one new rational prime freely adds at most $2b$ to each global $H^1$ dimension over $B$, by restriction to inertia or by the localization sequence. Thus it adds at most $4b$ to their sum. This deliberately generous bound applies uniformly to every conductor and every quadratic line chosen there.

<a id="eq:uniform-conductor-count"></a>
After the first conductor, the sum of the two dimensions over $\mathbb Q$ is at most $C_{\mathbb Q}+4$. The first loop killing the auxiliary $X_0$ spaces therefore has length $$N_0\le C_{\mathbb Q}+4.$$ During it, the sum of the $B$-dimensions is at most $C_B+4b(1+N_0)$. Every later split switch decreases that sum by at least one and never increases either space. There is at most one final inert switch. Hence in the quadratic case the total number $t$ of conductors satisfies, for example, $$\label{eq:uniform-conductor-count}
 t\le 2+C_B+4b+(1+4b)(C_{\mathbb Q}+4).$$ For $B=\mathbb Q$ the direct loop gives the simpler bound $t\le1+C_{\mathbb Q}+4$. These constants depend only on the fixed original data and the fixed choice of $B$.

Every original $\ell\in\Sigma$ has full Frobenius order in $L$ and its degree-$2^a$ layer, so has at most $b$ primes in either $A$ or $F$. Every conductor is totally ramified in both cyclic layers, so likewise has at most $b$ primes. Consequently $$u_A,u_F\le b(|\Sigma|+t).$$ The analogous scalar bound is $2|\Sigma|$, and the absolutely irreducible bound is $|\Sigma|$. This proves the required common constant $U$. The degrees are $d_A=b2^a$, $d=b2^n$, and $m=2^{n-a}$ in the marked cases. Taking $a$ and $n-a$ large makes $d_A$ and $[F:A]$ large independently. In the marked cases this makes $m$ large and gives $m/d=1/d_A$.

Since $K_\alpha/\mathbb Q$ has odd degree, $\alpha$ remains nontrivial on every field constructed in the distinct-character case. Inflation–restriction, with $H^0(G_F,k(\beta))=0$, proves injectivity of restriction from $A$ to $F$. Together with the scalar and absolutely irreducible cases proved above, this establishes all the field and restriction assertions of Proposition <a href="#prop:field-preparation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:field-preparation">28</a>.

It remains to derive <a href="#eq:strict-selmer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:strict-selmer">[eq:strict-selmer]</a>. Since the mod-two cyclotomic character is trivial, the Tate dual of $k(\beta)$ is $k(\beta^{-1})$. Its Selmer conditions dual to the strict ones are exactly those in <a href="#eq:dual-vanishing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dual-vanishing">[eq:dual-vanishing]</a>. The dual Selmer group is zero. The global invariant terms in the Selmer dimension formula cancel, whether $\beta=1$ or $\beta\ne1$. At a real place, the full condition contributes $\dim H^1_v-h^0_v=1-1=0$. At an unmarked dyadic place, local Euler characteristic gives contribution one if $\alpha_2\ne1$ and two if $\alpha_2=1$. At a marked place the zero condition contributes $-h^0_v=-1$. At a non-dyadic allowed place the full condition contributes $h^2_v\le1$, and an unramified condition elsewhere contributes zero. Adding these terms gives exactly the two bounds in <a href="#eq:strict-selmer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:strict-selmer">[eq:strict-selmer]</a>.

For later use, the degree choices can in particular ensure the strict inequalities <a id="eq:field-unmarked-gap"></a><a id="eq:field-marked-gap"></a><a id="eq:field-curve-gap"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 d+u_F+3&<2d-1 &&\text{in the unmarked reducible case},\label{eq:field-unmarked-gap}\\
 2d-2m+u_F+3&<2d-m-1 &&\text{in the marked case},\label{eq:field-marked-gap}\\
 2d-2m-4-u_F&>d.\label{eq:field-curve-gap}
\end{aligned}$$ For example, it is enough in the marked case to have $m>U+4$ and $m(d_A-2)>U+4$. The same freedom makes $d_A$ larger than any prescribed fixed cost in the initial trace cuts. These choices are legitimate because <a href="#eq:uniform-conductor-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:uniform-conductor-count">[eq:uniform-conductor-count]</a> and the bounds for $u_A,u_F$ are independent of both cyclic exponents.

<a id="sec:connectedness"></a>
### Connectedness and potential pro-modularity

We now connect a modular seed to the target. The field construction of Section <a href="#sec:fields" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:fields">6</a> makes the reducible locus small after recording local lines at selected dyadic primes. The unrestricted propagation theorem of Section <a href="#sec:propagation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:propagation">5</a> can then cross successive component intersections.

<a id="stmt-thm-potential-promodularity"></a>
<div id="thm:potential-promodularity" class="theorem">

**Theorem 33**. *Let $r$ satisfy the hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> and be not virtually solvable. Then $r$ is pro-modular over some solvable totally real Galois extension of $\mathbb Q$, of even degree and split completely at $2$.*

*More generally, fix a complete integral framed deformation family over $\mathbb Q$, with determinant $\chi=\det r$, containing the coefficient point defined by $r$, and with ramification confined to a finite set. The closed trace image of this family is pro-modular after restriction to one common extension of the stated kind, at one fixed tame level.*

</div>

To prove the family assertion, we will compare a curve in a Hecke component with a curve in an unrestricted deformation component containing the target family. The first curve requires a cuspidal lift of the target’s semisimple residue. We choose that lift before preparing the fields, so that its tame primes belong to the fixed allowed set used in the field estimates.

<a id="pm-h-38"></a>
#### A modular residual seed

<a id="stmt-lem-modular-seed"></a>
<div id="lem:modular-seed" class="lemma">

**Lemma 34**. *Every semisimple two-dimensional residual representation arising from the target has a classical cuspidal lift over $\mathbb Q$, after finite coefficient extension and allowing a finite enlargement of the tame level.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-modular-seed"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-modular-seed</summary>

*Proof.* For absolutely irreducible residue this is Serre’s modularity theorem, including characteristic two (Kisin 2007, Theorem 0.2) <a id="pm-cite-95"></a>[[Kisin2005Survey](#ref-Kisin2005Survey)]; the results are due to Khare–Wintenberger and Kisin (Khare and Wintenberger 2009a, 2009b; Kisin 2009a) <a id="pm-cite-96"></a>[[KhareWintenberger2009](#ref-KhareWintenberger2009), [KhareWintenberger2009II](#ref-KhareWintenberger2009II), [Kisin2009](#ref-Kisin2009)]. The characteristic-two parity condition is automatic.

For reducible residue, the existence of a cuspidal lift, including in characteristic two, is already proved by Billerey–Menares (Billerey and Menares 2016, Theorem 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-97"></a>[[BillereyMenares2016](#ref-BillereyMenares2016)]. The geometric argument below uses the same Eisenstein difference as their (Billerey and Menares 2016, Theorem 2.3) <a id="pm-cite-98"></a>[[BillereyMenares2016](#ref-BillereyMenares2016)]; we retain the details of integrality, cuspidality, and lifting needed here.

Write the reducible residue as $\bar\chi_1\oplus\bar\chi_2$ and put $\alpha=\bar\chi_1/\bar\chi_2$. After a finite-order twist it suffices to lift the eigensystem of $1\oplus\alpha$. The character $\alpha$ has odd order, is unramified at $2$, and has an even Dirichlet lift of odd conductor. Choose an even weight $k'\ge4$ and a normalized Eisenstein series $f$ with this character pair and $a_1(f)=1$. Take an odd level $N\ge5$ divisible by the conductor. Let $\ell$ be a prime congruent to one modulo $N2^a$, where $a$ will be sufficiently large. Put $$g(\tau)=f(\tau)-f(\ell\tau).$$ Regard this as a form of level $\Gamma_1(N\ell)$. Its positive Fourier coefficients at infinity are $2$-integral, and its constant term there is zero. The tame modular curve has good reduction, with geometrically integral special fiber (Katz and Mazur 1985, Corollary 10.9.5(2) and Table 10.9.6) <a id="pm-cite-99"></a>[[KatzMazur1985](#ref-KatzMazur1985)]. The $q$-expansion principle (Katz 1973, Theorem 1.6.1 and Corollary 1.6.2) <a id="pm-cite-100"></a>[[Katz1973](#ref-Katz1973)] therefore extends $g$ integrally: a vertical pole would be detected at that component’s cusp expansion.

We verify cuspidality modulo $\varpi$ at every cusp. For a cusp matrix $\gamma\in\mathop{\mathrm{SL}}_2(\mathbb Z)$, the primitive-column decomposition is $$\begin{pmatrix}\ell&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}\gamma
   =\gamma'
     \begin{pmatrix}e&t\\0&\ell/e\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad e\in\{1,\ell\}.$$ Since $\ell\equiv1\pmod N$, one can choose $\gamma'\equiv\gamma\pmod N$. The two constant terms in $g|_{k'}\gamma$ therefore differ by the factor $$1-(\ell/e)^{-k'}.$$ There are only finitely many constant terms of $f$ at level $N$. Choose $a$ larger than their denominator valuations, with an additional power sufficient for reduction modulo $\varpi$. The displayed factor makes every constant term of $g$ vanish in the residue field. Its coefficient of $q$ is one, so the reduction is nonzero. Away from $N\ell$, it is an eigenvector with the Eisenstein residual eigensystem $1\oplus\alpha$.

This mod-$\varpi$ cusp form lifts to the characteristic-zero cuspidal space in the same weight. Indeed on the tame modular curve, Kodaira–Spencer gives $\Omega^1\simeq\omega^2(-\mathrm{cusps})$. For this formula and positivity of $\omega$, use (Katz 1973, sec. 1.5 and Appendix A1.5.4) <a id="pm-cite-101"></a>[[Katz1973](#ref-Katz1973)]: after adjoining the required roots of unity, a symplectic full-level component maps to the $\Gamma_1$-curve by a finite cover of odd degree. The logarithmic Kodaira–Spencer isomorphism descends along this tame cover, and positivity descends along the finite map. On the special fiber, Serre duality (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Lemma 53.4.1 and Remark 53.4.3, Tags 0BS2 and 0BS4) <a id="pm-cite-102"></a>[[StacksProject](#ref-StacksProject)] identifies the obstruction $H^1(\omega^{k'}(-\mathrm{cusps}))$ with the dual of $H^0(\omega^{2-k'})$, which is zero for $k'\ge4$. The corresponding integral $H^1$-module is finite by proper coherent cohomology (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Lemma 30.19.2, Tag 02O6) <a id="pm-cite-103"></a>[[StacksProject](#ref-StacksProject)]. The multiplication-by-$\varpi$ exact sequence and the special-fiber vanishing make multiplication by $\varpi$ surjective on this module. Nakayama’s lemma makes it zero, so reduction of cuspidal sections is surjective. The finite integral cuspidal Hecke module has a characteristic-zero eigenpacket specializing to this residual eigensystem: its Hecke algebra is finite and torsion-free over $\mathcal O$, and the residual maximal ideal lies above a horizontal minimal prime. Finally undo the finite-order twist. The construction also covers $\alpha=1$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-39"></a>
#### Field choices

Fix the target’s semisimple residue $\bar r$ and determinant $\chi$. Choose a cuspidal lift of this residue by Lemma <a href="#lem:modular-seed" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:modular-seed">34</a>, and fix a finite allowed set over $\mathbb Q$ containing the bad primes of the target, the prescribed family when present, and this lift. This choice precedes the field construction below; it ensures that the bound for the number of allowed primes also covers the modular seed. In the reducible case write $\bar r^{\mathrm{ss}}=\bar\chi_1\oplus\bar\chi_2$ and $\alpha=\bar\chi_1/\bar\chi_2$. Use the fields $$\mathbb Q\subset A\subset F$$ of Proposition <a href="#prop:field-preparation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:field-preparation">28</a>, with $d_A=[A:\mathbb Q]$, $d=[F:\mathbb Q]$. There are no marks when $\bar r$ is absolutely irreducible or $\alpha|_{G_{\mathbb Q_2}}\ne1$. Otherwise choose the distinguished place $v_0$ of $A$ and mark its $m=[F:A]$ lifts to $F$. The numbers $u_A,u_F$ of allowed non-dyadic primes are uniformly bounded as these degrees grow.

<a id="eq:degree-choices"></a>
Choose the degrees sufficiently large that $$\label{eq:degree-choices}
 d>u_F+4,\qquad
 m>u_F+4\quad\text{if there are marks},\qquad
 2d-2m-4-u_F>d.$$ In the unmarked case set $m=0$. Since $m/d=1/d_A$ in the marked case and $m/d=0$ otherwise, the further fixed lower bounds on $d_A$ below can be imposed simultaneously.

<a id="pm-h-40"></a>
#### Charts with transverse local lines

Over the characteristic-two fixed-determinant pseudo-base, take the finite-type framed representation scheme and adjoin a local invariant projective line at every mark. Call the resulting scheme $Y$. In its closed residual fiber, call a point *transverse* if none of its marked lines is globally invariant. The condition is geometric: it must hold after extending its residue field to an algebraic closure. There is no additional condition when there are no marks. The transverse locus is open in the residual fiber, since the incidence of a global invariant line equal to a specified marked line is proper.

At a transverse point with marks, the local residual representation at each mark is scalar after untwisting. Indeed there is a global invariant line, and the different marked invariant line splits its restriction. The two local semisimple characters coincide. This simple observation is responsible for the strict Selmer condition in the next lemma.

<a id="stmt-lem-good-connectedness"></a>
<div id="lem:good-connectedness" class="lemma">

**Lemma 35**. *Let $F$, the marked set, and the fixed-determinant representation scheme $Y$ be as above, with the degree inequalities <a href="#eq:degree-choices" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:degree-choices">[eq:degree-choices]</a>. Let $y$ be a transverse closed residual point of $Y$, with finite residue field, and let $R'_y$ be the completion of $Y$ at $y$. Every component of $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}R'_y$ has dimension at least $2d-m$. Its components can be joined by a chain whose successive intersections have dimension at least $2d-m-1$. The globally reducible locus has dimension strictly less than $2d-m-1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-good-connectedness"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-good-connectedness</summary>

*Proof.* Retain the real local problems and the $m$ marked dyadic line problems as the local base. Reducing this base changes no spectrum.
