By Lemma <a href="#lem:marked-local-base" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:marked-local-base">15</a>, it is a complete local domain of dimension $$2d+5m.$$ The relative framed presentation of Proposition <a href="#prop:relative-presentation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:relative-presentation">9</a> has generators minus relations at least $$3-6m-h^0((\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}^0)^\vee(1))\ge-6m.$$ The last inequality uses the three-dimensionality of $(\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}^0)^\vee(1)$. A quotient of a complete local domain with this presentation has all components of dimension at least $2d-m$. Grothendieck’s connectedness theorem gives connectedness in dimension at least $2d-m-1$: in the form used here, cutting a complete local domain of dimension $N$, after adding $a$ formal variables, by $b$ equations gives connectedness in dimension at least $N+a-b-1$. This is the component-chain formulation of the connectedness theorem (Grothendieck 2005, Exposé XIII, Théorème 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-104"></a>[[SGA2](#ref-SGA2)].

We bound the reducible locus separately. It is empty for absolutely irreducible residue. Otherwise add a global invariant line by proper incidence. At a closed point of that incidence, choose an adapted basis and first hold this line fixed. Its movement costs one parameter. The diagonal global characters with fixed product vary in dimension at most one, by abelian Leopoldt over $F$; see Lemma <a href="#lem:pseudo-bad-loci" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pseudo-bad-loci">11</a>. Freeze those character parameters.

At a marked place the closed local matrices are scalar and the marked line is transverse to the chosen global line. An infinitesimal upper extension, with diagonal characters frozen, must therefore vanish locally: a nonzero upper nilpotent matrix cannot preserve a second transverse line. Thus its cohomology class is strict at the marks. Moving a marked line costs at most one further tangent parameter, giving $m$ in total. There is at most one upper coboundary parameter. The strict Selmer bounds in <a href="#eq:strict-selmer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:strict-selmer">[eq:strict-selmer]</a> therefore give the following dimensions: $$\begin{cases}
 d+u_F+3,&\alpha|_{G_{\mathbb Q_2}}\ne1,\\
 2d-2m+u_F+3,&\alpha|_{G_{\mathbb Q_2}}=1.
 \end{cases}$$ Here the three remaining parameters account for the diagonal base, the global line, and the possible coboundary. The relative tangent estimate bounds the number of generators over the diagonal base, so it is an upper bound for the whole formal reducible chart, not only for its tangent space at a fixed diagonal character. Properness of the global-line incidence transfers the bound to its image. Finite residue extensions do not affect these dimensions.

The first two inequalities in <a href="#eq:degree-choices" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:degree-choices">[eq:degree-choices]</a> make both bounds smaller than $2d-m-1$. This proves the lemma. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-chart-propagation"></a>
<div id="lem:chart-propagation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 36**. *At a transverse closed residual point, either every component of $Y$ through that point has potentially pro-modular pseudo-image, or none does. It suffices to begin with one component whose pseudo-image contains a potentially pro-modular, non-virtually-solvable curve having finite local images at the allowed non-dyadic places.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-chart-propagation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-chart-propagation</summary>

*Proof.* Work first in the completed chart. By Lemma <a href="#lem:good-connectedness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:good-connectedness">35</a>, two successive components in a connecting chain intersect in dimension at least $2d-m-1$. Choose an irreducible component of maximal dimension in that intersection. Its generic global representation is absolutely irreducible, because the reducible locus has smaller dimension. Pass first to the completed trace-and-character image, retaining the characters on the marked lines. Forgetting the global frame and the line choices loses at most $3+m$ dimensions, by Lemma <a href="#lem:dimension-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:dimension-comparison">5</a>. Proposition <a href="#prop:rep-space" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:rep-space">4</a> makes this image finite over its trace image, so forgetting the recorded characters causes no further dimension loss. Thus the pseudo-image of the chosen component has dimension at least $$2d-2m-4.$$ At each allowed non-dyadic prime impose that the trace of a chosen Frobenius lift equal its residual value. These $u_F$ equations leave a closed locus of dimension at least $2d-2m-4-u_F>d$. The bad pseudo-locus bounds of Lemma <a href="#lem:pseudo-bad-loci" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pseudo-bad-loci">11</a> and curve avoidance therefore produce a non-virtually-solvable curve in it. Lemma <a href="#lem:inertia-basechange" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:inertia-basechange">12</a> gives finite local images at the non-dyadic allowed places.

If the preceding component is potentially pro-modular, this curve is potentially pro-modular. Theorem <a href="#thm:localized-propagation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:localized-propagation">22</a> makes the next component potentially pro-modular. A starting curve as in the statement first makes its containing component potentially pro-modular by that same theorem. Iterating crosses the entire chain. Only finitely many extensions and levels occur. Take the compositum of their Galois closures and a common deeper level, using Lemma <a href="#lem:forward-basechange" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:forward-basechange">21</a>.

Completion is faithfully flat. Every component of $Y$ through the closed point is the contraction of a minimal prime of the completed chart. The component pseudo-images are the corresponding trace contractions. The conclusion therefore descends from the chart to $Y$. ◻

</details>

The local chart statement must next be joined across different residual extension classes. The transverse residual condition was chosen so that this joining does not require changing the semisimple residue.

<a id="stmt-lem-transverse-fiber"></a>
<div id="lem:transverse-fiber" class="lemma">

**Lemma 37**. *If nonempty, the transverse closed residual fiber of $Y$ is geometrically connected. Consequently, if one transverse chart has potentially pro-modular component images, every transverse chart does.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-transverse-fiber"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-transverse-fiber</summary>

*Proof.* Work over an algebraic closure of the residue field. For scalar residue with marks, a transverse residual representation is a nonzero additive extension after a scalar twist. It has a unique global invariant line. Its cocycle is zero at the marks. With the global line fixed, these cocycles form a vector space with the origin removed. The marked complementary lines form products of affine line charts. Conjugating the basis gives a connected parameter space whose image is the entire transverse fiber.

For distinct residual characters, use both upper triangular orientations. In either orientation, with its global line fixed, the extension cocycles form a vector space. In the marked case they vanish locally at the marks. The allowed choices of marked lines form a nonempty open subset of the product with projective lines, hence an irreducible parameter space. Varying the basis preserves connectedness. The two orientations meet at the split representation; when there are marks, choose lines avoiding both global invariant lines. Without marks there is no transversality condition, and the same two families meet at that split representation. For absolutely irreducible residue, the residual fiber is a conjugacy orbit. These parameter spaces are finite-dimensional algebraic spaces of cocycles: this follows either from the residual representation scheme or from the finite-dimensional residual $H^1$. This proves geometric connectedness.

There are finitely many components of the noetherian scheme $Y$. Their nonempty intersections with the transverse residual fiber form a finite closed cover. Make a graph with these components as vertices, joining two vertices when they meet in that fiber. The graph is connected because the fiber is connected. Choose a spanning tree and, for each edge, a closed point of the corresponding intersection in the transverse locus, after a finite coefficient extension. At that point Lemma <a href="#lem:chart-propagation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:chart-propagation">36</a> gives the same potential pro-modularity status to the two components.

Only finitely many such points and completed-chart component chains are needed. Their field extensions have a common Galois compositum, and forward base change gives one common deeper tame level. Thus the conclusion holds simultaneously for every component meeting the transverse fiber, and hence for every transverse chart. Every component through a transverse point is accounted for by completion, as in the preceding lemma. The finite coefficient extensions are harmless because the integral forms and their Hecke support commute with them. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-41"></a>
#### From the two curves to the target family

The proof first constructs large seed and target loci over $A$, then chooses a characteristic-two curve in each. Appropriate lattices put both curves in transverse charts over $F$. Connectedness transfers potential pro-modularity from the seed curve to the target curve; unrestricted propagation then reaches the entire containing target locus.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-transverse-fiber-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-transverse-fiber</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:potential-promodularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:potential-promodularity">33</a>.* *The seed and target loci over $A$.* For the seed and fields already chosen, solvable base change and Jacquet–Langlands give a cuspidal residual packet over $A$. It remains cuspidal by regularity, as in Lemma <a href="#lem:forward-basechange" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:forward-basechange">21</a>.

Its central character need agree with $\psi=\chi\varepsilon$ only modulo $\varpi$. At a sufficiently deep tame level their transformations coincide on reduction. The exact integral function model of Proposition <a href="#prop:completed-forms" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:completed-forms">16</a> therefore gives residual occurrence also for the specified central character $\psi$. Proposition <a href="#prop:hecke-dimension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:hecke-dimension">19</a> supplies a pro-modular component $C_{\mathrm{seed}}$ in the fixed-determinant pseudo-base over $A$ with $$\dim C_{\mathrm{seed}}\ge1+2d_A.$$

Independently choose an unrestricted global fixed-determinant framed component through $r|_{G_A}$, or containing the restricted framed support of the prescribed family. Such a component exists by taking a minimal prime below that support. Its generic representation is absolutely irreducible because it contains the absolutely irreducible target point. The relative presentation over the real local rings gives dimension at least $$1+2d_A+3-h^0((\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}^0)^\vee(1))$$ before forgetting the frame. The loss under trace contraction is at most three. Its pseudo-image $C_{\mathrm{target}}$ therefore has dimension at least $2d_A-2$. This large unrestricted component contains the trace image of the initially prescribed family; no lower dimension bound on that smaller family is required.

*Choosing curves in the two loci.* In each of $C_{\mathrm{seed}}$ and $C_{\mathrm{target}}$, pass to the special fiber and prescribe residual Frobenius trace at every bad non-dyadic place. If there are marks, prescribe in addition that the entire local pseudorepresentation at $v_0$ is its constant residual one. This last condition costs at most the number of topological generators of one fixed local pseudo-ring for $\mathbb Q_2$, a constant independent of $d_A$. All the other costs are bounded by $1+u_A$, with a fixed additional constant for the target dimension estimate. Taking $d_A$ sufficiently large leaves dimension greater than $d_A$. By Lemma <a href="#lem:pseudo-bad-loci" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pseudo-bad-loci">11</a>, each locus contains a non-virtually-solvable curve. The Frobenius constraints make its bad local images finite.

*Entering the transverse fiber over $F$.* We explain why both curves enter transverse charts over $F$. Without marks, any stable lattice suffices. With marks, the local pseudo at $v_0$ is constant and scalar after untwisting, so the curve-field representation has an invariant line there after a finite coefficient extension. Choose a nonzero vector $v$ on this line and let $$\mathcal V=\sum_{g\in G_A} k'[[t]]\,\rho(g)v.$$ Compactness bounds this module inside a fixed lattice. Global irreducibility makes it full rank, and it is stable by construction. The vector $v$ is primitive in $\mathcal V$: if it belonged to $t\mathcal V$, all its translates would, and Nakayama would force $\mathcal V=0$. Its reduction lies in no global invariant line, since its orbit spans $\mathcal V/t\mathcal V$.

Restrict the lattice to $G_F$. No new global invariant line appears in the residue. For distinct residual characters, a nonsplit extension remains nonsplit by the restriction injectivity in Proposition <a href="#prop:field-preparation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:field-preparation">28</a>; a split representation retains its two distinct character lines. For scalar semisimplification, the spanning property above rules out a split scalar residue, and its nonzero additive extension remains nonzero by the same injectivity. These descriptions hold after extending the residue field. Since $F/A$ is Galois, $G_A$ permutes the globally invariant lines of the restricted residue. Transporting the chosen local line to every prime over $v_0$ therefore preserves its avoidance of all these lines. Hence every transported marked line is transverse. Both curves pass through transverse points of the same residual scheme $Y$.

*Returning from the target curve to its containing locus.* The seed curve remains potentially pro-modular after restriction to $F$. Its finite bad local images allow Theorem <a href="#thm:localized-propagation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:localized-propagation">22</a> to start the propagation in its transverse chart. Lemma <a href="#lem:transverse-fiber" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:transverse-fiber">37</a> then gives potentially pro-modular images at every transverse chart. In particular the restricted target curve is potentially pro-modular. By definition, the original target curve over $A$ is therefore potentially pro-modular as well. Apply Theorem <a href="#thm:localized-propagation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:localized-propagation">22</a> over $A$ to this original curve and its containing locus $C_{\mathrm{target}}$. The whole locus, and therefore the target and the prescribed family trace image, becomes pro-modular after a common allowed extension.

The finite component graph in Lemma <a href="#lem:transverse-fiber" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:transverse-fiber">37</a> and the finite chains inside its selected charts require only finitely many propagation steps. Replace their fields by one Galois compositum and their levels by one deeper tame level, using forward base change. Total reality, complete splitting at $2$, and solvability over $\mathbb Q$ are preserved. This proves both assertions. ◻

</details>

The distinction between the two spaces in this proof is important. Marks and transverse lines produce connectedness in the global special fiber. The propagation theorem subsequently applies to unrestricted dyadic deformation loci. No ordinary or potentially crystalline restriction has been imposed on the target. The next section extracts its classical vectors.

<a id="sec:classicality"></a>
### Classicality and descent

Theorem <a href="#thm:potential-promodularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:potential-promodularity">33</a> places the target representation in the closed Hecke support after a solvable totally real base change. We now produce a locally algebraic vector with its eigensystem. The additional work is needed when the local representation at $2$ is reducible: the two possible orders in a unitary principal series need not have the same locally algebraic vectors. Throughout this section the target $r$ is not virtually solvable, as arranged in the preliminary reductions. In particular, its restriction to every finite extension is absolutely irreducible.

We retain the conventions of Section <a href="#sec:completed" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:completed">4</a>: $\chi=\det r$, the central character of completed forms is $\psi=\chi\varepsilon$, and the local block parameter is $r\otimes\varepsilon$. Cyclotomic exponents are counted with $\operatorname{exp}(\varepsilon)=1$. Thus a de Rham character of exponent $a$ has Hodge–Tate weight $-a$ in the convention used by the ordinary modularity theorem below. The field $F$ will always be totally real, solvable over $\mathbb Q$, of even degree, and split completely at $2$. Until the final subsection, assume that $r|_{G_{\mathbb Q_2}}$ is reducible. We will retain its higher-exponent line in a global family, show that the joint trace-and-character support is finite over weight space, and use dense points that become modular after normalization and dualization. Those points determine the inducing order to retain at the target. Specialization will then give an actual vector, whose local algebraicity is proved directly. The locally absolutely irreducible case will use Proposition <a href="#prop:block-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:block-comparison">17</a> in the final subsection.

<a id="pm-h-43"></a>
#### The local line and its global family

<a id="eq:rank-one-euler"></a>
<a id="eq:rank-one-geometric"></a>
We first record the rank-one consequence of Bloch–Kato that fixes the orientation. For a de Rham character $\theta$ of $G_{\mathbb Q_2}$, write $H^1_g(\mathbb Q_2,\theta)$ for the subspace of de Rham extension classes. The local Euler characteristic, duality, and the Bloch–Kato formulas give $$\begin{aligned}
 \dim H^1(\mathbb Q_2,\theta)
 &=1+\dim H^0(\mathbb Q_2,\theta)
       +\dim H^0(\mathbb Q_2,\theta^{-1}\varepsilon),\label{eq:rank-one-euler}\\
 \dim H^1_g(\mathbb Q_2,\theta)
 &=\dim D_{\mathrm{dR}}(\theta)/\mathop{\mathrm{Fil}}^0
   +\dim H^0(\mathbb Q_2,\theta)
   +\dim D_{\mathrm{cris}}(\theta^{-1}\varepsilon)^{\varphi=1}.
   \label{eq:rank-one-geometric}
\end{aligned}$$ The second formula follows from the exact annihilators of (Bloch and Kato 1990, Proposition 3.8) <a id="pm-cite-105"></a>[[BlochKato1990](#ref-BlochKato1990)], the $H^1_f$ and $H^1_e$ dimension formulas of (Bloch and Kato 1990, Corollary 3.8.4) <a id="pm-cite-106"></a>[[BlochKato1990](#ref-BlochKato1990)], and local duality; it is the rank-one consequence used here. These formulas have no restriction excluding $2$.

<a id="stmt-lem-higher-line"></a>
<div id="lem:higher-line" class="lemma">

**Lemma 38**. *If $r|_{G_{\mathbb Q_2}}$ is reducible and has distinct cyclotomic exponents, it has an invariant line whose exponent is greater than that of the quotient.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-higher-line"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-higher-line</summary>

*Proof.* Characters occurring as subquotients of a de Rham representation are de Rham. Suppose a chosen subcharacter has smaller exponent than the quotient, and let $\theta$ be their ratio. Its exponent is strictly negative, so $D_{\mathrm{dR}}(\theta)/\mathop{\mathrm{Fil}}^0=0$ and $H^0(\mathbb Q_2,\theta)=0$. The character $\theta^{-1}\varepsilon$ has strictly positive exponent. If its finite inertial part is nontrivial, its crystalline module is zero; otherwise its crystalline Frobenius eigenvalue has nonzero valuation and cannot be $1$. Equation <a href="#eq:rank-one-geometric" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rank-one-geometric">[eq:rank-one-geometric]</a> therefore gives $H^1_g=0$. The extension splits, and its other summand is the required line. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-ordinary-character-family"></a>
<div id="lem:ordinary-character-family" class="lemma">

**Lemma 39**. *Suppose $r|_{G_{\mathbb Q_2}}$ is reducible, and record the line of Lemma <a href="#lem:higher-line" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:higher-line">38</a>. Fix $\chi=\det r$. After enlarging coefficients, there is a complete local integral $\mathcal O$-algebra $\mathscr D$, together with its fixed global determinant $\chi$, its global trace, and a recorded local subcharacter $\eta_1$, with the following properties.*

1.  *$\mathscr D$ is horizontal, contains the target point, and $\dim\mathscr D\ge2$. Put $\eta_2=\chi\eta_1^{-1}$. On its globally absolutely irreducible locus the local representation has subcharacter $\eta_1$ and quotient $\eta_2$.*

2.  *For one field $F$ as above, the restricted trace lies in the Hecke support of completed forms at a fixed tame level.*

3.  *$\mathscr D$ is finite over the closed image of the global pseudodeformation ring for $F$. It is also finite over the image of the product of the dyadic pseudodeformation rings.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-ordinary-character-family"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-ordinary-character-family</summary>

*Proof.* Use a framed global deformation chart with fixed determinant and an invariant line at $2$, in an integral basis for the target and its line. Consider its completed local ring at the characteristic-zero target point. Corollary <a href="#cor:charzero-line-dimension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:charzero-line-dimension">10</a> gives dimension at least five for the local line chart and dimension two for the odd real factor, over the characteristic-zero coefficient field. The relative presentation of Proposition <a href="#prop:relative-presentation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:relative-presentation">9</a> has generators minus relations at least $-3$: its dual invariant term vanishes because the global representation is not virtually solvable. The global completed local ring therefore has dimension at least $5+2-3=4$. The characteristic-zero dimension comparison in Lemma <a href="#lem:curve-completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:curve-completion">7</a> then gives a horizontal integral component through the target in the original chart of dimension at least five.

Contract that component to its global trace and local subcharacter values. On the absolutely irreducible locus the only positive dimensional fibers are conjugacy fibers, of dimension three. The local characters are distinct on a dense open set, so recording the character does not leave an extra moving-line fiber there. The dimension comparison of Lemma <a href="#lem:dimension-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:dimension-comparison">5</a> gives a domain $\mathscr D$ of dimension at least two. Properness of the invariant-line incidence preserves the recorded subcharacter condition on the irreducible locus. Theorem <a href="#thm:potential-promodularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:potential-promodularity">33</a>, applied to an unrestricted framed component containing this family, gives one common $F$ and one tame level with the asserted trace support.

We give the finiteness argument, including the recorded character. Write $T_{\mathscr D}$ for the trace of the family. Let $A\subset\mathscr D$ be the closed restricted trace image, and let $q=[G_{\mathbb Q}:G_F]$. For every $g\in G_{\mathbb Q}$, $g^q\in G_F$ after replacing $q$ by the order of the finite quotient if necessary. The identity $$T_{\mathscr D}(g^q)=P_q\bigl(T_{\mathscr D}(g),\chi(g)\bigr)$$ has $P_q(X,Y)$ monic of degree $q$ in $X$. Hence every downstairs trace is integral over $A$. A local character value satisfies $$\eta_1(g)^2-T_{\mathscr D}(g)\eta_1(g)+\chi(g)=0,
 \qquad g\in G_{\mathbb Q_2},$$ and is integral as well. The global determinant algebra has finitely many topological trace generators, and a continuous local character is determined topologically by its values on the uniformizer, $-1$, and $5$. Adjoining these finitely many integral elements to $A$ gives a finite complete algebra, hence a closed subalgebra of $\mathscr D$; it contains its topological generators and is all of $\mathscr D$. Finally, Proposition <a href="#prop:block-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:block-comparison">17</a> makes the Hecke algebra finite over the product of the local central pseudodeformation images. Transitivity of finiteness proves the last assertion. ◻

</details>

We may discard the proper globally reducible loci, including reducibility after restriction to $F$. We may also require <a id="eq:generic-ordinary-ratios"></a>$$\label{eq:generic-ordinary-ratios}
 \eta_1\eta_2^{-1}\notin\{1,\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{-1}\}.$$ Indeed, each exceptional equality and the fixed determinant imply $\eta_1^2=\chi\varepsilon^a$ for $a\in\{0,1,-1\}$. The local trace image then has dimension at most one: its character values are integral over the fixed coefficient ring. Because $2$ splits in $F$ and the family comes from $\mathbb Q$, the same holds at all dyadic factors. Finiteness in Lemma <a href="#lem:ordinary-character-family" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:ordinary-character-family">39</a> shows that none of these loci contains $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathscr D$. Characteristic-zero points with finite coefficient fields satisfying these conditions are consequently dense.

<a id="pm-h-44"></a>
#### The ordinary torsion module

We now have a family carrying both global traces and the chosen local character. To control its character parameter, we construct a torsion space on which the traces and torus act jointly. Its finiteness over torus weight space will constrain the dimension of $\mathscr D$.

Fix the Hecke summand of the completed forms over $F$ carrying the restricted family, and write $\mathcal H$ for its torsion function space with coefficients $E/\mathcal O$. Put $$G_2=\prod_{v\mid2}\mathop{\mathrm{GL}}_2(\mathbb Q_2),\quad
 N_0^-=\prod_{v\mid2}n^-(\mathbb Z_2),\quad
 T_1=\prod_{v\mid2}(1+4\mathbb Z_2)^2,$$ where $$n^-(a)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\a&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 n^+(x)=\begin{pmatrix}1&x\\0&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 \mathbf p=\bigl(\operatorname{diag}(1,2)\bigr)_{v\mid2}.$$ All matrix formulas involving several places are interpreted coordinatewise. On $\mathcal H^{N_0^-}$ define <a id="eq:ordinary-U"></a>$$\label{eq:ordinary-U}
 U=\sum_{a\in(\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z)^{[F:\mathbb Q]}}n^-(a)\mathbf p.$$ The actions are the right translation actions with the representation group law. Let $$W=\{f\in\mathcal H^{N_0^-}:U^Nf=f\text{ for some }N>0\},
 \qquad M=W^\vee,
 \qquad \Omega=\mathcal O[[T_1]].$$ The dual is the compact Pontryagin dual. We transpose the commuting operator actions without inversion, so their scalar eigenvalues retain the conventions above.

<a id="stmt-prop-ordinary-weight-finiteness"></a>
<div id="prop:ordinary-weight-finiteness" class="proposition">

**Proposition 40** (Ordinary weight finiteness). *The space $W$ is an $\mathcal O$-submodule stable under $T_1$, the tame Hecke operators, and $U^{\pm1}$, and these actions commute. Its dual $M$ is finite over $\Omega$. The closed commutative algebra generated by these actions on $M$ is finite over $\Omega$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-ordinary-weight-finiteness"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-ordinary-weight-finiteness</summary>

<a id="eq:U-representatives"></a>
*Proof.* In one factor the identity $$\label{eq:U-representatives}
 n^-(a+2b)\operatorname{diag}(1,2)
 =n^-(a)\operatorname{diag}(1,2)n^-(b)$$ proves independence of representatives and preservation of lower invariance. Conjugation by $t=\operatorname{diag}(u_1,u_2)\in T_1$ replaces $a$ by $(u_2/u_1)a$, which permutes the residue classes. Thus $tU=Ut$ on the lower-invariant space. The tame Hecke actions commute with these local actions. A common multiple of two periods is a period of the sum of the corresponding vectors. Finally $U$ is bijective on $W$: on a vector of period $N$, its inverse is $U^{N-1}$, independently of the chosen period.

We show that one compact open subgroup fixes $W[\varpi]^{T_1}$. For $j\ge2$, $x\in2^j\mathbb Z_2$, and $a\in\mathbb Z_2$, direct multiplication gives <a id="eq:U-upper-improvement"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 n^+(x)n^-(a)\operatorname{diag}(1,2)
 ={}&n^-\!\left(\frac{a}{1+xa}\right)
       \operatorname{diag}(1,2)\notag\\
 &\cdot\operatorname{diag}(1+xa,(1+xa)^{-1})
       n^+\!\left(\frac{2x}{1+xa}\right).
       \label{eq:U-upper-improvement}
\end{aligned}$$ If $f$ is $T_1$-fixed and upper-$2^{j+1}$-invariant, the final two factors fix $f$. The map $a\mapsto a/(1+xa)$ permutes the lower residue classes, and <a href="#eq:U-representatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:U-representatives">[eq:U-representatives]</a> absorbs the changes of lift. Thus $Uf$ is upper-$2^j$-invariant. Once upper-$4$ invariance is reached, it is preserved by the same identity.

Each smooth vector of $W[\varpi]^{T_1}$ starts at some finite upper level. Iterate the preceding improvement far enough, taking an iteration number divisible by its period. The resulting vector is the original vector, so it is fixed by the single open compact subgroup generated by $N_0^-$, $T_1$, and $\prod_{v\mid2}n^+(4\mathbb Z_2)$. Proposition <a href="#prop:completed-forms" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:completed-forms">16</a> gives smooth admissibility; therefore $W[\varpi]^{T_1}$ is finite-dimensional over $k$. Compact Nakayama now gives finite generation of $M$ over $\Omega$.

Since $\Omega$ is noetherian and $M$ is finite, $\mathop{\mathrm{End}}_\Omega(M)$ is finite over $\Omega$. The closed algebra in the statement is an $\Omega$-submodule of this finite module, and hence is finite too. ◻

</details>

Let $R$ denote the fixed-determinant global pseudodeformation ring over $F$. The trace and torus actions define $$Q=R\widehat\otimes_{\mathcal O}\Omega
       \longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{End}}_\Omega(M).$$ Write $S$ for its image. It is finite over $\Omega$ and is closed: the source is compact and the target is Hausdorff. The larger action algebra in Proposition <a href="#prop:ordinary-weight-finiteness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:ordinary-weight-finiteness">40</a>, denoted by $\mathcal A$, is finite over $S$ and contains both $U$ and $U^{-1}$.

<a id="pm-h-45"></a>
#### Finite joint images and the weight map

<a id="eq:two-ordinary-orders"></a>
Set $$\Lambda=\mathcal O[[1+4\mathbb Z_2]]\simeq\mathcal O[[T]],\qquad
 [5]=1+T.$$ The map $\Lambda\to\mathscr D$ sends $[5]$ to $\eta_1(5)$, using local reciprocity. At each place of $F$ above $2$, there are two possible inducing assignments $$\label{eq:two-ordinary-orders}
 (\eta_1\varepsilon,\eta_2),\qquad (\eta_2\varepsilon,\eta_1).$$ For each choice $s$ of an assignment at every dyadic place, the fixed determinant and these formulas define a homomorphism $s:\Omega\to\Lambda$. Only characters on $1+4\mathbb Z_2$ enter this map; the unramified character values remain in the trace algebra.

<a id="stmt-lem-joint-image"></a>
<div id="lem:joint-image" class="lemma">

**Lemma 41** (Finite joint-image argument). *Let $\mathscr D$ be the domain of Lemma <a href="#lem:ordinary-character-family" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:ordinary-character-family">39</a>, let $M=W^\vee$ be the ordinary module above, and use the weight map $\Lambda\to\mathscr D$ defined by $[5]\mapsto\eta_1(5)$. Suppose a dense set of finite-coefficient characteristic-zero points of $\mathscr D$ occurs in the joint trace-and-weight support of $M$, with one of the finitely many assignments $s$ in <a href="#eq:two-ordinary-orders" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:two-ordinary-orders">[eq:two-ordinary-orders]</a> at each point. Then $\mathscr D$ is finite and dominant over $\Lambda$; in particular, $\dim\mathscr D=2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-joint-image"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-joint-image</summary>

*Proof.* For each $s$, form $S_s=S\widehat\otimes_{\Omega,s}\Lambda$, and let $T_s$ be the image of $R\widehat\otimes_{\mathcal O}\Lambda$ in $S_s$. Both are finite $\Lambda$-algebras, and their images are closed. Let $I_s$ be the kernel of the latter map. Each point in the dense set kills some $I_s$ after mapping to $\mathscr D$. Hence $$\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathscr D=\bigcup_s V(I_s\mathscr D).$$ The spectrum is irreducible, so one of these finitely many closed sets is the whole spectrum. Equivalently, the prime kernel of $R\widehat\otimes\Lambda\to\mathscr D$ contains one $I_s$. The closed trace-plus-weight image $B$ in $\mathscr D$ is therefore a quotient of $T_s$ and is finite over $\Lambda$.

By Lemma <a href="#lem:ordinary-character-family" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:ordinary-character-family">39</a>, $\mathscr D$ is finite over the restricted trace image $A$, and $A\subset B$. The same finite set of $A$-module generators generates $\mathscr D$ as a $B$-module. Thus $\mathscr D$ is finite over $\Lambda$. Its dimension is at least two, whereas $\dim\Lambda=2$; a nonzero prime kernel in the domain $\Lambda$ would lower that dimension. The map is therefore injective, and the dimension is exactly two. ◻

</details>

The hypotheses of the lemma hold here. At each finite-coefficient characteristic-zero point outside the excluded loci, Proposition <a href="#prop:block-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:block-comparison">17</a> supplies a Banach subrepresentation of completed forms whose local factors are unitary continuous inductions in one of the orders <a href="#eq:two-ordinary-orders" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:two-ordinary-orders">[eq:two-ordinary-orders]</a>; the two possibilities are precisely those of (Paškūnas and Tung 2021, Corollary 6.10(3)) <a id="pm-cite-107"></a>[[PaskunasTung2021](#ref-PaskunasTung2021)] for the normalized parameter $r\otimes\varepsilon$. In $\mathop{\mathrm{Ind}}_B^{\mathop{\mathrm{GL}}_2(\mathbb Q_2)}(\delta_1\otimes\delta_2)$, with $B$ the upper Borel and unnormalized induction, take the function supported on $Bn^-(\mathbb Z_2)$ and equal to $1$ on $n^-(\mathbb Z_2)$. It is lower-invariant and has torus character $(\delta_1,\delta_2)$. Subdivision into the two lower disks shows that its $U$-eigenvalue is $\delta_2(2)$, a unit. The product vector has the analogous properties at all dyadic places.

Its nonzero image in completed forms can be scaled into the integral lattice. Modulo every power of the coefficient uniformizer its unit eigenvalue has finite multiplicative order, so the reduction belongs to $W$. Duality consequently places this trace-and-weight point in $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}S$. Finite coefficient extension is harmless, either by flat extension of the torsion space or by taking scalar coordinates. Lemma <a href="#lem:joint-image" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:joint-image">41</a> applies.

Thus the family is finite and dominant over the one-variable weight space. We can now choose a dense set of character values there and lift them to the family. At these points the local extension will satisfy the ordinary modularity theorem after the normalization and dualization described next.

<a id="pm-h-46"></a>
#### A dense set of ordinary modular points

<a id="eq:translated-torsion-weights"></a>
Let $w=\operatorname{exp}(\chi)$. After a finite extension of $E$, choose $c$ with $c^2=5^{w+1}$. Consider weights $$\label{eq:translated-torsion-weights}
                 \eta_1(5)=c\zeta,
       \qquad \zeta\in\mu_{2^\infty}.$$ These are continuous characters of $1+4\mathbb Z_2$: this group is procyclic with generator $5$, and $c\zeta$ lies in the open unit neighborhood of $1$ in its finite coefficient field. We are specifying a character value, not asserting that $(w+1)/2$ is an integral Hodge–Tate weight.

The weights in <a href="#eq:translated-torsion-weights" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:translated-torsion-weights">[eq:translated-torsion-weights]</a> are Zariski dense in $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\Lambda$. Indeed, after dividing by its maximal scalar power, a nonzero element of $\mathcal O[[T]]$ is a unit times a distinguished polynomial by Weierstrass preparation and has only finitely many zeros in the open unit disk. Finite dominance then gives a dense set of coefficient points of $\mathscr D$ above these weights, even after removing any prescribed proper closed subset. To see the last assertion directly, a nonzero ideal of the finite integral domain $\mathscr D$ meets $\Lambda$ nontrivially: an integral equation of minimal degree for a nonzero element of the ideal has nonzero constant term. Thus every proper closed subset has proper closed image in $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\Lambda$.

On inertia, the ratio at these points has the form <a id="eq:ordinary-ratio-xi"></a>$$\label{eq:ordinary-ratio-xi}
                    \eta_1\eta_2^{-1}=\varepsilon\xi,$$ where $\xi$ has finite order. This follows from $\eta_1^2/\chi$ on $1+4\mathbb Z_2$, and the remaining unit quotient is finite. The finite part of $\chi$ is fixed; at most finitely many choices of $\zeta$ make $\xi$ trivial. We discard them, as well as the globally reducible loci before and after restriction to $F$.

<a id="eq:normalized-ordinary-characters"></a>
There is a single continuous normalizing character over the family. The character $\eta_2$ on inertia factors through the local unit group $\mathbb Z_2^\times$ by local class field theory. Denote this unit character by $\eta_{2,\mathrm{u}}$, and put <a id="eq:normalizing-character"></a>$$\label{eq:normalizing-character}
 \lambda(g)=\eta_{2,\mathrm{u}}\bigl(\varepsilon(g)\bigr)^{-1},
 \qquad g\in G_{\mathbb Q}.$$ This character is continuous and unramified outside $2$. At a point chosen above, write $r'=r_x\otimes\lambda_x$ and $\eta'_j=\eta_{j,x}\lambda_x$. Then $$\label{eq:normalized-ordinary-characters}
                \eta'_1=u_h\xi\varepsilon,
          \qquad\eta'_2=u_l,$$ with $u_h,u_l$ unramified and $\xi$ of nontrivial finite inertia.

<a id="stmt-lem-dense-thorne-points"></a>
<div id="lem:dense-Thorne-points" class="lemma">

**Lemma 42**. *Every representation $r'$ just constructed is odd, absolutely irreducible, finitely ramified, ordinary, and potentially crystalline with cyclotomic exponents $\{1,0\}$. It is modular.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-dense-thorne-points"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-dense-thorne-points</summary>

*Proof.* The two diagonal characters in <a href="#eq:normalized-ordinary-characters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normalized-ordinary-characters">[eq:normalized-ordinary-characters]</a> are de Rham. Their ratio $\theta=(u_h/u_l)\xi\varepsilon$ has $H^0(\theta)=H^0(\theta^{-1}\varepsilon)=0$, the latter because the finite inertial character is nontrivial. Thus <a href="#eq:rank-one-euler" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rank-one-euler">[eq:rank-one-euler]</a> gives $\dim H^1(\mathbb Q_2,\theta)=1$. In <a href="#eq:rank-one-geometric" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rank-one-geometric">[eq:rank-one-geometric]</a> the de Rham tangent term has dimension one, while the other two terms vanish. Hence every extension class belongs to $H^1_g$ and $r'$ is de Rham.

The $p$-adic monodromy theorem makes it potentially semistable (Berger 2002, Theorems 0.6 and 5.19) <a id="pm-cite-108"></a>[[Berger2002](#ref-Berger2002)]. Its two finite inertial characters are distinct. Monodromy commutes with inertia, so it preserves their two eigenspaces; it vanishes on each by nilpotence. Thus it is potentially crystalline.

Twisting preserves absolute irreducibility and finite ramification. For a complex conjugation $c_\infty$, $\lambda(c_\infty)^2=1$, so the determinant remains odd. Finally, in the convention $\mathop{\mathrm{HT}}(\varepsilon)=-1$, the dual has weights $\{0,1\}$ and ordinary shape $$(r')^\vee|_{G_{\mathbb Q_2}}\sim
 \begin{pmatrix}
   u_l^{-1}&*\\0&\varepsilon^{-1}u_h^{-1}\xi^{-1}
 \end{pmatrix}.$$ All the hypotheses of (Thorne 2026, Theorem D) <a id="pm-cite-109"></a>[[Thorne2026](#ref-Thorne2026)] are now satisfied. That theorem makes the dual modular, and hence makes $r'$ modular. ◻

</details>

The modular representations in this lemma have a common bound for their tame conductors. Indeed, the original noetherian Galois family has, at each non-dyadic bad prime, a common open inertia subgroup on which its action is unipotent, as in Lemma <a href="#lem:inertia-basechange" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:inertia-basechange">12</a>. The finite inertial quotient is fixed, and the rank-two monodromy contribution is bounded. This bounds the Artin conductor after the fixed base change to $F$. The normalizing characters add no non-dyadic inertia. Consequently, after replacing the tame compact subgroup by one smaller subgroup, all these modular forms occur at a single tame level. Solvable base change, iterated through prime-degree cyclic extensions, and Jacquet–Langlands put them in the definite forms over $F$; see (Langlands 1980, vol. 96, chap. 11, Lemma 11.3 and Proposition 11.4) <a id="pm-cite-110"></a>[[Langlands1980](#ref-Langlands1980)] <a id="pm-cite-111"></a>[[ArthurClozel1989](#ref-ArthurClozel1989)], (Arthur and Clozel 1989, vol. 120, chap. III, Theorems 4.2 and 5.1), and (Jacquet and Langlands 1970, Theorem 16.1).

<a id="pm-h-47"></a>
#### The required inducing order

We next track the order at the dense modular points, rather than selecting it from a semisimple Banach fiber. In the geometric reciprocity convention $\varepsilon=x|x|$, one has $\mathop{\mathrm{WD}}(\varepsilon)=|\cdot|$. For the normalized characters <a href="#eq:normalized-ordinary-characters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normalized-ordinary-characters">[eq:normalized-ordinary-characters]</a>, the two characters of $\mathop{\mathrm{WD}}(r'\otimes\varepsilon)$ are $$\theta_h=u_h\xi|\cdot|^2,
          \qquad\theta_l=u_l|\cdot|.$$ Here the same notation for a finite or unramified character denotes its character under local reciprocity. A regular algebraic Hilbert form on $\mathop{\mathrm{GL}}_2$ is essentially self-dual via its central character. Local–global compatibility identifies the smooth principal series: the underlying semisimple Weil representation has these distinct inertial characters, and no monodromy can join them. This also follows from the compatibility up to the monodromy order of (Barnet-Lamb et al. 2014a, Corollary 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-112"></a>[[BLGGTLocalGlobal2014](#ref-BLGGTLocalGlobal2014)], since that order preserves the underlying semisimple Weil representation and monodromy is zero in this case.

In the conventions of Proposition <a href="#prop:block-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:block-comparison">17</a>, put the smooth inducing characters in the order $$(\theta_l,\theta_h|\cdot|^{-1})
             =(u_l|\cdot|,u_h\xi|\cdot|).$$ The algebraic factor at cyclotomic exponents $\{1,0\}$ is $\det$. Multiplication by that factor changes the pair to $$(u_l\varepsilon,u_h\xi\varepsilon)
                         =(\eta'_2\varepsilon,\eta'_1).$$ For unnormalized smooth induction, the intertwiner changes an inducing pair $(A,B)$ to $(B|\cdot|,A|\cdot|^{-1})$. The two orders here are therefore intertwined with the appropriate modulus factors, with no exceptional ratio because their inertial characters differ. Equivalently, this is the weight-two instance of the ordinary universal completion formula (Breuil and Emerton 2010, Proposition 2.2.1) <a id="pm-cite-113"></a>[[BreuilEmerton2010](#ref-BreuilEmerton2010)]; that proposition applies at $2$ and concerns the locally algebraic induction. In particular, the lower-disk vector used above already belongs to this locally algebraic representation.

<a id="eq:favorable-ordinary-order"></a>
Undo the twist by multiplying definite functions by $\lambda_x^{-1}$ evaluated on the reduced norm, via global reciprocity. Rational reduced norms are totally positive, so this is well-defined on the definite quotient. It changes the central character back to $\chi\varepsilon$ and changes the inducing assignment to $$\label{eq:favorable-ordinary-order}
                       (\eta_2\varepsilon,\eta_1)
                 \quad\text{at every }v\mid2.$$ Lower invariance is preserved, and the $U$-eigenvalue is multiplied by a unit. All dyadic places have the same order because they are split over $\mathbb Q$ and the family and normalizing character descend from $\mathbb Q$.

At this common tame level, let $M$ and $S$ again denote the ordinary dual and its joint trace-and-weight image algebra. Write $s_{\mathrm{fav}}:\Omega\to\Lambda$ for the single assignment <a href="#eq:favorable-ordinary-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:favorable-ordinary-order">[eq:favorable-ordinary-order]</a>, and set $$S_{\mathrm{fav}}
   =S\widehat\otimes_{\Omega,s_{\mathrm{fav}}}\Lambda,
 \qquad
 I_{\mathrm{fav}}
   =\ker\bigl(R\widehat\otimes_{\mathcal O}\Lambda
                         \longrightarrow S_{\mathrm{fav}}\bigr).$$ Let $T_{\mathrm{fav}}$ be the image of this map. The untwisted vectors just constructed show that $I_{\mathrm{fav}}$ vanishes at the dense family points used in Lemma <a href="#lem:dense-Thorne-points" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:dense-Thorne-points">42</a>. Its image in the domain $\mathscr D$ is therefore zero. In particular, the original target defines a coefficient point of $T_{\mathrm{fav}}$, including when its local character ratio satisfies one of the exceptional equalities excluded from the dense testing set.

The algebra $S_{\mathrm{fav}}$ is finite over $T_{\mathrm{fav}}$, so lying over lifts this point to $S_{\mathrm{fav}}$ after a finite coefficient extension. Its restriction to $S$ has the target trace and the torus character prescribed by $s_{\mathrm{fav}}$. Since $M$ is finite over $S$ and the $S$-action is faithful, $\mathop{\mathrm{Supp}}_S M=\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}S$. Thus this point belongs to the joint trace-and-weight support needed below.

The favorable character order has now reached the target. To use it in a classicality argument, we still need a nonzero vector carrying that order. The next step obtains one from a scalar quotient of the finite action module, without assuming that ordinary vectors or locally algebraic vectors commute with specialization.

<a id="pm-h-48"></a>
#### Specialization to a genuine vector

<a id="stmt-lem-eigenvector-specialization"></a>
<div id="lem:eigenvector-specialization" class="lemma">

**Lemma 43** (Eigenvector specialization). *Let $S$ be the joint trace-and-weight image algebra acting on $M=W^\vee$ at the fixed tame level above, and let $\mathcal A$ be its finite faithful action algebra containing $U^{\pm1}$, as in Proposition <a href="#prop:ordinary-weight-finiteness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:ordinary-weight-finiteness">40</a>. Let a finite-coefficient characteristic-zero point of $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}S$ belong to the support of $M$. After a finite coefficient extension it gives a nonzero vector in completed forms with the specified trace and torus eigencharacters, invariant under $N_0^-$, and with a unit $U$-eigenvalue.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-eigenvector-specialization"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-eigenvector-specialization</summary>

*Proof.* The algebra $\mathcal A$ containing $U^{\pm1}$ is finite over $S$. Since it is the faithful action algebra of $M$, and $M$ is finite over it, its spectrum is the support of $M$. Lying over the given point and passing to a finite coefficient field $E'$ therefore gives a joint scalar quotient of $M[1/2]$. Write $\mathcal O'$ for its ring of integers, with uniformizer $\varpi'$.

The corresponding nonzero map $$M\otimes_{\mathcal O}\mathcal O'\longrightarrow E'$$ is continuous. Indeed, $M$ is finite over weight space and its weight values lie in the formal open unit disk. Its image is a compact $\mathcal O'$-submodule spanning $E'$, hence a lattice; rescale it to $\mathcal O'$. The scalar actions preserve this lattice. In particular the $U$-eigenvalue is a unit because $U^{-1}$ preserves it as well.

Applying $\mathcal O'$-linear Pontryagin duality, with values in $E'/\mathcal O'$, produces an equivariant injection $$E'/\mathcal O'\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow
                    W_{\mathcal O'}\subset\mathcal H_{\mathcal O'}.$$ The image $f_n$ of $\varpi'^{-n}$ has exact order $\varpi'^n$ and satisfies $\varpi'f_{n+1}=f_n$. Taking the Tate module gives a nonzero continuous integral completed-forms vector. Explicitly, a compatible sequence of continuous functions modulo $\varpi'^n$ determines a continuous $\mathcal O'$-valued function, because each of its reductions is continuous; conversely every such integral function gives that sequence. Exact orders ensure that the resulting vector is nonzero. All Hecke and torus eigenrelations, lower invariance, and the $U$-eigenrelation pass to this inverse limit. This construction finds a divisible copy inside $W$; it does not require $W$ itself to be divisible. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:nonnegative-algebraic-degree"></a>
Apply the lemma to the target. Its torus eigencharacter, restricted to a sufficiently small open subgroup, is algebraic with exponents $e_{1,v},e_{2,v}$ satisfying $$\label{eq:nonnegative-algebraic-degree}
 e_{2,v}-e_{1,v}
   =\operatorname{exp}(\eta_1)-\operatorname{exp}(\eta_2)-1\ge0.$$ The inequality follows from the choice of the higher line and the integrality and distinctness of the target weights. We have therefore obtained a genuine ordinary eigenvector whose torus exponents have the order needed for an algebraic representation. It remains to identify the algebraic form of its orbit on a sufficiently small open subgroup.

<a id="pm-h-49"></a>
#### A unit ordinary vector is locally algebraic

<a id="stmt-lem-polynomial-orbit"></a>
<div id="lem:polynomial-orbit" class="lemma">

**Lemma 44**. *Let $V$ be a continuous unitary Banach representation of $G_2$ over a finite extension $E'/\mathbb Q_2$. Suppose $0\ne f\in V$ is fixed by $N_0^-$, has torus character with integral exponents $e_{1,v},e_{2,v}$ on a sufficiently small open subgroup, and satisfies $Uf=\alpha f$ with $\alpha\in\mathcal O'^\times$. If $\delta_v=e_{2,v}-e_{1,v}\ge0$ for all $v$, then $f$ is locally algebraic, of algebraic type $$A=\bigotimes_{v\mid2}
                \det^{e_{1,v}}\operatorname{Sym}^{\delta_v}(E'^2).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-polynomial-orbit"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-polynomial-orbit</summary>

<a id="eq:polynomial-Gauss"></a>
<a id="eq:polynomial-uniform-error"></a>
*Proof.* Choose $m\ge2$ so the stated torus character holds on $\prod_v(1+2^m\mathbb Z_2)^2$, and put $X=(2^m\mathbb Z_2)^{[F:\mathbb Q]}$. Lower invariance gives $$U^jf=\sum_{b\bmod 2^j}n^-(b)\mathbf p^jf,$$ independently of the integral lifts of $b$. For $x\in X$ the map $a\mapsto a/(1-xa)$ permutes these residue classes. With $c=1-xa$, direct matrix multiplication gives $$\label{eq:polynomial-Gauss}
 n^+(x)n^-\!\left(\frac a{1-xa}\right)\mathbf p^j
 =n^-(a)\mathbf p^j
        \operatorname{diag}(c^{-1},c)n^+(2^jxc).$$ Define the $V$-valued polynomial $$P_j(x)=\alpha^{-j}\sum_{a\bmod2^j}
              \prod_v(1-x_va_v)^{\delta_v}\,
                    n^-(a)\mathbf p^jf.$$ Its multidegree is bounded by $\delta$. Use a $G_2$-invariant nonarchimedean norm on $V$. The torus eigenrelation, <a href="#eq:polynomial-Gauss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:polynomial-Gauss">[eq:polynomial-Gauss]</a>, and the ultrametric inequality give $$\label{eq:polynomial-uniform-error}
 \sup_{x\in X}\|n^+(x)f-P_j(x)\|
 \le \sup_{y\in(2^{j+m}\mathbb Z_2)^{[F:\mathbb Q]}}
                         \|n^+(y)f-f\|\longrightarrow0.$$ There is no factor for the number of residue classes: the norm of a sum is bounded by the largest norm of a summand. The unit eigenvalue and unitarity also introduce no growing factor. Continuity at the identity proves the final convergence.

For each $v$, choose $\delta_v+1$ distinct points in $2^m\mathbb Z_2$. Evaluation on their product grid and inversion of the resulting Vandermonde matrices recover all coefficients of a polynomial of multidegree at most $\delta$, continuously in its values. This polynomial space is therefore closed in $C(X,V)$. Equation <a href="#eq:polynomial-uniform-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:polynomial-uniform-error">[eq:polynomial-uniform-error]</a> implies <a id="eq:upper-orbit-polynomial"></a>$$\label{eq:upper-orbit-polynomial}
                n^+(x)f=\sum_{0\le\nu\le\delta}x^\nu v_\nu.$$

<a id="eq:algebraic-orbit-coefficients"></a>
For completeness, we identify an algebraic representation containing the resulting orbit. Put $H=\prod_v(1+2^mM_2(\mathbb Z_2))$ and write $h_v=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A_v&B_v\\C_v&D_v\end{smallmatrix}\right)$. Its Gauss decomposition is $$h_v=n^+(B_v/D_v)
       \operatorname{diag}(\det(h_v)/D_v,D_v)n^-(C_v/D_v).$$ Every factor lies in the neighborhoods already considered. Hence $$\label{eq:algebraic-orbit-coefficients}
 hf=\sum_{0\le\nu\le\delta}
       \prod_v\det(h_v)^{e_{1,v}}
               B_v^{\nu_v}D_v^{\delta_v-\nu_v}\,v_\nu.$$ These are regular algebraic functions on $G_2$, also when some $e_{1,v}$ is negative, since powers of the determinant are invertible in its coordinate ring.

Let $z_0=\bigotimes_ve_2^{\delta_v}\in A$, with the determinant factors understood. In the binomially scaled monomial basis $E_\nu$ of $A$, one has $n^+(x)z_0=\sum_\nu x^\nu E_\nu$. Define $\Theta:A\to V$ by $\Theta(E_\nu)=v_\nu$. Formula <a href="#eq:algebraic-orbit-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:algebraic-orbit-coefficients">[eq:algebraic-orbit-coefficients]</a> says $\Theta(hz_0)=hf$ for $h\in H$. The vectors $kz_0$, $k\in H$, span $A$, by the same upper-unipotent interpolation. Thus $$\Theta(hkz_0)=hkf=h\Theta(kz_0),\qquad h,k\in H,$$ proves that $\Theta$ is $H$-equivariant. Since $H$ is Zariski dense, its invariant subspace $\ker\Theta\subset A$ is an algebraic subrepresentation. The representation $A$ is irreducible and $\Theta(z_0)=f\ne0$, so $\Theta$ is injective. Its image contains $f$ and has algebraic type $A$. This proves local algebraicity without any assertion that algebraic vectors commute with specialization. ◻

</details>

The vector of Lemma <a href="#lem:eigenvector-specialization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:eigenvector-specialization">43</a> meets these hypotheses by <a href="#eq:nonnegative-algebraic-degree" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:nonnegative-algebraic-degree">[eq:nonnegative-algebraic-degree]</a>. It is therefore a nonzero locally algebraic vector with the target eigensystem.

<a id="pm-h-50"></a>
#### Completion of the proof over $\mathbb Q$

If $r|_{G_{\mathbb Q_2}}$ is absolutely irreducible, the preceding ordinary argument is unnecessary. Proposition <a href="#prop:block-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:block-comparison">17</a> and the local correspondence give nonzero locally algebraic vectors in the target eigenmodule: for an absolutely irreducible parameter, regular de Rham is exactly the required condition (Colmez, Dospinescu, and Paškūnas 2014, Theorems 1.1 and 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-114"></a>[[CDP2014](#ref-CDP2014)] <a id="pm-cite-115"></a>[[Colmez2010](#ref-Colmez2010)], (Colmez 2010, Theorem 0.20). The all-prime correspondence covers the scalar residual case at $2$. Since $2$ splits completely in $F$, this applies at every dyadic factor. The parameter in this application is $r\otimes\varepsilon$. We translate the opposite Hodge–Tate sign convention in these local correspondence sources using cyclotomic exponents, obtaining the algebraic factor fixed in Proposition <a href="#prop:block-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:block-comparison">17</a>.

In either local case, the definite-forms comparison of Proposition <a href="#prop:completed-forms" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:completed-forms">16</a> now gives a regular algebraic automorphic eigensystem on the definite quaternion algebra over $F$. It cannot be a reduced-norm character, since its global Galois representation is absolutely irreducible. Jacquet–Langlands therefore gives a regular algebraic Hilbert cusp form (Jacquet and Langlands 1970, Theorem 14.4) <a id="pm-cite-116"></a>[[JacquetLanglands1970](#ref-JacquetLanglands1970)]. Its Galois representation exists at $2$ by (Barnet-Lamb et al. 2014b, Theorem 2.1.1) <a id="pm-cite-117"></a>[[BLGGT2014](#ref-BLGGT2014)] and agrees with $r|_{G_F}$ by the unramified Hecke identities and Chebotarev.

Finally choose a tower of prime-degree cyclic extensions from $\mathbb Q$ to a solvable Galois closure of $F$, replacing $F$ by that closure if needed. At each stage the Hilbert representation upstairs is invariant under the cyclic Galois group: its conjugates have the same unramified eigensystems because the Galois representation extends downstairs, so strong multiplicity one identifies them (Langlands 1980, vol. 96, chap. 3, Lemma 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-118"></a>[[Langlands1980](#ref-Langlands1980)]. Cyclic descent supplies a cuspidal representation downstairs, unique up to characters of that cyclic extension (Arthur and Clozel 1989, vol. 120, chap. III, Theorems 4.2(d) and 5.1) <a id="pm-cite-119"></a>[[ArthurClozel1989](#ref-ArthurClozel1989)]; see also the original degree-two construction in (Langlands 1980, vol. 96, chap. 11, Lemma 11.3 and Proposition 11.4) <a id="pm-cite-120"></a>[[Langlands1980](#ref-Langlands1980)]. When needed, apply the cited unitary formulation after twisting by a power of the idelic norm, and undo that twist after descent. Every real place splits in the totally real extension, so the corresponding local extension is $\mathbb R/\mathbb R$; archimedean base change therefore preserves the regular algebraic real-place type. The Galois representation of the descended packet and the given representation become isomorphic on restriction upstairs; absolute irreducibility of that restriction implies that they differ by a character of the cyclic quotient. Twist the descended automorphic representation by the inverse finite Hecke character to correct this difference, and continue down the tower. Over $\mathbb Q$ the resulting representation is attached to a classical cusp eigenform, with the Tate twist dictated by the original weights. This proves Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The proof route. The connectedness construction uses the propagation theorem; the two upper boxes are cooperating inputs to occurrence. The target enters through that construction. The common field $F$ is solvable, totally real, of even degree and completely split at $2$. The ordinary family is used in the reducible local branch. Both branches produce a classical packet before descent.",
      "figure_id": "fig:proof-route",
      "pdf_page": 4,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:proof-route",
          "exact_quote": "The proof route. The connectedness construction uses the\npropagation theorem; the two upper boxes are cooperating inputs to\noccurrence. The target enters through that construction. The common\nfield \\(F\\) is solvable, totally real, of even d",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n box/.style={draw,rounded corners=2pt,align=center,font=\\small,\n text width=5.7cm,inner sep=7pt},\n arr/.style={-{Latex[length=2mm]},thick},node distance=9mm]\n\\node[box] (prop) {Unrestricted propagation at a curve\\\\\nSections~\\ref{sec:deformation}--\\ref{sec:propagation}};\n\\node[box,right=7mm of prop] (conn) {Controlled fields and transverse connectedness\\\\\nSections~\\ref{sec:fields}--\\ref{sec:connectedness}};\n\\draw[arr] (prop.east) -- (conn.west);\n\\node[box,below=14mm of prop,xshift=3.2cm,text width=8cm] (occ)\n{Potential pro-modularity of the target family\\\\over one common field \\(F\\)};\n\\draw[arr] (prop.south) -- (occ.north west);\n\\draw[arr] (conn.south) -- (occ.north east);\n\\node[box,below=13mm of occ,xshift=-3.2cm] (irr)\n{Locally irreducible target:\\\\local Langlands correspondence};\n\\node[box,below=13mm of occ,xshift=3.2cm] (red)\n{Locally reducible target:\\\\finite ordinary support,\\\\dense classical points\\\\and polynomial orbit};\n\\draw[arr] (occ.south) -- (irr.north);\n\\draw[arr] (occ.south) -- (red.north);\n\\node[box,below=14mm of irr,xshift=3.2cm,text width=8cm] (vec)\n{Locally algebraic target vector\\\\and cuspidal packet over \\(F\\)\\\\\nSection~\\ref{sec:classicality}};\n\\draw[arr] (irr.south) -- (vec.north west);\n\\draw[arr] (red.south) -- (vec.north east);\n\\node[box,below=of vec,text width=8cm] (end)\n{Prime-degree cyclic descent and finite-character corrections\\\\\nClassical cuspidal eigenform over \\(\\mathbb Q\\)\\\\up to Tate twist};\n\\draw[arr] (vec) -- (end);\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Flowchart diagram detailing the proof route consisting of seven rectangular boxes arranged in five vertical tiers with directed arrows indicating the flow:\n- Tier 1 (top):\n  - Left box: \"Unrestricted propagation at a curve / Sections 3–5\"\n  - Right box: \"Controlled fields and / transverse connectedness / Sections 6–7\"\n  - A horizontal arrow points from the right side of the left box to the left side of the right box.\n- Tier 2:\n  - Centered box: \"Potential pro-modularity of the target family / over one common field $F$\"\n  - Two arrows point towards this box: one diagonally downward from the bottom of the top-left box, and one diagonally downward from the bottom of the top-right box.\n- Tier 3:\n  - Two parallel boxes branched from the Tier 2 box via two downward-diverging arrows:\n    - Left box: \"Locally irreducible target: / local Langlands correspondence\"\n    - Right box: \"Locally reducible target: / finite ordinary support, / dense classical points / and polynomial orbit\"\n- Tier 4:\n  - Centered box: \"Locally algebraic target vector / and cuspidal packet over $F$ / Section 8\"\n  - Two arrows converge into the top of this box from the bottoms of the two Tier 3 boxes (one diagonally downward from the left box, one diagonally downward from the right box).\n- Tier 5 (bottom):\n  - Centered box: \"Prime-degree cyclic descent / and finite-character corrections / Classical cuspidal eigenform over $\\mathbb{Q}$ / up to Tate twist\"\n  - A vertical arrow points straight downward from the bottom of the Tier 4 box to the top of this Tier 5 box."
    }
  ],
